## orders_acceptance (id 15, phase 8) — 2026-09-03 — **the reuse run's first three-round feature, and the one that measured where #8 draws the blocking line**

**Effort:** 3 implementation sessions + **3 review passes**, **≈5.5 h wall-clock** (bounded below by `f8281d3` at 06:50, above by this record; per-phase from file mtimes, an estimate recorded as one) — implementation r1 **~1.1 h** (14:53 → 15:51, report 15:59), coordinator verification r1 **~1.0 h** (`arm-probe.sh` restores 16:40 → 17:40, plus four review launches lost to server-side 529s), **review pass 1 ~0.9 h** (REJECTED), implementation r2 **~0.6 h** (17:52 → 18:28), **review pass 2 ~0.6 h** (REJECTED), implementation r3 **~0.6 h** (18:49 → 19:26), **review pass 3 ~0.7 h** (APPROVED).
**#7 baseline:** 2 implementation sessions, **2 review passes**, ~4 h — its own first rejection; 1 blocking + 7 non-blocking on pass 1, approved on pass 2 with **6 non-blocking left open**.
**#8 result: ~1.4× slower, +1 implementation session, +1 review pass.** A loss, recorded as one. §"the benchmark question" says exactly what the extra round-and-a-half bought.
**Tests:** `Orders.UnitTests` 56 → 64 → **71**; `Orders.IntegrationTests` 43 → 45 → **46**; solution **312 passed / 0 failed** across eleven projects. NetArchTest **15/15**, `tests/Architecture.Tests/` byte-untouched throughout — no rule was needed for a composition root and none was skipped.
**Gates:** `./quality.sh` exit 0 in **243 s**; `dotnet format --verify-no-changes` clean; `./init.sh` exit 0 **run in the `in_review` state**, which is the state its own new lockstep check was broken on. `specs/shared/` byte-untouched across all three rounds; **no `R<n>` claimed** — the matrix stays `16 green / 1 scoped / 46`, independently re-derived twice.

**What was built:** the `orders.create` NATS responder (one `BackgroundService`, one DI scope per inbound request, `IDispatcher` resolved from that scope), the synchronous `fulfillment.stock.check` RPC client over real NATS core request-reply, `PlaceOrderCommandHandler` (reference data → stock check → *then* open the unit of work), the self-initialising `ORD-######` allocator under `WITH (UPDLOCK, ROWLOCK)`, the read-only reference catalogue, `design.md` §9.2's RPC error mapper, wire-shape request validation, and — the first in this repository — a runnable service host, factored into `OrdersHost.CreateBuilder` so a test can drive the same composition `Program.cs` calls.

**Three rejections, one defect class.** Every one of the seven defects across three passes was the same shape: **the behaviour is correct and nothing would notice it being reverted.** Not one was a wrong line of code.

1. **Round 1 — D1 (blocking).** `NatsStockAvailabilityChecker`'s `NatsNoRespondersException` → `UNAVAILABLE` vs `NatsNoReplyException` → `TIMEOUT` split. The implementer had *found this bug live during the feature* (the library throws rather than returning a null `Data`, contradicting its own XML doc) and fixed it in production code and in the test stand-in — with no regression test. Collapsing the two branches left **56/56 unit and 43/43 integration green**. `design.md` §9.2 makes the distinction load-bearing for feature 42's retry classification. Also D2 (the stand-in's leak fix skipped its `finally` on any non-`OperationCanceledException` fault) and D3 (`Host.CreateApplicationBuilder` enables `ValidateOnBuild` only in Development, so a missing *port* booted clean and surfaced as an `INTERNAL_ERROR` RPC reply at `LogWarning` — while a missing *handler* correctly killed the real host at `Program.cs:40`, proven by running the executable).
2. **Round 2 — D4, D5, D6.** All three round-1 fixes landed correctly, and **three of the four were themselves unguarded.** Reverting A2's mapper arm to `INTERNAL_ERROR` — the exact symptom A2 existed to fix — left 304 tests green; two of `asyncapi.yaml`'s four required fields were checked by code and by no test; and `ValidateOnBuild = true` → `false` in `Program.cs` left the suite green, because the regression test supplied those options *itself* and so proved the container validates when asked, never that the host asks.
3. **Round 3 — closed.** The coordinator changed the question put to the implementer from *"does my fix work?"* to **"what fails if my fix is reverted?"**. Seven reviewer mutations, **seven fired, zero survivors** — including two nobody had named: deleting the validator's **call site** (guarded only because the new wire-level test deliberately starts *no* stand-in, so an unvalidated request comes back `UNAVAILABLE` instead of `VALIDATION_FAILED`), and removing `AddDispatcher` from the composition root. Plus a real-executable probe: with a port unregistered the shipped binary now dies at `Build()` before `Application started` — the exact inverse of round 1's observation on the same mutation.

**#7's blocking defect was avoided in substance, and it is worth being precise about it.** Every fixture in this feature uses three pairwise-distinct, non-zero money values (2500 / 50 / 2450) with explicit `Assert.NotEqual` guards, and swapping `InitialDiscount` for `TotalAmount` in the responder's reply mapping fails a real integration test. #7 needed a second implementation session and a second review pass to find that; #8 never had it. **#8 shipped no incorrect line that any of the three passes found** — #7's blocking defect was a *wrong mapping* on the wire feeding the R42 credit-simulator predicate. The transfer eliminated the class it named.

### The benchmark question — what the reuse bought, and where the extra round-and-a-half went

**The two assessments produced the same defect class and drew the blocking line in different places. That, and not "#8's code was worse" or "#8's review was harder", is what the three rounds measured.**

Read #7's own record for this feature. It was approved on pass 2 with six non-blocking defects open, and two of them are #8's blocking ones almost verbatim:

- #7's **N2**: *"the DI guard proves the two compilers differ but nothing guards `emitDecoratorMetadata` or the six `dev` scripts themselves, so reverting either would leave the whole suite green."* → that is **#8's D6**. Non-blocking in #7; blocking in #8.
- #7's **N3**: *"the ESLint selector matches only `TSParameterProperty`, so a manually-assigned bare-typed constructor parameter evades it (demonstrated)."* → a guard covering half its claim, which is **#8's D5**. Non-blocking in #7; blocking in #8.
- #7's carried **D4**: `progress/current.md` out of lockstep for the third time. #8 hit it too, and turned it into a mechanical `init.sh` check — which then had its own bug (it had no `in_review` branch and so fired on correct state during every review pass) and was fixed. #7 recorded it three times and automated nothing.

So the honest statement is not that the review found harder things — **it found the same things** — but that #8 enforced as blocking what #7 disclosed and shipped. And it enforced the generalisation #7's own hand-off notes point at: #7's portable artefact for this feature was *"a zero-discount fixture makes money assertions look complete while proving nothing"*; #8 generalised it to *"a guard that survives its own reversion proves nothing"* and treated the general form as a gate. **Three rounds is the price of that policy. It bought seven armed guards where #7 shipped two unarmed ones.**

**The counterweight, so this is not read as #8's review being better.** #7's reviewer produced live-system evidence #8's did not: 24 allocators racing in 24 real MySQL transactions (gap-free `1..24`), a virgin counter continuing correctly from `ORD-900000`, an RPC timeout *measured* at 808 ms against an 800 ms budget, a real `kill -TERM` proving the shutdown drain, and a read of `kafkajs`'s upstream source. #8's reviewer ran mutation probes, traceability walks and real-executable boots — and left `EfCoreOrderNumberAllocator`'s concurrency and its seed-from-a-non-empty-table branch unprobed (advisory A11, owed to feature 16; the SQL was verified statically and is correct). **#7's review was broader on interrogating the running system; #8's was sharper on mutation.** Both instruments belong in the trilogy's record; neither is the better one.

**What the reuse saved here, plainly.** The requirement semantics, `asyncapi.yaml`'s four payload schemas, `design.md` §9.2's error table and §10's handler shape were **read, not designed** — the mapper is a transcription of a settled table and the handler's ordering (reference data → stock check → open the unit of work) was decided before a line was written. That is where #7's first implementation session went and #8's did not. **What it could not save** was three rounds of learning that #8's own failure mode is under-guarding rather than mis-implementing — a fact about this assessment's process that no amount of #7 to read would have surfaced.

**Defects open at approval (4, none blocking):** **A3** `observed` in the stock-check assertion is last-write-wins across the harness's own startup probes — not misleading, and now retirable in two lines using the `CompanyCode == "PROBE"` discriminator `StartSilentAsync` introduced. **A5** three error-code conventions now coexist in Orders (Application `SCREAMING_CASE`, domain `<subject>.<snake_case>`, and a Presentation type with no `Code` at all), all deliberate, none recorded as such — one sentence owed in `design.md` §9. **A10** `src/Orders/Domain/README_PLACEHOLDER.cs` is a load-bearing assembly anchor for all fifteen architecture rules (`design.md` §10.2 mandates keeping it, and the implementer correctly declined to delete it and said why); it should be re-anchored on `typeof(…Domain.Order)` and deleted, **slotted into feature 17**, which adds the next service needing an anchor of its own. **A11** the order-number allocator has no direct test — see the counterweight above. Plus **A7/A8**: the architecture-rule count was quoted stale twice in two briefs ("fourteen", then "fifteen, one more than before") when it has been 15 since feature 43 — a one-line `init.sh` assertion on the `[Fact]` count would end it.

**Notes for #9:**

- **Change the question you ask of your own fix.** Not *"does it work?"* — it does, that is why you wrote it — but ***"what fails if I revert this?"*** #8 needed two rejections to make that substitution and closed the loop in one round afterwards. It is the cheapest process change in this repository's history and it is free to inherit.
- **The most dangerous code in a feature is the code you fixed last.** You fixed it because you *observed* it was wrong, and observation feels like proof. It is not. This held twice in one feature: round 1's blocking defect was the branch the implementer had just repaired from a live discovery, and round 2's three defects were three of the four repairs made in response to round 1.
- **Arm the call site, not only the callee.** A validator with seven passing unit tests and a mapper arm with its own case can both be green while the validator is never invoked. Delete the *call* and see what fails. The test that catches it must be designed so its obvious form would have been vacuous — #8's was, deliberately, by starting no stand-in responder so that an unvalidated request returns a *different* error code rather than accidentally the right one.
- **When you inherit a predecessor's non-blocking defect list, decide explicitly which entries you will treat as blocking — and write it down before your first review, not after your third.** #8 made that decision implicitly three times and paid a round each time. For this feature the whole list is #7's N2 and N3, and both are published.
- **A guard that fires on correct state is as expensive as one that never fires.** #8's own new lockstep check had no `in_review` branch and failed every review pass on a correctly-written session file. If you automate a checkpoint, enumerate every valid status it must tolerate before you ship it — and arm it in the state you expect to *pass*, not only in the state you expect to fail.

**Wrap-up addendum — the first run outside Testcontainers.** Everything above was proven against ephemeral containers. The wrap-up ran the service against the **deployed** stack for the first time: `docker compose -f docker-compose.infra.yml up -d`, `dotnet run --project src/Seed` (which applies the migrations itself), the Orders host, and a `natsio/nats-box` stand-in for Fulfillment. An order placed over `orders.create` returned `ORD-000007` (the seed holds one to six) with `totalAmount: 49998`, and the matching `order.placed.v1` envelope appeared on `otc.orders.facts.v1` with its seven fields in the contract's declared order. The two negative probes returned `VALIDATION_FAILED` and `UNAVAILABLE` — not `TIMEOUT` — as designed. The README's recipe is that run, verbatim, and drafting it before running it produced three wrong claims in nine lines: see `docs/PROCESS.md` §11.2.

## order_saga_orchestrator (id 16, phase 8) — 2026-09-04 — **the most expensive feature in the build, and the one that measured what reuse does to where the risk lives**

**Effort:** 1 day, **4 implementation passes + 4 review rounds**, **≈8.7 h wall-clock** (bounded below by this session's start at 08:34, above by this approval at ~17:15; previous commit `850f32c` closed the night before at 20:15) — spec pass **~1.3 h** (08:34 → 09:53), human gate **~0.2 h** (09:53 → 10:05), implementation pass 1 **~2.3 h** (10:15 → ~12:35, including the live-stack walkthrough and the 28-row arming table), **review round 1 ~0.4 h → REJECTED** (D1, D2), fix round 1 **~0.8 h** (13:15 → 14:05), **review round 2 ~0.35 h → REJECTED** (D3), fix round 2 **~0.8 h** (14:35 → 15:25), **review round 3 ~0.4 h → REJECTED** (D4, D5), fix round 3 **~0.7 h** (15:55 → 16:36), **review round 4 ~0.6 h → APPROVED**. Per-phase figures from file birth/modification times and probe-log timestamps, not a stopwatch — estimates, recorded as such.

**#7's baseline for the same feature: 1 spec session, 1 gate revision, 1 implementation session, 1 review pass — approved first time, ≈1 h 45 min implementation, ≈35 min review.** #7's own history calls it *"the cheapest `sdd: true` feature of phase 8 by a wide margin, on the most complex surface"*. #8 spent roughly **three times** #7's total on it. **This is a loss, it is recorded as one, and the reason is attached rather than explained away.**

**Tests:** `Orders.UnitTests` 205 → **233**; `Orders.IntegrationTests` 46 → **63**; `Architecture.Tests` 15 → **16** (`FactConsumerConfinementTests` new, `FactPublisherConfinementTests` corrected); solution green across eleven projects, `./quality.sh` exit 0. `specs/shared/test-matrix.md` moves **16 → 25 green, 1 → 3 scoped, 46 → 35 not-yet-green** (R19–R23, R25–R28 green; R24's API half and R29's dead-letter row ratified `TODO`). `specs/shared/` otherwise **byte-identical to #7's**, re-derived at approval, not carried.

### What the reuse bought, and what it could not

**The orchestrator itself was transcribed, and it was right on the first pass.** The fourteen-row step table, the compensation mapping, the precondition matrix, the redelivery semantics and the `R29` retry/dead-letter split all arrived from #7 as settled decisions with citations. The spec pass closed **20 of 22** open points from #7's own gate record rather than re-deriving them. **Across four review rounds, not one reviewer defect touched that code.**

**What could not be transcribed was the proof.** #7 got its commit contract from `kafkajs` and its dispatch contract from `@nestjs/cqrs` — ambient guarantees supplied by the framework, which its tests could assume rather than establish. #8 hand-rolls both against `Confluent.Kafka` and its own dispatcher, so every one of those guarantees is #8's to prove. **Every hour of the extra six went into test infrastructure, and every defect any reviewer found across four rounds was in test code:**

1. **Round 1 — D1 (blocking).** `SO9`'s "the committed offset advances **only after** a successful handler" was inferred from a redelivery instead of read from the broker. Flipping `EnableAutoCommit` to `false` — a change that makes every restart replay every fact on three topics, forever, silently — left the whole suite green. The repair reads the `orders.saga` group's committed offset via `IConsumer.Committed` on a throwaway consumer, with a `TaskCompletionSource` gate replacing a wall-clock guess; the first rewrite of it failed on *correct* code and was caught before review.
2. **Round 2 — D3 (blocking).** `R25_...` published `stock.reserved.v1` directly to Kafka without waiting for the relay-published `order.placed.v1` to have been consumed. Both facts share the `Placed` precondition, so whichever won decided the outcome and the test could go red on correct code. Fixed with a **state gate** — wait for the `stock.reserve` command to reach `sent`, which exists only because `order.placed.v1` was consumed — not a longer sleep.
3. **Round 3 — D4 (blocking) and D5 (required).** A *second*, independent unsynchronised gate in the same test: the redelivery loop waited on `(correlationId, marker)` while asserting on `(correlationId, eventType, marker)`, so from iteration 2 onward the wait was satisfied by an **earlier iteration's** row and gated nothing. Nine of ten iterations were unguarded on every run, and the only thing holding the test together was the incidental latency of an unrelated `SELECT`. D5: the fix round's arming table had credited that failure to D3.
4. **Round 4 — approved.** The wait now takes the event type and the wait predicate is **literally the assertion's predicate**, so the failure is removed by construction.

**The only production defect in the entire feature — the sweeper's double-claim no-op, where `DispatchAsync` re-claimed a row the sweeper had just claimed and silently did nothing — was found by the implementer's own arming, before any review.**

### The finding, in one sentence

**Reuse moves the residual risk from *"did we model it right"* to *"does our proof actually prove it"* — and a wrong proof is green.** A transcription is checkable by reading it against its source; a proof is not, because a test that proves nothing and a test that proves everything both pass. That is why this feature cost three times #7's figure while containing, by the reviewer's count, zero wrong lines of shipped logic. **#7's framework made the guarantees ambient; #8's stack makes them yours, and yours means you must prove them.**

### The process finding — attribution versus isolation, and it caught both roles

**Three times in this feature a red run in its own suite was closed by *attribution* — an explanation of why the failure was believed to be a known cause — rather than by *isolation*, loading the mutation that would confirm or refute that explanation.** Round 1's fix pass attributed an `R25_...` failure to memory pressure; it was D3. Round 2's fix pass attributed a differently-shaped `R25_...` failure to "the same D3 root cause" on the strength of 15 clean runs; it was D4, an independent defect D3's fix could never have touched. Both attributions were written by someone who had, minutes earlier, correctly diagnosed the *previous* defect — which is exactly what made each feel like confirmation rather than a second, unloaded hypothesis.

The implementer wrote the rule in round 3, in their own words, and it is the most transferable paragraph the feature produced: **before writing a sentence that explains a red run in your own suite, isolate the failing test and loop it under the mutation the *new* explanation implies — not the one already believed to be the cause.** A green re-run, however many times repeated, answers *"does this still happen sometimes"* and never answers *"is this the same cause as last time"*.

**And it bound the reviewer in the very same round.** Round 4 re-ran the reproduction vehicle that had failed 2-of-4 for the reviewer and 3-of-4 for the implementer hours earlier; with the fix in place it passed 4-of-4, which looked like clean confirmation. The **control leg** — the *unfixed* code through the same vehicle — also passed 4-of-4. The vehicle had simply gone quiet as machine load fell. **A pass/fail ratio on that race is not stable across a few hours on one machine, so no ratio — 1-in-12, 3-of-4 or 15/15 — could ever have armed it.** The answer was not a larger sample but a different question: with a deliberately unmatchable event type in the wait, the test's duration goes from 16 s to **3 m 37 s** (ten iterations each burning their full 20 s budget), which proves the per-iteration gate is real, deterministically, on one run, at any load. **When the probabilistic vehicle dies, change the question, not the sample size.**

### A credit the round count hides

**This review found a real defect in already-closed, already-committed feature 15**: `EfCoreOrderNumberAllocator`'s self-seeding branch does `IF NOT EXISTS … INSERT` with no lock hint and no duplicate-key handling, so two genuinely concurrent *first-ever* allocations against a never-seeded sequence table can both lose the race. Found by a test written for this feature's A11 debt, reproduced deterministically after two redesigns (a `Barrier`-based version **deadlocked** live and had to be killed), reported rather than fixed — it is feature 15's code — and filed as **backlog id 45**, with *"invert, don't delete"* written into its acceptance criteria so whoever fixes it does not silently delete the test that proves the bug. Four rounds is expensive; four rounds that also reach backwards into closed, committed work is not simply expensive.

### A harness lesson paid for in this feature, and now a convention

Round 3's review reformatted `feature_list.json` with a JSON round-trip, then undid it with `git checkout -- feature_list.json` — and the file was **uncommitted**, so the checkout discarded every uncommitted change including the leader's backlog entry for the allocator defect. `CLAUDE.md` now carries the rule: **no agent runs `git checkout --` on `feature_list.json`, ever; to undo an edit to it, re-edit it.** Two things make it worth a rule rather than care. The existing no-`git checkout` clause is justified by files being *untracked*, so a reader who has internalised it concludes it does not apply to a tracked file — when in fact it applies **more**, because that file is almost always dirty with other writers' work. And **`init.sh` cannot catch the damage**: a backlog with a feature missing is still a valid backlog, still has at most one `in_progress`, still satisfies SDD coherence. That is the third disguise of the guard-that-does-not-guard in this repository — a check that fires on nothing, a check run against the wrong artefact, and now a check whose invariants are all satisfied by an incorrect state.

### Notes for #9

- **Budget the proof, not the model.** When you inherit a settled design, the implementation is cheap and the *evidence* is not. Plan the test infrastructure — commit contracts, fact-ordering gates, broker-side reads — as first-class work with its own hours, or it will arrive as review rounds.
- **Synchronise on state, never on time.** Every defect in this feature's tests was a wait that did not prove what the next line read. The repair is always the same shape: find an observable row or offset that *exists only because* the thing you are waiting for happened, and wait for that. `stock.reserve`/`sent` proves `order.placed.v1` was consumed; a broker-committed offset proves a commit happened; a `(correlationId, eventType, marker)` row proves *that* fact was ignored.
- **Make the wait's predicate identical to the assertion's predicate.** D4 was a wait that was strictly weaker than the read it guarded, and it survived a full review round because the gap was invisible on a green run. If they are not the same predicate, write down why.
- **A helper's contract must warn where it is unsafe.** D4 existed because a `count > 0` wait was correct for its original single-fact caller and silently wrong for a loop. The fix that lasts is the doc-comment at the definition naming the trap, not only the corrected call site.
- **Run the control leg.** Before you report "N/N green with the fix", run the same vehicle **without** the fix in the same session. If it is also green, you have measured nothing.
