## orders_saga_terminal_rejection_classification (id 42, phase 8) — 2026-09-04 — **reuse working exactly as intended, and still 2× #7's clock**

**Effort:** **1 implementer pass, APPROVED first time, 0 blocking defects, 5 advisories.** No spec session (`sdd: false`; the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 42's three acceptance bullets plus the seam feature 16 left at `specs/order_saga_orchestrator/design.md` §6.1/§6.3/§12). **≈1.1 h wall-clock end to end**, bounded below by the previous commit `c70e643` at 17:10 and above by this approval at ~18:15. Implementation **≈22 min of captured write bursts** (`progress/current.md` 17:18 → the impl report's own final save 17:40, with the six touched source and test files landing 17:22–17:25) — understating true effort, since it excludes read/think time before the first write and excludes the coordinator's `./quality.sh` run (exit 0, `dotnet format` clean, **508 passed / 0 failed** across eleven projects, `./init.sh` exit 0), which the implementer deliberately did not run and said so. Review **≈35 min** (17:45 → 18:15): three independent mutation probes with forced `--no-incremental` rebuilds, the nine-code split re-derived from `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml` before opening #7's file, #7's dispatcher catch read directly to adjudicate the disclosed divergence, a real `diff -rq specs/shared` against the #7 checkout, `Architecture.Tests` re-run (16/16), and a grep of the whole `src/` tree for consumers of the status enum. Timings from file mtimes and probe logs, not a stopwatch — estimates, recorded as such.

**#7's baseline for the same feature: 1 implementer pass, approved first time, ≈9–10 min of captured writing, ≈20–25 min of review, 1 disclosed non-blocking finding.** One of the cheapest features in either build. **#8 ran roughly 2× on implementation and ~1.5× on review — a loss, recorded as one**, and the more informative of the two losses this phase produced.

**What was built:** `NatsSagaCommandsAdapter` no longer collapses every `RpcError`-shaped NATS reply into a retryable `SagaCommandTransportError`. `IsTerminalRpcErrorCode` splits `asyncapi.yaml`'s closed twelve-code enum into nine terminal business outcomes and three transient/infra codes; a terminal code raises the new `SagaCommandBusinessRejectionError`, which `SagaCommandDispatcher` catches **before** its retry filter and resolves on the **first** attempt via the new `ISagaCommandStore.RejectAsync` → `status = 'rejected'`, `next_attempt_at = NULL` — never `ParkAsync`'s retry-eligible path. No migration (`nvarchar(10)`, no `CHECK`; `rejected` is 8 characters, pre-authorised by design.md §12). Closes the live-reproduced failure mode: a `stock.release` against an already-`consumed` reservation retrying 81+ times, permanently unresolvable.

**Tests:** `Orders.UnitTests` 233 → **248** (+15), `Orders.IntegrationTests` 63 → **64**, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16 unchanged; solution green across eleven projects. `specs/shared/test-matrix.md` correctly **untouched** — no `R<n>` covers the terminal-vs-transient split (R29 is the timeout/backoff/park clause), and the `R42` at `test-matrix.md:219` is the unrelated shared compensation requirement, a numeric coincidence with this backlog id. #7 recorded the identical finding. `specs/shared/` byte-identical to #7's apart from `test-matrix.md`, re-verified by real `diff` at approval.

### The three review probes, and the one that mattered

1. **The classifier, re-armed at finer grain than the implementer's own.** The implementer disabled the whole switch (`=> false`, 10 of 23 adapter tests red) — which proves the classifier is load-bearing but cannot distinguish a genuine per-code `[Theory]` from one shared assertion. The review removed **one** code (`PRECONDITION_FAILED`) and killed exactly the two tests naming it, leaving the other seven terminal and all three transient cases green. Per-code coverage is real.
2. **The dispatcher short-circuit**, `RejectAsync` → `ParkAsync`: `Assert.Empty() Failure: Collection was not empty`. The assertion is on the *kind* of resolution, not the count of attempts.
3. **The sweeper exclusion — the probe that found what no arming the implementer performed could have reached.** The integration test's last line is `Assert.DoesNotContain(due, …)`, a negative assertion in a database holding exactly one row: on its face it could pass because the sweep returned nothing for an unrelated reason. The implementer's own guard-3 mutation (`"rejected"` → `"parked"`) dies at the *first* assertion three lines earlier and never executes the sweep. Widening `ClaimDueAsync`'s SQL to `(status = 'pending' OR status = 'rejected')` fired it: `Assert.DoesNotContain() Failure: Filter matched in collection`. The row is genuinely due by every other criterion — `created_at` sitting exactly on the 10 s `PendingGraceMs` boundary against a frozen `FakeClock`, lease cleared — and status alone excludes it. **Live, not vacuous.**

### The judgement calls, both upheld

**The closed set.** Nine terminal codes, three transient — re-derived from `asyncapi.yaml:2839-2855` before reading #7, and identical to #7's `isTerminalRpcErrorCode` member for member. Each of the nine names a decision the responder's domain reached about *this* request, unchangeable by re-asking under the same idempotency key; each of the three names a condition a later attempt can genuinely resolve. `TIMEOUT` is caller-produced by the schema's own description and never reaches the branch, but is listed transient for completeness. The one code that is a judgement rather than a tautology is `NOT_FOUND` — a visibility race on an eventually-consistent responder could in principle make a retry productive — and it is not one here, because every saga command follows the fact that establishes its subject's state and both sides live in this repository.

**Failing open on an unknown code.** An `RpcError` code outside the closed set falls **transient**, where #7's TypeScript has a `never`-typed default that throws. Checked, not accepted: #7's dispatcher catch is *generic*, so an unrecognised code's thrown `Error` is retried there too — TS's exhaustiveness device is compile-time, and its runtime behaviour is transient. C#'s catch is *filtered*, so throwing would escape `DispatchClaimedAsync` past the lease with neither park nor reject. **The divergence is in mechanism only; the behaviour matches #7's.** And the default is right on its own merits: the two wrong answers are asymmetric. A wrongly-terminal command is unrecoverable by construction — nothing re-claims a `rejected` row and no operator redrive exists — while a wrongly-transient one is bounded waste on a row whose status, attempts and last error an operator can see. Decisively, **the transient side is the side feature 27 will hook for DLQ/alerting**, so the code nobody modelled lands in the branch that escalates and the business "no" lands in the branch that only logs. That is the correct assignment.

**The inherited deferral, confirmed identical.** The dead-letter hook is deliberately not wired to the new `rejected` path. Confirmed by reading that `src/Orders` contains **no** DLQ mechanism at all, so there was nothing to half-wire; feature 27 `observability_reliability` owns it and carries the acceptance bullet. #7 deferred it the same way with the same reasoning — *"the responder legitimately said no" is a different signal from "the responder is broken"* — and #8 disclosed it in two places rather than deciding silently. The debt is real and is now on record: a terminal rejection leaves the order in an intermediate status with **only a log line**, no `order.saga_failed.v1` and no DLQ entry. Still strictly better than the pre-fix near-silent infinite retry, which is why it does not block.

**Feature 15's `UNAVAILABLE`/`TIMEOUT` split — untouched.** A third category was added; the two existing exception types keep their construction sites and message formats verbatim, and the new catch clause sits before the old filter rather than replacing it. `NatsStockAvailabilityChecker` was not disturbed, and the implementer confirmed by reading it that it has no `RpcError`-body branch to disturb.

### The finding

**Reuse removed the entire design cost and did not remove the evidence cost — and at this scale that is all the number can be measuring.** This feature had the most complete inheritance in the build: #7's committed answer to the one real judgement, its test list, its arming evidence, its disclosed deferral, its disclosed divergence, and a live-bug reproduction, all available before the first file was opened. Nothing was left to decide, no round was spent, no blocking defect was found — and it still ran ~2×. The gap is entirely inside the proof: a real MS-SQL Testcontainers test for the sweeper exclusion where #7 asserted its query structurally by reading it, three forced `--no-incremental` rebuild cycles the arming protocol demands and a TypeScript stack does not, and a compile-time constraint (`false` is not a legal `when` filter under `TreatWarningsAsErrors`) that turned one arming edit into two. **Feature 16 found this at 3× on 8.7 hours with a complex surface; feature 42 finds the same ratio-in-kind at 2× on 22 minutes with a settled one.** The second measurement is the cleaner of the two.

**And the extra evidence paid for itself in the same session:** probe 3 exists only because #8 wrote a sweeper-exclusion integration test at all. #7's review had no such assertion to leave unarmed.

### Notes for #9

- **When a mutation dies at an earlier assertion than the one you are trying to arm, you have armed nothing about that assertion.** The implementer's guard 3 flipped `"rejected"` to `"parked"` and reported the test red — truthfully — but the failure fired three lines above the sweeper assertion, which never executed. If a test makes several independent claims, each claim needs a mutation that leaves the others intact.
- **A negative assertion over a one-row table proves nothing on its face.** `Assert.DoesNotContain` passes trivially on an empty result. Either add a control row that *must* be returned, or arm it with the mutation that would make the excluded row appear.
- **The stale-binary trap fires in the false-red direction too, and it fired on the reviewer here.** `dotnet build projA projB` in one invocation is rejected by MSBuild (`For switch syntax, type "MSBuild -help"`); the build silently does not happen, `--no-build` then runs the still-armed binaries, and correct restored source "fails". Build each project separately and check the exit code — do not infer a rebuild from a command that printed something.
- **Check a predecessor's disclosed divergence against its *runtime* behaviour, not its source's apparent intent.** #7's `never`-typed exhaustive default looks like "unknown code ⇒ throw ⇒ terminal"; its dispatcher's generic catch makes the actual behaviour transient. Reading only the adapter would have produced a spurious defect against correct code.
- **A doc that is superseded by the feature it predicted is still a doc that disagrees with the code.** #7 corrected `design.md` §6.1/§6.3 inside this feature; #8 did not, and the taxonomy table now describes behaviour that no longer exists (advisory A1). Cheap to fix in the same commit, expensive to discover from a spec three features later.

---

## order_number_allocator_seed_race (id 45, phase 8) — 2026-09-04 — **closes Phase 8; the third lost-in-translation defect, and the first one whose fix was proved at the engine rather than at the test**

**Effort:** **1 implementer pass, APPROVED first time, 0 blocking defects, 4 advisories.** No spec session (`sdd: false`; the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 45's four acceptance bullets). **≈1.0 h wall-clock end to end**, bounded below by feature 42's approval at 17:59 and `progress/current.md`'s rewrite at 18:04, above by this approval at ~19:00. Implementation **≈29 min** (18:04 → the impl report's final save at 18:33; the test file landed 18:15 and the backlog transition 18:32). Review **≈25 min** (18:36 → 19:00): six engine-level probes against a throwaway RCSI database on the same MS-SQL image, an independent re-arming with a forced `--no-incremental` rebuild verified by `strings` on the produced assembly, `Orders.IntegrationTests` 65/65, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, a solution-wide `dotnet format --verify-no-changes`, and #7's allocator read directly. Timings from file mtimes and probe logs, not a stopwatch — estimates, recorded as such. **One measurement caveat, recorded rather than smoothed:** `EfCoreOrderNumberAllocator.cs`'s mtime now reads 18:43 because the reviewer restored it byte-identically after arming, so the implementer's true last write on that file is bounded only from above by 18:32.

**#7 baseline: none — #7 never had this bug, and the row therefore has no comparison.** Verified in #7's committed code, not assumed: `apps/orders/src/infrastructure/persistence/order-number-allocator.ts:71-78` seeds with an unconditional `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE next_value = next_value`, run on **every** call, where only the first insert does anything. There is no existence check, so there is nothing to race. **This is pure #8 cost — 0.9 h that #7 never spent — and the Phase 24 table must show it as an unmatched row, not as a win or a loss.**

**Tests:** `Orders.IntegrationTests` 64 → **65**. `OrderNumberAllocatorTests` 3 → 4: one test inverted and renamed, one added, two untouched. `Architecture.Tests` 16/16 unchanged, `dotnet format` clean, `./init.sh` exit 0, `./quality.sh` green (implementer's run; the reviewer ran targeted substitutes rather than the world). `specs/shared/test-matrix.md` correctly **untouched** — no `R<n>` covers the seeding idiom, and grepping the matrix for `allocator`, `order_number` and `ORD-######` returns nothing, so there was no row to flip and adding one would have been a false parity claim.

**What was fixed:** feature 15's self-seed was `IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 ...) BEGIN INSERT ... END` — two engine-level operations, an unlocked read then a conditional write. Under `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT`, which is on for all four deployed databases and (since feature 14) for every Testcontainers database too, the read takes no lock at all, so two genuinely concurrent **first-ever** callers both see "not exists" as true and the loser gets a primary-key violation on `order_number_sequences`. It is now one atomic statement: `INSERT INTO ... SELECT 1, seed.next_value FROM (SELECT ISNULL(MAX(CAST(SUBSTRING(...) AS int)), 0) + 1 ...) AS seed WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM dbo.order_number_sequences WITH (UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK) WHERE id = 1)`. The allocation path's own `WITH (UPDLOCK, ROWLOCK)` claim — feature 15's, correct, out of scope — is byte-identical.

### The proof moved down a layer, and that is the transferable part

**The fix's correctness is a claim about SQL Server's lock manager under RCSI, so the review tested SQL Server's lock manager under RCSI** rather than reasoning about it from the test suite's colour. Three probes, in a throwaway `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON` database on the same image compose runs:

1. With one session holding an uncommitted `INSERT` of the key, a plain `SELECT ... WHERE id = 1` returned **0 immediately** — served from the version store, no lock, exactly the mechanism the bug depended on. The same read `WITH (UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK)` **blocked to `Msg 1222, Lock request time out period exceeded`**. That single pair is the whole argument, observed.
2. The shipped statement under a forced interleaving: **0 rows inserted, no error**. The same statement with the hint removed: **`Msg 2627`**. Change in kind, at the engine, with no C# involved.
3. Four concurrent sessions each running the shipped statement against an empty table: **one insert, three no-ops, no deadlock**. That is what `UPDLOCK` buys over a bare `HOLDLOCK`, and it is why the fix does not trade a duplicate-key error for a 1205.

**The C# arming was then re-run independently and held**: hint removed, `touch`, `dotnet build --no-incremental`, **the armed SQL confirmed present in the built assembly with `strings` before the runs**, three separate `dotnet test` invocations, three identical failures at the same line. Restore from backup, rebuild, `strings` again, 4/4 green. No `git checkout --` at any point.

### The implementer got the hard call right, and the hard call was about the test, not the code

**The obvious inversion was available and would have been accepted by a less careful review.** "Sixteen concurrent first-ever allocations all succeed" is deterministic once fixed and only *probabilistic* as a detector of breakage — and this repository has already paid for that distinction twice, at feature 16, where the same sixteen-caller vehicle went **6/6 green under CPU load** while the bug was present. The implementer wrote that test anyway, because the acceptance bullet names it literally, and then **explicitly declined to arm against it**, saying so in the test's own doc comment rather than only in its report. The guard it armed against instead drives two sessions and **polls `sys.dm_exec_requests` until it observes B blocked by A**, with a bounded wait and a loud failure if the interleaving never happens — the wait's predicate is the thing the assertion depends on, which is the exact repair feature 16 needed three review rounds to arrive at. **A lesson from a previous feature was applied, unprompted, before the review asked for it. That is the cheapest thing that happens in this repository and it should be recorded when it does.**

**The reviewer's own probe added a wrinkle worth keeping:** on an idle machine the sixteen-way test failed 3/3 armed too. It is a stronger detector today than its comment claims — and the comment should stay conservative anyway, because "today, idle" is not a property of a guard.

### The finding: three defects, one class, and a process that cannot see it

`order_number_allocator_seed_race` is the **third** instance in this build of **a property #7 got for free from its engine or its language being dropped when the *shape* of #7's code was carried across** — after JSON key ordering (MySQL's `json` column normalisation mistaken for a wire requirement) and money column widths (MySQL's `int` made safe by JavaScript numbers being doubles). Here, `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE` is atomic *because the statement is the unit of atomicity*; `IF NOT EXISTS ... INSERT` reads like the same sentence and is two statements.

**All three shipped green, through a review, against passing tests, and all three were found by a later feature's review reaching backwards into closed, committed work.** That is the cost worth naming: not the fix (0.9 h here), but the re-opening — a backlog entry, a fresh briefing, a full review, and a hole in the effort record where the original feature's true cost belongs.

**Nothing in the current process would catch the fourth, and the reason is structural.** Traceability runs `R<n>` → test, and every one of the three satisfied its requirement text exactly ("allocate a unique sequential reference", "integer minor units", "camelCase, nulls omitted"). The arming protocol does not reach them either: arming proves a guard fires when a behaviour is **deleted**, and in all three the behaviour was present and correct on the single-caller path the guard exercised. **The process never asks what property of #7's version made it correct** — only whether #8 produces the same output on the paths anyone thought to test.

**Proposed, for the human gate rather than decided here:** one line per ported idiom in the implementer's report — *"#7 relied on X (atomicity / width / ordering / isolation); in #8 that property is supplied by Y"* — required wherever #8 renders a #7 statement in a different engine or language, with the rule that **when the property was engine-provided in #7 and must be hand-built in #8, it needs a guard test of its own.** It is a small mechanical addition to a document the implementer already writes, checkable by a reviewer in seconds, and it is the question that would have caught all three at the moment they were written.

### The `git checkout --` incident, and the second amendment it produced

**It happened again, one feature after the rule was written for it.** An implementer transitioned the backlog with `json.load`/`json.dump`, `ensure_ascii` defaulted to `True` and re-escaped every em-dash in the file, and the instinct to undo a mangled file reached for `git checkout -- feature_list.json` — which restored HEAD and briefly destroyed the leader's uncommitted state (feature 42 `done`, feature 46 added). It was **disclosed in full, unprompted**, under a heading naming it a mistake, with the mechanism, the collateral and the recovery route; the state was reconstructed and then verified **independently by the leader**, which matters, because the implementer's own three checks (valid JSON, three intended semantic changes, `init.sh` green) all pass on a file that is internally consistent and wrong.

**`CLAUDE.md`'s amendment moves the rule upstream to the trigger**, and that is why it is expected to work where the first version did not: *to undo an edit, re-edit it* is advice addressed to a moment that only exists after the damage, whereas *do not rewrite this file to change one value* is addressed to the action both incidents actually started with. Two refinements were recommended at review and are recorded here for whoever next touches that section: state the post-condition as **"the diff shows only the lines you intended"** rather than "one insertion and one deletion" (this session's own legitimate edit was 17/2, because it also added id 46, and a check that cries wolf on correct work stops being run); and accept that **prose was already proven insufficient** — the rule was in the offending agent's context when it broke it. The mechanical form is for `init.sh` to keep a snapshot of `feature_list.json` and warn when the live file has **lost** an id or a status the snapshot had. `CLAUDE.md` says `init.sh` cannot catch this; it cannot catch it *from the file alone*, but it can catch it from the file plus its own history, and both incidents would have surfaced within seconds.

### Notes for #9

- **When the fix is a claim about the database engine, prove it against the database engine.** Two sessions, a throwaway database at the isolation level that actually ships, and a lock timeout will settle in ninety seconds what an afternoon of reading documentation will not. PostgreSQL's counterpart of this whole feature is `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, which is atomic like #7's MySQL and unlike #8's first attempt — so #9 will probably not have this bug either, which is precisely why it should check *why* rather than assume the good outcome.
- **Verify your test databases have the isolation level your deployed ones do, and verify it by querying, not by reading the init script.** #8 shipped concurrency tests for several phases against Testcontainers databases with RCSI **off** while every deployed database had it on. Feature 14 fixed it; nothing would have found it otherwise.
- **A guard whose armed run can go green on a lucky schedule is not a guard.** If you cannot make a concurrent test fail *every* time when the fix is reverted, do not count it as arming — drive the interleaving explicitly and **assert that you observed it** (`sys.dm_exec_requests`, `pg_stat_activity`, `pg_locks`), with a bounded wait that fails loudly when the interleaving never happened.
- **A correctness rewrite can silently change the cost profile.** #8's atomic form scans the whole `orders` table on *every* allocation where feature 15's scanned it only when seeding — plan-verified, `Rows = 5000, Executes = 1`, with the anti-semi-join discarding the row afterwards. It happens to match #7's own unconditional `max()`, so it is parity rather than regression against the baseline; it was still not costed in the report. Take the plan, not the intent.
- **Ask what made the predecessor correct, not what the predecessor looked like.** Three times now.

---

## Phase 8 closing assessment — seven features, seven rejection rounds, and the phase where the benchmark stopped being about the code

| | #7 (NestJS) | #8 (.NET) | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| `orders_aggregate` (13) | ~1.5 h, approved first pass, 16 open points at its gate | **~1.15 h**, approved first pass, **0** open points at its gate | **~0.77× — faster** |
| `cqrs_dispatcher` (43) | **no counterpart** (`@nestjs/cqrs`, a package) | **~3 h**, 4 passes, **2 rejections** | **n/a — unmatched row** |
| `outbox_and_idempotency` (14) | ~2.4 h, approved, 10 non-blocking defects | **~3.4 h**, approved first pass | 1.4× **slower** |
| `orders_acceptance` (15) | ~4 h, 2 review passes, 1 rejection | **~5.5 h**, 3 review passes, **2 rejections** | 1.4× **slower** |
| `order_saga_orchestrator` (16) | ~2.9 h, approved first pass | **~8.7 h**, 4 review rounds, **3 rejections** | **~3× slower** |
| `orders_saga_terminal_rejection_classification` (42) | ~0.5 h, approved first pass | **~1.1 h**, approved first pass | 2× **slower** |
| `order_number_allocator_seed_race` (45) | **no counterpart** (#7 never had the bug) | **~0.9 h**, approved first pass | **n/a — unmatched row** |
| **Comparable subset** | **~11.3 h** | **~19.9 h** | **~1.76× slower** |
| **Phase 8 total** | **~11.3 h** | **~23.8 h**, of which **3.9 h has no #7 counterpart at all** | — |

**The single most important number in this table is 3.9 h in rows that cannot be compared, and the second is that both of them are #8's own tax.** `cqrs_dispatcher` exists because MediatR v13 is commercially licensed and `@nestjs/cqrs` is not — a *packaging* difference, not a language one, and the human gate ruled it binding across all six services precisely so the benchmark would keep comparing like with like. `order_number_allocator_seed_race` exists because #8 mistranslated an idiom that was never unsafe in #7. Neither is a row where .NET was slower; both are rows where .NET had to *do something #7 did not*, and a benchmark that quietly folded them into a ratio would be reporting a language penalty for a licence and a mistake.

**Where the phase actually went wrong is legible and it is not the code.** Seven rejection rounds, all concentrated in three features, and **not one blocking defect in any of them was a wrong line of production code**. `cqrs_dispatcher`'s two were guards reading the wrong artefact. `orders_acceptance`'s seven defects across three passes were all one shape — *"the behaviour is correct and nothing would notice it being reverted."* `order_saga_orchestrator`'s five were **all in test code**: an offset read inferred from a redelivery rather than from the broker, two unsynchronised gates that made green runs meaningless, and an arming table that credited the wrong defect. The orchestrator itself — fourteen saga steps, the compensation mapping, the precondition matrix — was transcribed from #7 and **was right on the first pass**, across four review rounds.

**So the phase measured the thing this repository exists to measure, and the answer is uncomfortable: reuse eliminated the design cost almost entirely and left the evidence cost untouched.** #7 got its commit contract from `kafkajs`, its dispatch contract from `@nestjs/cqrs` and its upsert atomicity from MySQL — ambient guarantees its tests could *assume*. #8 hand-rolls all three against `Confluent.Kafka`, its own dispatcher and MS-SQL, so every one of those guarantees is #8's to *prove*. That is the whole of the 1.76×. Feature 42 is the cleanest measurement of it in the build: complete inheritance, nothing left to decide, no round spent, zero blocking defects — **and still 2×, entirely inside the proof.** Feature 16 found the same ratio-in-kind at 3× on 8.7 hours; feature 42 found it at 2× on 22 minutes. **Two very different surfaces, the same shape of answer.**

**The phase's second finding is about where defects now live, and it should change how #9 budgets review.** Three of this build's defects have been **lost-in-translation** defects — JSON key ordering, money column widths, the allocator seed race — and all three shipped green through a review, satisfied their requirement text exactly, and were found later by a **different** feature's review reaching backwards into closed, committed work. The traceability we do have (`R<n>` → named test) is structurally incapable of catching them, because they are not requirement violations; they are dropped *implementation properties*. Phase 8 also produced the two `feature_list.json` destruction incidents and one amendment each, which is the same lesson in a different register: **the guards this repository keeps needing are not guards on behaviour, they are guards on the things everybody assumed.**

**What Phase 8 delivered, so the hours are not read as pure overhead:** the Orders aggregate and state machine, a hand-rolled CQRS dispatcher with boot-time handler-graph validation (which `@nestjs/cqrs` does not offer), the transactional outbox with relay and idempotent consumption, the full `orders.create` acceptance path, the saga orchestrator with its durable command queue, sweeper and both compensation paths, the terminal-versus-transient RPC classification, and a defect fix reaching back into feature 15. `Orders.UnitTests` **248**, `Orders.IntegrationTests` **65**, `Architecture.Tests` **16**, solution green across eleven projects. `specs/shared/` **byte-identical to #7's** apart from `test-matrix.md`'s Status column, re-verified by real `diff` at feature 42's approval — **six code features and two defect fixes without a single silent fork.**

**Notes for #9:**

- **Budget review at parity with implementation for this phase, not at a third of it.** #8's phase 8 spent roughly as long in review and fix rounds as in first-pass implementation. That is not a sign of a bad implementer; it is what happens when the design is free and the proof is not. Plan the test infrastructure — commit contracts, fact-ordering gates, broker-side reads, forced interleavings — as first-class work with its own hours, or it arrives as rejections.
- **Name the unmatched rows before the phase starts.** Ask, up front, *which features exist only because of my stack's packaging or licensing?* For Python that list plausibly includes a saga/command-bus choice and an outbox relay idiom. Those rows are real cost and must be recorded, but they are not evidence about the language, and a table that blends them is not a benchmark.
- **The reuse dividend is largest where the baseline was still learning.** Phase 6 established it; phase 8 confirms it in the other direction — feature 16 was #7's *cheapest* `sdd: true` feature on its most complex surface, and it was #8's most expensive by a factor of three. **Expect the widest gaps precisely where the predecessor's record says "this one was easy."** That is the sentence that should make you slow down, not speed up.
- **Every defect worth having found in this phase was found by a mutation probe or a direct query to the running system, and none by reading.** Arm the guard, take the plan, read the broker's committed offset, ask `sys.dm_exec_requests` who is blocking whom. A green suite is the beginning of the review, not its result.

---

## fulfillment_stock (id 17, phase 9) — 2026-09-05 — **Phase 9 opens; three review rounds, two blocking defects of one shape, and the first outing of the ported-idiom ledger**

**Effort:** **1 spec session, 1 implementer pass + 2 fix rounds, 3 review passes — REJECTED, REJECTED, APPROVED.** 2 blocking defects, 6 advisories (4 closed in-feature, 3 filed as backlog ids 48/49/50, 1 new one recorded at approval). **≈4 h 13 min wall-clock end to end** (spec_author dispatched 03:36Z, approval 07:51Z): spec + human gate **≈27 min**; implementation **≈1 h 51 min** (04:03Z → 05:54Z) including eleven armings and the live-stack walkthrough; review pass 1 **≤19 min**; fix round 1 **≈25 min**; review pass 2 **≈30 min**; fix round 2 **≈21 min**; review pass 3 **≈20 min**. **Review-and-fix totalled ≈1 h 55 min against an implementation of ≈1 h 51 min — parity, exactly what Phase 8's closing assessment told the next phase to budget for, reproduced in the very next feature.** Timings from file mtimes and subagent dispatch timestamps, not a stopwatch — estimates, recorded as such.

**A correction carried from the reviews, and it matters because this is the measurement:** review pass 1's own report recorded itself as "≈50 min". The verdict file's mtime bounds it at **≤18.7 min**. Rounds 2 and 3 both corrected it; **≤19 min is the number, and ≈50 min must not be cited.** A review that over-reports its own duration by 2.6× corrupts the one thing this repository exists to produce.

**#7's baseline for the same feature: 1 spec session (≈8 min), ≈1 h 35 min implementation, 2 review passes — rejected then approved, on 1 blocking defect.** #8's implementation ran **≈1.17×** #7's clock — the closest parity any `sdd: true` feature in this build has reached — and then spent an extra round-and-a-half on review. **Both builds were rejected on this feature; both rejections were "a branch that works and nothing observes it".** #8 was rejected for it twice, one round apart, and the reason is the finding below.

**What was built:** a pure `StockItem` aggregate + `Reservation` entity + `OrderStockReservation` domain service (all-or-nothing reserve/release across an order's lines); one `StockRpcResponder` `BackgroundService` over the five `fulfillment.stock.*` NATS subjects, handling requests **concurrently** (bounded `SemaphoreSlim(32)`, one DI scope per request — deliberately not Orders' sequential responder shape, ruled at the gate); the reserve/release lock protocol under MS-SQL `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT ON` (one single-row `WITH (UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK, ROWLOCK)` statement per distinct product code, in an application-fixed invariant-uppercase-ordinal order); Fulfillment's own banner-marked copies of the outbox writer / relay / Kafka publisher; and the bounded Orders-side change adding `x-correlation-id` / `x-request-id` to every saga command. **`Fulfillment.UnitTests` 0 → 79, `Fulfillment.IntegrationTests` 30 → 48, `Orders.UnitTests` 219 → 254, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16; solution green across twelve projects.** `specs/shared/` byte-identical to #7's apart from `test-matrix.md`'s Status column, re-verified by real `diff` against the #7 checkout at round 1 and by `git diff` (9 insertions / 9 deletions) at rounds 2 and 3.

### The claim Phase 8 could not demonstrate, finally closed — and confirmed by query

Phase 8 left four `stock.reserve` saga commands **parked** on the live stack with `last_error` reading *"no responder is subscribed"*, and a design claim that starting Fulfillment would drain them. It does. Confirmed at approval **from the two databases, not from the report**: `ORD-000007` – `ORD-000010` all `stock_reserved`, their `stock.reserve` rows `sent`, one `credit.hold` row each parked (Billing does not exist yet — the designed steady state); `ORD-000011` placed fresh through `orders.create` and accepted at `attempts = 0`, **the first genuinely end-to-end order acceptance in this repository**; reservations matching every parked payload; `IBERFOODS/PRD-0001 reserved_units = 5` and `PRD-0002 = 6`, the exact sums.

**And the cross-service chain is exact on all five orders**: each `otc_fulfillment.outbox` row's `correlation_id` equals `otc_orders.orders.id` and its `causation_id` equals that order's `stock.reserve` `saga_commands.id`, digit for digit — two databases with no shared foreign key, joined only by identifiers carried in messages. That is `CLAUDE.md`'s database-per-service rule **observed on real data** rather than asserted in a design. `F2` (`reserved_units` = the sum of `reserved` reservations) holds across the whole live database, not just the touched rows: the correlated query returns zero rows.

### The finding: deletion and corruption are different attacks, and half the space was going unprobed

**D1** (round 1): the `stock.rejected.v1` branch's only artefact — its outbox row — was never observed above the pure domain. Skipping the rejected path's `SaveChangesAsync` left 79 unit and 48 integration tests green. **D2** (round 2): `tasks.md` G7 said the release happy path asserts *"exactly one `stock.released.v1` carrying the request's `reason`"*; the test counted the row and never opened it. Corrupting `reason` **and** another fact's `retailerCode` on the wire left the whole suite green.

**D2 escaped round 1 because all six of round 1's probes attacked emission *deletion*. Not one corrupted a payload field.** The reviewer marked its own miss rather than the fix's, and that admission is now a convention in two places: `CLAUDE.md`'s arming protocol gained *"Deleting the emission is one mutation family, not the whole of arming. Corrupt the payload too"*, and the `reviewer` agent's check 5 gained *"probe two mutation families"*, both citing this feature. **A guard that counts rows without reading them passes the first attack and fails nothing on the second.**

Round 3 closed the family rather than the instance: five probes, three of them corruptions of values that cross a service boundary — the released `reason`, the causation id stamped on the outbox row (swapped for the correlation id, a plausible copy-paste that leaves both columns non-null and well-formed), and the Kafka partitioning key. **All five killed by named tests, at named lines.** D2's fix was attacked from both directions and answers both in one test: absence fails at `Assert.Single` on line 48, corruption at the `Reason` equality on line 53, and neither failure implies the other.

### The ticked-but-absent pattern reached three instances, and D2 exposed the limit of the remedy

The saga orchestrator's committed-offset task, this feature's G3/G5, and this feature's G7. In all three the implementer armed **every flagged task perfectly** (11 of 11, then 12 of 12) and the defect sat in an **unflagged** task whose prose nonetheless made a countable or identity claim. The mechanism is not carelessness: ticking records *"a test covers this scenario"*, while the task asked something narrower — *does a test fail if I delete or corrupt the specific thing this sentence names?*

The remedy landed mid-feature: `CLAUDE.md` and `.claude/agents/spec_author.md` now put the flagging obligation on the spec author. **D2 exposed what that cannot do: an amendment cannot retro-flag a task list already written and already approved at a gate.** G7 pre-dated the amendment, so the amendment could not have caught it — only a sweep of the existing prose could. So round 2 demanded one, round 3 spot-checked it independently on ten claims of its own choosing (seven not on the sweep's list), and it held: every countable-claim sentence in groups A–I is backed by a test that reads the specific field, count or identity it names. **For task lists written after the amendment the flag should hold; for the ones already approved, someone has to grep — and the grep has to be verified by a second party, because a sweep that missed one would be cited as clearance.**

The tenth claim was the one exception, and it is instructive because it is a *different* shape: `tasks.md` C3, `design.md` §6.3 and a test's own XML doc all say `StockRpcPayloadTests` reads `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml` as text. It does not — it compares against hand-retyped key lists, and so does `SagaCommandPayloadTests`, the Orders instrument the design names as the precedent. **The design described the instrument it wished existed, and the implementer faithfully copied the instrument that does exist while copying the sentence that describes the other one.** Non-blocking: the review parsed all sixteen schemas out of `asyncapi.yaml` and confirmed no drift, and a probe renaming one reply property killed the test, so the retyped list does guard the direction that can actually happen. Recorded as advisory A6, with backlog wording, because feature 19 copies this instrument into three more services and would copy the false sentence with it.

### Did the ported-idiom ledger earn its cost? — yes, and the question the human gate left open is answered

**Cost: ≈15–20 min of a spec pass that ran ≈27 min against #7's ≈8. Twelve rows. Return: four properties that would otherwise have been silent losses, none of them visible to traceability or to arming.**

1. **L7 — `CONFLICT` banned from this service's error mapper.** #7 mapped its concurrency error to `CONFLICT` and its orchestrator retried every code, so it was safe *there*. In #8, feature 42 made `CONFLICT` terminal: a deadlock victim answered `CONFLICT` would mark the `saga_commands` row `rejected`, which `ClaimDueAsync` structurally never re-claims — the order's saga would end permanently, **while satisfying `R32`'s requirement text exactly**. The guard reads the terminal set out of the Orders adapter's own source rather than retyping the nine codes.
2. **L1 — explicit `UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK` under RCSI.** #7 got a blocking current read free from `FOR UPDATE` under `REPEATABLE READ`; under RCSI the idempotency read takes no lock at all and returns a pre-insert snapshot, so two `stock.reserve` for the same order can both proceed. Its guard is the best test in the feature: it polls `sys.dm_exec_requests` and **asserts the interleaving was observed** before it asserts the outcome.
3. **L2/L3 — the lock order.** #7 got a total order free from InnoDB's index scan. MS-SQL guarantees nothing about a multi-row seek's lock-acquisition order, so #8 built it per-row in an application-fixed order — and only because the row forced the question noticed that a *plain ordinal* sort lets two callers spelling a code with different case derive different orders for the same row.
4. **L4 — `long` counters.** C# arithmetic is unchecked by default here; the `long` sum for repeated lines is genuinely load-bearing, and a probe narrowing it to `int` was killed.

**Its honest limits were disclosed rather than dressed up:** L2's arm stayed green (guarded by construction, not by observation) and L8's reproduces on a majority of runs, not all. **And its blind spot is now measured:** the ledger caught neither D1 nor D2 nor A6, because none is a ported property — the ledger asks *"what supplied this over there, and does that thing exist here?"*, not *"is the sentence I just wrote about my own repository true?"* Three of this feature's four findings were of the second kind.

**Verdict: keep the convention unchanged for phases 10–13. Its 0-for-3 predecessor record is 4-for-4 on its first outing, for about a fifth of a spec session.** That is the answer to the question the Phase 8 gate left open when it adopted the ledger without evidence.

### Phase-9 dependency this feature created

**Feature 46 `orders_stock_check_rpc_error_discriminator` is now reachable for the first time.** Until this feature there was no responder that could send an error reply to `stock.check`; `NatsStockAvailabilityChecker` deserialises an `RpcError` body into `Lines = null` and throws a bare `NullReferenceException` out of order acceptance. `FS22` keeps that path off the ordinary route today — an unknown product answers `available: 0, sufficient: false`, never an `RpcError`, and the review confirmed the guard by reading the test — so the seam is reachable but unexercised. **Close it deliberately in phase 9 rather than letting an integration run discover it.** The same shape appears in `FS22`'s own assertion, which detects an `RpcError` only by throwing at `Assert.Single(null)`.

### Notes for #9

- **Arm both mutation families or you have armed half of one.** Deleting a write makes a row disappear, which any count-shaped assertion catches. Corrupting a field's value leaves the row present and requires an assertion that opens it. Two of this feature's guards counted without reading, and a whole review pass went by before anyone attacked the second way.
- **A negative claim about a document is checkable — so check it, do not accept it.** The sweep this feature produced was correct, and it was still worth spot-checking ten claims independently, because a sweep that missed one would have been cited as clearance for the rest of the phase. Pick your own candidates rather than re-walking the author's list; seven of the ten checked here were not on it.
- **A convention amendment cannot reach backwards into approved task lists.** When you tighten a rule mid-project, ask immediately which already-gated artefacts the tightening cannot touch, and schedule the manual sweep for those in the same breath. Otherwise the amendment reads as closure while the old artefacts still carry the defect.
- **When a design cites one of your own tests as an instrument, open the test.** `design.md` §6.3 asserted that an existing test reads the schema file as text. It never has. The claim then propagated into a task list, into a new test's doc comment, and would have propagated into three more services. **A specification's statements about the repository's own code are as checkable as its statements about behaviour, and nothing in the process checks them.**
- **Prove the cross-service chain by querying both databases, not by trusting one service's log.** Correlation and causation ids matching digit for digit across two databases with no shared foreign key is the strongest available evidence that the messaging architecture is real, it takes two queries, and it closed a claim that had been outstanding since the previous phase.

**Correction to commit `5a84e81`'s message.** It states *"508 → 635 passing across thirteen projects"*. The measured figure is **623 across twelve** — `Architecture.Tests` 16, `Billing.IntegrationTests` 23, `Contracts.UnitTests` 21, `Cqrs.UnitTests` 23, `Fulfillment.IntegrationTests` 48, `Fulfillment.UnitTests` 79, `Notifications.IntegrationTests` 7, `Orders.IntegrationTests` 65, `Orders.UnitTests` 254, `Seed.IntegrationTests` 6, `Seed.UnitTests` 34, `SharedKernel.UnitTests` 47 — from a `./quality.sh` run (exit 0) made *after* the commit. The commit's number was arithmetic on remembered per-project figures rather than a reading of a run, and the project count was simply wrong. Recorded here rather than by rewriting a pushed commit.

It is the same defect this build keeps naming, committed by the coordinator into the one artefact the repository treats as process evidence: **a number asserted instead of measured, stated with the confidence of a measurement.** The relevant rule already exists — *an unarmed command in a report is an unarmed test with a wider audience* — and a commit message reaches further than a report. The operative form: **do not write a figure into a commit message that you have not read off a run in the same session.**

---

## orders_stock_check_rpc_error_discriminator (id 46, phase 9) — 2026-09-05 — **the fourth lost-in-translation defect, the first found in code written before the ledger existed, and a feature with no #7 baseline at all**

**Effort:** **1 implementation pass + 1 fix round, 2 review rounds — REJECTED, APPROVED.** No spec session (`sdd: false`; the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 46's four acceptance bullets). 2 blocking defects and 4 advisories on round 1; 0 blocking and 4 advisories on round 2, 2 of them folded into existing backlog ids and 1 offered as new wording. **≈1 h 40 min wall-clock end to end**, bounded below by `progress/current.md`'s rewrite to this feature at 11:41 (the previous commit `df18ed4` closed at 11:30) and above by this approval at ~13:21. Within that: implementation round 1 **≈11:41 → ~12:0x**; **review round 1 ends 12:27** (its own probe restore on `RpcJson.cs` is stamped 12:19, so it was running mutations at least from then); fix round 2 **≈12:28 → 13:00** (the two test files at 12:32, the stand-in and the new checker test 12:41–12:42, the backlog transition 12:59, the impl report's final save 13:00); **review round 2 ≈13:00 → 13:21** (five mutation probes with forced `--no-incremental` rebuilds, a programmatic enum diff against `asyncapi.yaml`, an enumeration of every `RpcErrorPayload` construction in `src/`, #7's adapter and mapper read at the cited lines, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, `Orders.UnitTests` 262/262, four integration tests over real NATS/MS-SQL/Kafka). **Measurement caveat, recorded rather than smoothed:** four source files carry mtimes from *arming restores* rather than from authoring — `OrdersCreateErrorMapper.cs` reads 13:06 and `tests/Fulfillment.IntegrationTests/StockCheckTests.cs` 13:08 because this review restored them after probing, and `RpcJson.cs` 12:19 / `NatsStockAvailabilityChecker.cs` 12:43 for the same reason one round earlier. The per-phase splits above are therefore bounds, not measurements. The round-1 implementation report's own save time is unrecoverable: the fix round rewrote the file, so its birth time now equals its mtime.

**#7 baseline: none. #7 never had this defect.** Its `apps/orders/src/infrastructure/messaging/nats-stock-availability.adapter.ts` decoded the reply as a union of the success and `RpcError` shapes from the day it was written (`let body: StockCheckReplyPayload | RpcError`, then an `isRpcErrorReply` type guard at line 98), and it guarded a malformed body at line 90 in the same method. There is no #7 counterpart feature, no #7 clock to compare against, and inventing one would be a false parity claim. **The whole ≈1 h 40 min — both review rounds included — is #8-specific rework**, and it sits on top of feature 15 (`orders_acceptance`), which shipped the checker without the discriminator and was itself the build's first three-round feature at ≈5.5 h. That is the honest shape of this entry: a defect the reuse run created for itself by translating a union decode into a single typed decode, paid for twice.

### Why it is the fourth instance of the class, and what is new about it

The ledger's three founding instances were wire key ordering, money column width and the counter seed. This is the fourth, and it is the **first found in code written before the ledger was adopted** — the ledger has been mandatory only since the Phase 8 gate, and `NatsStockAvailabilityChecker` predates it. The property #7 supplied for free was *"an error reply is recognised before it is treated as data"*, which TypeScript's union-plus-type-guard decoding idiom made structural; C#'s `JsonSerializer.Deserialize<T>` has no such step, so `{"code":…,"message":…}` deserialised into `StockCheckReplyPayload` with `Lines = null` and the next line's LINQ `.Select` threw out of order acceptance. It satisfied its requirement text exactly, and both of this repository's strongest guards were blind to it — which is the class's whole signature.

**It also produced the fifth instance, in the same thirty-line method, during the repair.** #7's malformed-JSON guard was still absent after a feature dispatched specifically to port a guard from that file. That single observation is what decided backlog id 52 (the retroactive boundary sweep): a dedicated repair pass on a file does not surface the next gap in it. The guard was then closed in round 2 rather than deferred, which correctly *removes* a site from id 52's scope instead of duplicating it.

### The finding: a negative claim about the repository is a search result, not a reading

Three sweeps in three features have been reported clear and disproved within minutes — feature 17's A6, this feature's round-1 null-dereference sweep, and the residue found in round 2 itself. Every one was executed faithfully against a criterion that was wrong, so re-reading harder would not have helped. **And the instance missed here had already been written down in this file**: the Phase-9 dependency note committed at 11:30 says verbatim that `FS22` detects an `RpcError` only by throwing at `Assert.Single(null)`, eleven minutes before the feature that swept for exactly that shape and reported none. A prose record did not stop a prose sweep from missing it.

The remedy adopted in the round-2 verdict is mechanical rather than exhortative: **a claim of absence is reportable only as the enumerating command, its complete output, and one classification line per hit.** Then a missed instance is visible as an unclassified line instead of invisible as a sentence, and any reader can re-run it in ten seconds — which is how round 2 found three further sites (one of them, `OrdersCreateAcceptanceTests.cs:159`, asserts nothing whatsoever: `RpcJson.Deserialize<OrdersCreateReplyPayload>(replyMsg.Data!); // the request succeeded` constructs an all-defaults object from an `RpcError` body without throwing). Wording for `CLAUDE.md` is in the review; the three sites carry backlog wording rather than a fix, because a fix inside a sweep is a change nobody reviewed.

### The judgement that did not go the leader's way, and why the evidence settled it

The leader put a second instance of D1 to the review — `StockCheckTests.cs:29-31`, the `R31` test in the same file — asking whether the fix had stopped at the line the verdict cited. It had not, and the distinguishing property is sharper than "does this test deserialise an error body into the success shape" (they all do): it is **"is the first assertion after the deserialisation satisfied by an all-defaults object?"** `FS22`'s was — `Assert.False(payload.Available)` passes on `Available = false` — so it sailed on to a null throw. `R31`'s is not: probe P10 made the real responder answer a real `RpcError` and the test failed at line 30 on `Assert.True(payload.Available)`, one line before `Assert.Single`. A happy-path positive assertion is different in kind from a negative claim proven by accident. **The charge was dismissed on a probe, not on an opinion** — and the same probe shape then found the three sites that do have the defect, which is the outcome worth generalising: when adjudicating "is this the same defect", build the mutation that would tell them apart.

### Two things the fix round got right that are worth naming

- **The clamp was closed at the wire boundary without collapsing the information #8 deliberately adds.** #7 answers `UNAVAILABLE` for every error reply; #8 forwards recognised codes and clamps only the unrecognised ones, to `UNAVAILABLE` — #7's own answer for that case — with the responder's original code preserved in `details.responderCode`. The divergence from #7 is now narrower than round 1 assumed, and it is recorded in the ledger form in the report **and** in the source's own XML doc, where the next translator will meet it.
- **The report diagnosed its own failed sweep instead of quietly fixing it.** The round-2 D1 section reconstructs the wrong criterion the round-1 search used, which is what made the mechanism above formulable at all.

### And one it did not

**The repaired guard was not armed.** Six arming rows in the fix round, none for `FS22` — the guard whose repair *was* the defect. The review supplied it (probe P9: the real responder made to answer a real `RpcError`, the guard failing with its own message naming the code it found). `A tick is not evidence the assertion exists` applies to a *repair* exactly as it applies to a new test, and this is the third feature in which that sentence has had to be written.

### Notes for #9

- **A hand-retyped copy of a closed enum is a guard that cannot see its own narrowing.** The clamp's twelve codes are correct today and were verified against `asyncapi.yaml` code by code — but deleting `CONFLICT` from the mapper's set left the whole 262-test project green, because the test holds its own retyped copy of the same list. There are now three copies of this one enum in `src/Orders`. Parse the schema; the helper to do it (`RepositoryPaths.Find` + `File.ReadAllText`) already existed one file away in the same test project.
- **`Deserialize<T>` into a positional record never throws for a wrong-shaped body** unless you opt in. Absent constructor parameters take their defaults, so an error payload becomes an all-defaults success object and any assertion satisfied by those defaults will pass. Python's own decoders will differ here — check what #9's does before porting a test that relies on an exception.
- **When you translate a union decode into a typed decode, the discrimination step is the ported property, not the deserialiser.** That one line in a ledger would have cost thirty seconds at spec time and saved this entire feature.

## stock_release_deterministic_event_id (id 49, phase 10) — 2026-09-05 — **a backlog entry closed inside another feature's pass, in about a minute, and the cheapest closure in the build**

**Effort:** **no session of its own.** Closed opportunistically inside `fulfillment_despatch`'s implementation pass, which had to open the same file. **Bounded wall-clock ≈1 minute of authoring**, from the mtimes of the three files it touched — `src/Fulfillment/Application/StockReservationService.cs` 14:09:17, `tests/Fulfillment.UnitTests/OrderStockReservationTests.cs` 14:09:42, `tests/Fulfillment.IntegrationTests/StockItemRepositoryTests.cs` 14:09:49 — a 32-second window about eight minutes into a session whose `progress/current.md` was rewritten at 14:01, itself eleven minutes after the previous commit at 13:59. **Measurement caveat:** the fourth file, `src/Fulfillment/Domain/OrderStockReservation.cs`, carries an mtime of 14:42 because *this review* restored it after arming the guard, so the signature change itself is not separately datable. **1 review round, 0 defects.** Review: `progress/review_fulfillment_despatch.md` §3 P1.

**#7 baseline: none, and the reason is the point.** #7 had no equivalent defect to fix because it had no equivalent seam: `order-stock-reservation.ts` mints ids where it needs them, and the `newId` delegate is a **#8 construction** — a deliberate purity/determinism seam added so a domain service can be tested without a clock or a GUID source. So the whole cost is #8-specific, and so is the whole benefit. Not a translation loss; a #8 convention that #8 then failed to keep in one of three places.

**What it was.** `OrderStockReservation.Reserve` took `Func<UniqueId> newId` and used it for every id; `Release`, in the same file, minted its fact's `EventId` with `UniqueId.New()` directly. `specs/fulfillment_stock/design.md` §3.3 promised *"no ids beyond those `newId` supplies"*, and for one fact it was not true. Harmless in production — a GUID is a GUID — and asymmetric in a way that makes the next reader unsure which of the two shapes is the rule.

**How it was verified rather than believed.** The signature change is trivial to confirm by reading. The guard is not: this review armed it independently by reverting `EventId: newId()` to `EventId: UniqueId.New()` and running the project. `OrderStockReservationTests.Release_TheFactsEventId_IsTheOneTheNewIdDelegateReturned` **failed** with `Assert.Equal() Failure: Values differ` at `OrderStockReservationTests.cs:53`, 1 failed / 102 passed; restored, force-rebuilt, 103/103. Both acceptance bullets hold, and the second one — *"a named unit test asserts the released fact's `EventId` is the one the delegate returned"* — is the one that needed the probe, because a test can be named for a property it does not check.

### The thing this closure exposed, which is worth more than the closure

**The same arming, applied to the same file, found that the property is guarded in exactly one of three places.** The `sed` used for the probe matched all three `EventId: newId()` sites in `OrderStockReservation.cs` — `StockRejected` (line 161), `StockReserved` (185) and `StockReleased` (234) — and **only one test failed**. The reserved and rejected facts' event ids can both be minted directly on a fully green 103-test suite.

And in `fulfillment_despatch`'s own new code the property is unguarded twice more: a test named `Create_TheAdvicesIdAndTheFactsEventId_AreBothMintedByTheNewIdDelegate` asserts only that the two ids **differ**, so both `newId()` call sites in `OrderDespatch.Create` survive replacement by `UniqueId.New()` with the suite green. That is the blocking defect that rejected feature 18 on the same day this entry closed.

So the honest summary is: **id 49 fixed one instance of a class and the process then found four more, three of them pre-existing and one newly written after the lesson was paid for.** A backlog entry that names a single site invites a single-site fix; the entry that would have prevented this reads *"the `newId` discipline is asserted for one of three facts"*, not *"`Release` mints its own id"*.

### Notes for #9

- **When you add a determinism seam, count the call sites and guard every one, or the seam is a convention rather than a property.** A delegate that three of five sites use is harder to reason about than no delegate at all, because a reader cannot tell whether a direct mint is a decision or a slip.
- **Arming with a blunt instrument is a feature, not a flaw.** The `sed` here was broader than intended and matched three sites instead of one; that over-reach is what surfaced the unguarded two. A precise mutation proves one guard; a slightly greedy one, run against a suite you expect to go red, tells you how much of the file is actually held.
