## fulfillment_despatch (id 18, phase 9) — 2026-09-05 — **closes Phase 9; rejected on a test that asserted the opposite of its own name, and the first feature where a concurrency guard was armed rather than counted**

**Effort:** **1 implementation pass + 1 fix round, 2 review rounds — REJECTED, APPROVED.** No spec session (`sdd: false`; the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 18's two acceptance bullets read against `specs/shared/` R36 and `domain-model.md` §4.3's F6/F7/F8). 1 blocking defect + 1 required report change + 4 advisories on round 1; **0 defects and 2 advisories on round 2**, one of them a backlog entry still owed by the leader. **≈1 h 46 min wall-clock end to end**, bounded below by `progress/current.md`'s rewrite to this feature at **14:01:28** (the previous commit `68a4065` landed 13:59) and above by this approval at **≈15:47**. Within that: **implementation ≈14:01 → 14:37** (36 min — `impl_fulfillment_despatch.md` birth 14:37:36, domain files 14:14–14:24, the twenty-three new tests 14:14–14:16); **review round 1 ≈14:38 → 14:56** (18 min — verdict file 14:55:43, id 49's history entry 14:56:20, six mutation probes and a whole 53-test integration suite inside it); **fix round ≈14:57 → 15:29** (32 min — `OrderDespatchTests.cs` 14:57:55, `OrderStockReservationTests.cs` 15:02, `DespatchCreateTests.cs` 15:13, `test-matrix.md` 15:15, `feature_list.json` 15:20, report 15:29:29); **review round 2 ≈15:29 → 15:47** (≈18 min, of which ≈7 min is Testcontainers wall-clock: a baseline race run, three armed race runs, one armed happy-path run and the whole 54-test suite at 3 m 18 s, plus two `--no-incremental` solution builds and a solution-wide `dotnet format` check).

**Measurement caveat, recorded rather than smoothed.** Four source files carry mtimes from **arming restores**, not from authoring: `OrderDespatch.cs`, `OrderStockReservation.cs`, `EfCoreStockItemRepository.cs` and `StockFactPayloadMapper.cs` were all restored by round 2's probes between 15:33 and 15:44, and `DespatchCreationService.cs` reads 14:43 for the same reason one round earlier. Their content is sha256-identical to the submitted versions (`5374af01…`, `9516a4e9…`, `bc1d9c59…`, `cmp`-IDENTICAL respectively). The phase splits above are therefore **bounds, not measurements**, and the implementation figure in particular is an upper bound on authoring and a lower bound on nothing.

**#7's baseline for the same feature: ≈41 min implementation + ≈22 min review = ≈1 h 03 min, approved first pass, 0 blocking defects, 5/5 hostile mutations killed.** #8 ran **≈1.7×** that. **The whole of the gap is the extra rejection round and the fix that followed it (≈50 min), and the defect that caused it was in test code, not production code** — for the second phase running, and the sixth feature running.

**What was built:** the DESADV half of Fulfillment. A `DespatchAdvice` aggregate created once and never mutated (`Create` refuses an empty line list per F6 and appends its single `order.despatched.v1` before returning, so no caller can observe an aggregate whose fact was not recorded); the pure order-scoped `OrderDespatch.Create` that consumes every `reserved` reservation of the order across its stock items and emits one line per consumed reservation, 1:1, never merged (F7); a `DespatchCreationService` reusing feature 17's lock protocol **unmodified** — non-locking pre-read, then the same stock-rows-first `WITH (UPDLOCK, HOLDLOCK, ROWLOCK)` that `stock.release` takes, which is what makes despatch un-deadlockable against a concurrent reserve or release; three F8 layers (a fast path that returns the existing despatch without opening a transaction, an in-lock re-read for the racing caller, and the pre-existing `uq_despatches_order_reference`); a `DES-######` allocator copied from the **fixed** `EfCoreOrderNumberAllocator` (feature 45's atomic seed, never the check-then-act shape it replaced); and a sixth subject on the existing `StockRpcResponder` rather than a second responder class. `Fulfillment.UnitTests` **83 → 105**, `Fulfillment.IntegrationTests` **48 → 54**, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, solution green across twelve projects, `dotnet format` clean at solution level. **No new package, no migration, no `src/Orders` change, no `src/Contracts` change** — all four enumerated by command in the report rather than asserted.

**Backlog id 49 was closed inside this pass** (its own entry is above) and its lesson was then paid for a second time in the same feature — see below.

### The defect: a test whose name asserted provenance and whose assertions proved only non-collision

`OrderDespatchTests.Create_TheAdvicesIdAndTheFactsEventId_AreBothMintedByTheNewIdDelegate` constructed a `Queue` of two known ids, passed the dequeuing delegate to `OrderDespatch.Create`, and then asserted `Assert.NotEqual(default, advice.Id)` and `Assert.NotEqual(advice.Id, fact.EventId)`. **The queue was never compared against anything.** Both `newId()` call sites survived replacement by `UniqueId.New()` on a fully green 103-test suite, and the arming table cited the test as killed — which it was, by *deleting the emission*, a mutation that kills it for an unrelated reason.

**This is the same property as backlog id 49, in the feature that closes backlog id 49, written after that lesson was paid for.** Id 49 was a production-code gap (`Release` minted its own id); this was the test written specifically to prove the property, one layer up, not proving it.

The implementer's own diagnosis is the sharpest sentence the feature produced and it is worth keeping verbatim: *"`Assert.NotEqual` proves non-collision; it can never prove provenance. When a test's own name or doc comment says a value **is** a specific thing, the assertion must be `Assert.Equal(knownValue, actual)` against a value the test itself supplied."*

### What round 2 added, and why the third mutation family is the finding

The fix was armed by the implementer with the two mutations the verdict named, and round 2 ran both independently — both fail, at **different line numbers**, which is what proves the two halves are pinned separately. Round 2 then ran a third mutation nobody had specified: **hoist both mints and pass them crossed** (`idB` as the advice id, `idA` as the fact's `eventId`). Nothing is deleted, nothing is externally minted, both ids still come from the delegate and both are still distinct — and the test still fails.

That probe is the generalisable part. This build already knows that **deleting the emission** and **corrupting a payload field** are two different families that find different defects (feature 17). This feature adds a third face of the second: **substituting the *source* of a value with something that produces an equally valid-looking value.** A deletion probe cannot see it (the field is present), and a corruption probe can only see it *if the test supplied the expected value* — which is exactly what `Assert.NotEqual` fails to do. The rule that follows is about the test's **inputs**, not its assertions: for any field whose value the test does not control — ids, clocks, generated references — the test must inject the source (a `newId` delegate, a fake clock) or bracket it, or the field is unguarded no matter how many probes are run against it. `despatchDate` in this same feature is the positive instance: round 1 found it asserted nowhere, and the fix brackets the RPC with `before`/`after` so that a corrupted clock value fails `Assert.InRange` — which round 2 confirmed by adding three minutes to it in the payload mapper.

### The concurrency case, and why five green runs were not the evidence

The fix round added the `despatch.create` versus `stock.release` race the `R36` matrix row had always named and had until now realised only by argument, and reported it green over five independent runs. **Five green runs are evidence of nothing** — green is the expected outcome both when the guard is live and when the two requests never actually overlap. Round 2 armed it instead: stripping all three lock hints from `EfCoreStockItemRepository` made it **fail on iteration 0 of 10, on three consecutive runs**, with both sides reporting success on the same reservation — the double-spend the protocol exists to prevent. So the guard's signal is a change in **kind** (a state the correct code cannot produce) and not in likelihood, and the ten iterations are belt-and-braces on top of a signal that arrives immediately.

Two things about that test are worth carrying forward. It **caught a real error in its own first draft** — the author assumed the losing `stock.release` would answer F5's `already_released` no-op, and the running system answered `PRECONDITION_FAILED` because `StockItem.Release` refuses a *consumed* reservation as a terminal state (F4/FS10); the test was corrected to match the system rather than the reverse. And its "exactly one fact" check reads through a `WaitForAsync(rows.Count > 0)` before `Assert.Single`, which is safe here only because the despatch row and its outbox row commit together — **do not copy that shape into Billing**, where two facts can legitimately be written by two different commits.

### Notes for #9

- **Read every test's assertions back against its own name before submitting it.** The mismatch here was visible in twenty lines for the whole life of the feature and cost a review round. It is the cheapest check in this document.
- **An identity or provenance claim needs the test to supply the value.** If the expected value is not in the test, the assertion is about the shape of the output, not about where it came from — and no amount of arming will tell you otherwise, because the deletion probe kills it for the wrong reason and looks like success.
- **Arm the race, do not run it more times.** Repetition raises confidence in a signal you have not shown exists; one mutation of the mechanism the race is about (here: the lock hints) settles it in one run.
- **A delegate seam is only a property if every call site uses it.** Round 2 probed all four mint sites in `OrderStockReservation.cs`; before this feature exactly one was guarded, and after it four are. Count the sites when you add the seam.

---

## Phase 9 closing assessment — three features, one closed backlog entry, six new ones, and the phase where the benchmark started measuring the harness

| | #7 (NestJS) | #8 (.NET) | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| `fulfillment_stock` (17) | ≈3 h 44 min (spec ≈8 min, impl ≈1 h 35 min, 2 review passes + 1 fix), 1 rejection | **≈4 h 13 min** (spec + gate ≈27 min, impl ≈1 h 51 min, 3 review passes + 2 fixes), **2 rejections** | **≈1.13× slower** |
| `orders_stock_check_rpc_error_discriminator` (46) | **no counterpart** — #7 never had the defect | **≈1 h 40 min**, 1 rejection | **n/a — unmatched row** |
| `fulfillment_despatch` (18) | ≈1 h 03 min, approved first pass | **≈1 h 46 min**, 1 rejection | **≈1.7× slower** |
| `stock_release_deterministic_event_id` (49) | **no counterpart** — the `newId` seam is a #8 construction | **≈1 min**, no session of its own | **n/a — unmatched row** |
| **Comparable subset** | **≈4 h 47 min** | **≈5 h 59 min** | **≈1.25× slower** |
| **Phase 9 total** | **≈4 h 47 min** | **≈7 h 39 min**, of which **≈1 h 41 min has no #7 counterpart at all** | — |

**On the comparable subset this is the closest to parity any code phase of #8 has reached** — 1.25× against Phase 8's 1.76× and Phase 6's larger gaps — and the reason is exactly what Phase 8's assessment predicted: Fulfillment inherited the transaction shape, the outbox, the relay, the dispatcher and the responder pattern from work #8 had already paid for, so almost nothing in this phase was a *first* of anything. The reuse dividend is real and it is now visible in the ratio rather than in the prose.

**But the two numbers that matter more are 1 h 41 min of unmatched rows and the shape of the 1.7× on feature 18, and neither is a language cost.** The unmatched hour and three quarters is entirely #8's own tax: feature 46 was a defect #8 created by translating a TypeScript union decode into a single typed decode, and feature 49 was a seam #8 invented and then failed to keep in three of four places. A benchmark that folded those into the ratio would be reporting .NET as slower for #8's own mistakes.

**And feature 18 reproduces Phase 8's most uncomfortable finding exactly.** #7's feature 18 was the cheapest feature of its phase — 1 h 03 min, approved first pass. #8's was 1.7× that, and the entire difference is one rejection round on a **test assertion**, in code that was behaviourally correct everywhere both reviews probed it. **Expect the widest gaps precisely where the predecessor's record says "this one was easy"** now has three independent confirmations (features 16, 42, 18) and should be treated as a law of this build rather than an observation.

### The confound that appeared this phase and will grow: the benchmark is partly measuring the harness

#7's review of feature 18 ran five hostile mutations and approved first pass. #8's ran six in round 1 and eight more in round 2, across **three** mutation families, and required every claim of absence to arrive as a command with its output. The round-1 rejection was for a test that #7's standard would very likely have passed — #7's own report for the same feature records N2, an asymmetry finding, as non-blocking prose. **So a measurable part of #8's 1.7× is a deliberately raised bar, not a slower language**, and the same is true of feature 17's third review round.

This did not matter much through Phase 8, when the harness was roughly #7's. It matters now: three convention amendments landed inside this phase alone (the ported-idiom ledger's first enforcement, the absence-as-command rule, and the two-mutation-families rule from feature 17), each of which *creates* review rounds by design. **Recommendation for Phase 10 onwards, and it costs one sentence per rejection:** record, for each rejection round, whether the defect would have been caught by #7's review standard. Without that field the final trilogy table will report harness maturity as a language penalty, and the one thing this repository exists to produce will be wrong in a way nobody can unpick afterwards.

### What the ported-idiom ledger actually bought, on the evidence rather than the hope

It was adopted at the Phase 8 gate with a 0-for-3 detection record behind it and the claim that *writing the line is most of the value*. Phase 9 is its first real test, and the honest reading is narrower than the hope:

- **It held up as an artefact.** Feature 17 authored twelve properties; feature 18 was the first feature to *extend* ledgered code rather than author it, and round 1 checked all twelve claims against what the new code does — **every one held**, including the two most likely to be assumed (the allocator's atomic seed and the outbox's per-row awaited insert). For a class with a 0-for-3 history, a ledger whose claims survive line-by-line checking is a real result.
- **Its value in this phase was as a review instrument, not as a prevention record.** The gap round 1 found (the un-hinted in-transaction re-read at `DespatchCreationService.cs:67`, correct only because RCSI makes the snapshot statement-scoped) was found *by walking the ledger's shape against new code* — the ledger did not contain the row. **You cannot observe a prevented defect**, so the ledger's central claim remains untested; what is observable is that it gave a reviewer a walkable structure and that structure found something.
- **It could not have caught this phase's actual translation defect.** Feature 46 — the fourth lost-in-translation defect — sits in code written *before* the ledger existed. That is honest rather than damning, but it means the ledger's protection begins now and covers nothing already shipped, which is precisely why it spawned backlog id 52 (the retroactive boundary sweep). **The ledger created that debt; it did not discharge it.**
- **The failure mode it has is legible: the row that mattered was the one nobody wrote.** A ledger built from recollection will always miss the boundary nobody thought about. Id 52's method — enumerate the value-crossing boundaries, then walk them — is the fix, and Phase 10 should derive Billing's ledger rows from an enumerated boundary list rather than from memory. That is the experiment worth running: four ported mechanisms, ledger rows written at spec time from an enumeration, and then a count of translation defects found afterwards. **If Billing ships without one, that is the first positive evidence the ledger prevents rather than merely documents.**

### A backlog that grew by six during a three-feature phase: working, or behind?

The phase opened with 46 backlog items and closes with 52; it closed exactly one entry (id 49, opportunistically, in about a minute). Both readings are available and the evidence favours a split answer.

**It is a process working, on three counts.** First, **provenance**: of the six new entries, ids 48, 51 and 52 are findings about work shipped in *earlier* phases and id 53 is two-thirds about earlier work — so the backlog grew mostly by **discovery**, not by production. Only ids 49 and 50 are defects this phase authored, and one of them was closed inside it. Second, **the instrument**: every one of the six was found by a mutation probe, an enumerating command or a ledger walk — none by prose review — and the enumerating-command rule adopted mid-phase found three sites in ten seconds that a careful prose sweep had reported clear minutes earlier. A backlog that grows because the detector got better is not the same thing as a backlog that grows because the work got worse. Third, **closability**: each entry carries concrete acceptance bullets and an arming clause, so none of them is a wish. Id 49's one-minute closure inside an adjacent feature is the proof of concept.

**It is also a process discovering it was behind, on one count that should not be softened.** Id 52 exists because a rule adopted at the Phase 8 gate cannot be applied retroactively to four services shipped before it, and id 51 exists because an instrument copied into three services carries a false sentence in three documents. Neither shrinks on its own, and **phases 10–13 port five more services from the same source** — so the discovery rate is about to be applied to a much larger surface while the closure rate stands at one per phase, achieved by luck of adjacency.

**The reading, then:** the number six is not the problem and suppressing it would be the worst possible response. The problem is that **five of the six are scheduled for Phase 10 by nothing stronger than a `phase` field**, and Phase 10 is a three-feature Billing phase that will also be the ledger's first prevention experiment. Either those five get real hours in the Phase 10 plan — ids 48, 50 and 53 are each one named test and are cheapest inside the features that already open those files (Billing copies the responder, so id 50 belongs there; Billing copies the payload tests, so ids 51 and 53 belong there) — or they slide to Phase 21 and become decoration. **The one thing that has been shown to work in this build is closing a backlog entry inside a feature that already has the file open**, and Phase 10's brief should say which entry attaches to which feature before the first implementer is dispatched.

### What Phase 9 delivered, so the hours are not read as overhead

The whole Fulfillment service: the `StockItem` aggregate and reservation lifecycle, the all-or-nothing order-scoped reserve/release domain service, the six-subject NATS responder handling requests concurrently under a bounded semaphore with one DI scope per request, the reserve/release/despatch lock protocol under RCSI, Fulfillment's own outbox writer/relay/Kafka publisher, the `DespatchAdvice` aggregate and its three F8 layers, the `DES-######` allocator on the fixed atomic-seed idiom, and the Orders-side `RpcError` discriminator that closed a defect ported away in feature 15. `Fulfillment.UnitTests` **0 → 105**, `Fulfillment.IntegrationTests` **30 → 54**, `Orders.UnitTests` **219 → 262**, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, twelve test projects green, `dotnet format` clean at solution level. **`specs/shared/` is still byte-identical to #7's apart from `test-matrix.md`'s Status column** — re-verified by real `diff -rq` against the #7 checkout at every approval in the phase. Three features, two defect fixes, **zero silent forks**.

---

## billing_credit (id 19, phase 10) — 2026-09-05 — **Phase 10 opens; the ledger's first demonstrable save, its first decorative guard, and the first feature to close four backlog entries inside itself**

**Effort:** **1 spec session + 1 human gate + 1 post-gate spec revision + 1 implementation session + 2 review rounds + 1 fix round — REJECTED on round 1, approved on round 2.** The seventh feature in this project to be rejected and re-submitted (after 15, 17, 16, 42, 45, 46, 18). Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-05), bracketed at the start by Phase 9's closing commit `6023a0d` at **16:00**: **spec ≈16:19 → 17:36** (`specs/billing_credit/` created 16:18:55, `design.md` 17:35 — 1 053 lines, gate record `progress/spec_billing_credit.md` 17:36), **human gate** (two recommendations overruled, producing `BC29`/`BC30`; the standing "fix it now" instruction overturned two deferrals, producing `BC31`/`BC32`), **post-gate revision ≈17:36 → 17:38**; **implementation ≈17:38 → 20:07, ≈2 h 29 min** — group A's cross-service refactor first (32 files across `src/Orders`, `src/Fulfillment`, `src/SharedKernel`, mtimes 17:38–17:50), then Billing itself from 17:55, the id-51 instrument at 18:55, id 48 at 19:04, id 54's ledger row at 19:36, `tasks.md` and the `in_review` transition at 20:07; **review round 1 ≈20:07 → 21:09, ≈1 h 02 min** — 34 probes across three mutation families, four container-backed suites, a byte-level parity check of seven files × three services, verdict **REJECTED, 4 blocking defects, all guards**; **fix round ≈21:09 → 21:42, ≈33 min** (7 test files, 5 production files armed and restored, `specs/billing_credit/requirements.md` §2 rows); **review round 2 ≈21:42 → ≈22:02, ≈20 min** — 8 mutations, each the one that disproves its own defect's property, plus an enumerating script over every reply-deserialising test site. **Total ≈5 h 39 min from the first spec file to the final verdict**, of which **≈1 h 22 min was review across two rounds** and ≈33 min was rework.

**Three buckets, not one ratio, because #7's `billing_credit` refactored no shipped service, touched no shared kernel and closed no backlog entries:**

| Bucket | #8 | #7 counterpart | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Baseline-comparable** — the Billing service (groups B–H, I) with its spec, gate, both reviews and the fix round | **≈4 h 20 min** | **≈3 h 15 min** | **≈1.33× slower** |
| **Gate-ordered, no #7 counterpart** — group A's seven-file × three-service outbox-relay unification, the `KafkaOptions` `required` client id, and `src/SharedKernel`'s `checked` money arithmetic | **≈45 min** | none | n/a |
| **Backlog closures, no #7 counterpart** — ids 48, 50, 51, 53, 54 | **≈35 min** | none | n/a |

**1.33× on the comparable subset is the closest to parity a `sdd: true` service feature has reached in this build** — against feature 17's and 18's Phase 9 figures and Phase 8's 1.76× — and roughly ≈1 h 20 min of the phase's clock has no #7 counterpart at all.

**Confound column, per the rule adopted at Phase 9's close.** Round 1's four defects: **D3 is the one #7's standard would have caught** — it is #7's own `W3`/`N5` probe shape, which #7 ran, found and shipped as a non-blocking nit. **D1, D2 and D4 are raised-bar finds.** D1 required *re-running a task list's prescribed mutation* rather than reading its arming table; D2 required diffing a backlog entry's named line numbers against a claim of closure; D4 required mutating a validation branch the theory never covered. **Three of four attributable to the harness, not the language**, and round 2's ≈20 min belongs in the same column. Without this field the trilogy table would read ≈1 h of harness maturity as a .NET penalty.

**What was built.** The whole Billing credit service: the `BuyerCredit` aggregate with an append-only ledger and the `availableCredit = limit − activeHolds − openExposure` identity, hold/release/consume with `CR-######` codes, the three-subject NATS responder (`billing.credit.hold` / `.release` / `.list`) under a bounded semaphore with one DI scope per request, Billing's own outbox + relay + Kafka publisher emitting `credit.approved.v1` / `credit.rejected.v1` / `credit.released.v1`, and the first genuine **compensation** this repository has run end to end against the live stack. Plus the gate-ordered cross-service work: `FactEvent`/`IFactPayloadMapper` extracted, one canonical outbox-relay family of seven files copied byte-identically into three services under the `OB1` parity guard, and `Money` arithmetic made `checked`. `Billing.UnitTests` 0 → 94, `Billing.IntegrationTests` 0 → 51, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, `dotnet format` clean, **827 tests green** across fourteen projects.

**Why it was rejected, and what that says.** All four defects were **guards**, not behaviour — every production path was correct on every probe. Three of the four have the same root: **a countable claim was written, ticked, and never seen to fail.** D1 is the sharpest instance in the build so far, because the task list *prescribed the exact mutation* (`tasks.md` H8: drop `TimeSpan.Zero`) and the arming table recorded the guard as "confirmed green today" — and applying the prescribed mutation left Billing 92/92 and 51/51 fully green, because the test re-implemented the mapper's conversion instead of reading through it. A guard being green is the one piece of evidence that cannot distinguish a guard from a decoration.

### The ledger experiment — Phase 9's open question, answered

Phase 9 set the bar: *"if Billing ships without a translation defect, that is the first positive evidence the ledger prevents rather than merely documents."* **It did, and the verdict is: the enumeration-first ledger earned its cost.**

- **`L26` is the first observable save in this build.** The gate-ordered `KafkaOptions` unification *destroyed* the per-service Kafka client id; the ledger row is why it came back, as a `required` member with no default. Confirmed at the compiler by the reviewer (`CS9035` when the initialiser is deleted). Traceability could not have seen it — the requirement was met either way — and arming could not have seen it, because every tested path behaves identically. A property destroyed and re-created inside one feature, caught by the instrument built for exactly that.
- **Enumerating the boundaries produced rows recollection would not have.** `L7` (the hinted committed-exposure Σ, with `SumAsync` appearing nowhere but in a comment forbidding it), `L11` (the `datetime2(3)` → `DateTimeOffset` conversion, a property #7 got from a `mysql2` pool option) and `L12` (the loud parse of the ledger `type` token). Phase 9's diagnosis was *the row nobody wrote*; enumeration is the answer to it.
- **And the instrument grew a new failure mode here: the row whose Guard column is decorative.** `L11`'s property was correct in the code and its named guard could not fail. That is the ledger's own version of the guard-that-does-not-guard, one level up. **Now a convention** in `CLAUDE.md`: *a ledger row's Guard column is itself a countable claim, and a countable claim is not done until it has been seen to fail.*
- **The caveat that must not be dropped:** you cannot observe a prevented defect, so "the ledger prevents" is an inference from `L26` rather than a measurement. It is now an inference with one concrete instance behind it instead of none.

### Backlog: four entries closed inside the feature, one opened

**48** (fresh `NatsHeaders` per saga command), **50** (responder `StopAsync` fault isolation, fixed in *both* services), **51** (RPC payload schemas parsed from `asyncapi.yaml` in all three unit-test projects, plus the twelve-value `RpcError.code` enum), **53** (three integration tests that detected a wrong-shaped reply only by a dereference, or not at all) and **54** (the Fulfillment ledger row nobody wrote, `L13`) all closed here, because the widened group A already had those files open. **52 stayed standalone**, exactly as its own acceptance requires. That is **five closures in one feature against Phase 9's one**, and it confirms the economics Phase 9 identified: *close a backlog entry inside a feature that already has the file open.*

One new advisory was opened rather than fixed: **`BC32`'s universal quantifier still over-reaches its guards at six sites** — `tests/Billing.IntegrationTests/CreditListTests.cs:39, :73, :77` (a file this feature authored) and `tests/Fulfillment.IntegrationTests/StockListTests.cs:33, :38, :44`. Found by an enumerating script, not by reading. These are the *opaque-failure* tier (a null dereference, so the test fails loudly but uninformatively) rather than the *silent-pass* tier that earned id 53 its `BC` id, and round 1 had fixed the scope at three sites — so it is recorded for the leader to file rather than made a second rejection.

### Notes for #9

- **A prescribed mutation is evidence only when it has been run.** If `tasks.md` names the mutation that must make a guard fail, run *that* mutation — not a nearby one, and not "the guard is green today". Feature 19's D1 is the case where the prescription was right, the code was right, and the guard was decorative.
- **Assert the value that came back through the adapter, never the value the test can recompute.** `new DateTimeOffset(row.CreditDate, TimeSpan.Zero)` written *in the test* asserts the test's own arithmetic. The property lives at `Mapper.ToDomain`; the assertion must too.
- **Deserialising an `RpcError` body into a typed reply record yields all-defaults without throwing.** JavaScript destructuring gave `undefined` and #7's assertions caught it; C# and Python both need the reply's own discriminating field asserted *before* any collection is touched, or a wrong-shaped reply is detected by a `NullReferenceException` at best and not at all at worst.
- **A `long`-vs-`int` money column, an `IF NOT EXISTS` seed and a storage-normalised key order were the first three ported-idiom losses; a timezone-less `datetime2` and a per-service client id were the next two.** Every one sits at a boundary where a value crosses into or out of the process. Enumerate those boundaries at spec time — the list is short and it is the cheapest defence this build has found.

**APPROVED on round 2** (4/4 round-1 defects closed and independently re-armed; **8/8 round-2 mutations killed by the named test**, and where the mutation was branch-specific, by that test alone; 2 new advisories, neither blocking). Full record: `progress/review_billing_credit.md` (round 1 §§1–9, round 2 appended below it).

## billing_credit_simulator (id 20, phase 10) — 2026-09-06 — **the phase's smallest feature, approved first pass, and the cleanest #7 comparison in the build — which is why the ≈1.6× is worth stating plainly**

**Effort:** **1 implementation session + 1 review pass, approved first time** — no spec phase, no human gate (`sdd: false`, exactly as #7). Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-06), bracketed at the start by the previous commit `19c623e` at **05:10**: **implementation ≈05:41 → 06:15, ≈34 min** — `current.md` 05:41, `Program.cs` 05:47, `.env.example` 05:47, `SimulatorCreditDecisionTests.cs` 05:50, `CreditSimulatorTests.cs` 05:52, `test-matrix.md` 05:55, `BillingServiceCollectionExtensions.cs` 06:01, `feature_list.json` 06:03, the `quality.sh` run 06:12, the impl report 06:15; on the commit bracket #7 itself used, **≈65 min**, of which ≈31 min is the gap between the commit and the first artefact. **Review ≈06:16 → ≈06:45, ≈29 min** — seven mutations, four container-backed runs (the full 55-test Billing integration suite plus three filtered), a real `diff -rq` of `specs/shared/` against the #7 checkout, and an independent enumeration of every integer literal in every Billing integration test. **Total ≈1 h 04 min from first artefact to verdict, ≈1 h 35 min on the commit bracket.** *Note: `SimulatorCreditDecision.cs`, `BillingOptions.cs` and `Program.cs` carry review-time mtimes (06:17–06:31) from mutation restores; all three are sha256-identical to the submitted versions and those timestamps are not implementation activity.*

**Against #7 — the like-for-like comparison this repository exists to produce, and #8 loses it:**

| Bucket | #8 | #7 | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| Implementation, commit-bracketed | ≈65 min | ≈20 min | **≈3.3×** |
| Implementation, first artefact → report | ≈34 min | ≈15 min | **≈2.3×** |
| Review | ≈29 min | ≈18 min | **≈1.6×** |
| **Total, first artefact → verdict** | **≈1 h 04 min** | **≈39 min** | **≈1.6×** |

**Same scope, same process, same first-pass approval, no spec phase on either side, and the seam pre-cut by feature 19 in both** — so almost nothing here cancels in #8's favour. Two things account for the gap and neither is a language effect. **First, #8 committed what #7 only probed:** the 200 000-draw measured failure-rate theory is a **test in the repository** here, whereas in #7 the identical measurement was a reviewer's throwaway probe that left with the review; add the boot-level `BillingHostCreditFailureRateBootTests`, the ported-idiom ledger row with its `IsFinite` guard, and the explicit re-verification of four inherited #7 findings, and roughly half the delta is deliverable that #7 does not have. **Second, ≈31 min elapsed between the previous commit and the first artefact** — dispatch and briefing overhead this harness pays per feature and #7 paid in five minutes. That half bought nothing and should not be narrated as if it did.

**What was built.** `SimulatorCreditDecision` behind `ICreditDecisionPort` — the `.99` cents rule evaluated **first** and winning unconditionally, then the failure-rate draw against an **injected** `Func<double>` defaulting to `Random.Shared.NextDouble` — plus `CreditSimulatorOptionsLoader` (R43's start-up validation: absent/empty → `0`, `double.TryParse` under `NumberStyles.Float` + `double.IsFinite` + closed-interval range, throwing with the offending value named), one option property, one DI line, one `Program.cs` line and `.env.example`. **42 insertions / 13 deletions across 8 tracked files, one new adapter, two new test files.** No domain, application or presentation file changed. `Billing.UnitTests` 94 → **136**, `Billing.IntegrationTests` 51 → **55**, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, `dotnet format` clean, **873 tests green** across thirteen projects.

**Approval evidence.** **7 hostile mutations, 6 killed by a named test, 1 survived.** The two that matter most: **the ordering property** — swapping the two `if` blocks so the pseudo-random draw runs before the `.99` predicate killed **exactly one** test (`TheCentsRuleWinsOverTheFailureRateRuleWhenBothCouldApply`, 1 failed / 135 passed), which is the correct discrimination, because every other R42 test is written at `failureRate = 0` and cannot see the swap; and **the payload family** — corrupting the refusal's **reason** (cents branch returning `SimulatedFailureRate`) was killed **at the wire** by two integration tests reading the value out of the RPC reply and the outbox `payload` column, `Expected: "simulated_cents_rule" / Actual: "simulated_failure_rate"`. Halving the effective rate (`_random() < _failureRate * 0.5`) was caught **only** by the measured 200 000-draw theory, which is the argument for committing that test rather than probing it. No probabilistic guard exists anywhere in this feature: with the generator injected, every claim is a deterministic assertion.

**Three findings, none blocking, and the confound column per the Phase 9 rule:**

| Finding | Would #7's standard have caught it? | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **N1** — the impl report's headline total says **883**; its own per-project itemisation and the `quality.sh` log both sum to **873** (827 at feature 19 + 42 unit + 4 integration = 873) | **Yes** | #7's reviewer re-ran the suites and compared counts; this is that check firing. The number that flows into README / PROCESS at wrap-up is **873** |
| **A1** — R43's *"fail to start"* clause is guarded at the loader and inside a test-authored `configure` delegate, but **deleting the production read at `src/Billing/Program.cs:23` leaves the suite green** | **No — raised bar** | Structural and pre-existing: **34 `GetEnvironmentVariable` calls across three `Program.cs` files are all unguarded**, and no test project compiles any of them. #7 explicitly declined the equivalent check. Filed for the leader before Notifications, Projector and Gateway add three more composition roots |
| **N2** — #7's own N2 (*fixture amounts are now silently subject to the `.99` rule; the protection is prose that rots*) was inherited as a **one-off search**, not the durable guard it asked for | **Yes, weakly** | The claim is true today — I re-enumerated every integer literal in every Billing integration test and the only `≡ 99 (mod 100)` hits are the intentional `24_999`s — and the full 55/55 run proves it. But feature 21 opens those files next, and a mysterious `simulated_cents_rule` in an invoicing test is the cheapest possible future time-sink |

The other four of #7's six findings were genuinely inherited: **N1** closed by construction (the R44 integration test compares the two payloads' **actual** key sets to each other, never to a hand-typed literal — the tautology #7 shipped), **N3** closed by the ported-idiom ledger and the `IsFinite` guard, **N5** closed by trimming the retention comment to the honest claim, **N6** already back-ported into the copied `test-matrix.md`.

### The ledger's second data point, and the first one that was cheap

Feature 19 produced the ledger's first observable save (`L26`, an expensive one — a property destroyed by a refactor and re-created). This feature produced a **cheap** one, which is the more repeatable evidence. The row reads: *#7 needed a hand-written plain-decimal-numeral regex before `Number()` because JS coerces `'0x1'` → `1` and whitespace → `0`; `double.TryParse` under `NumberStyles.Float` supplies that property in .NET — **except** for `"NaN"`/`"Infinity"`, which parse successfully in **both** runtimes and must still be hand-guarded.* The implementer wrote that row by running a nine-value probe rather than by recollection, kept the guard the row demanded, and put the reasoning in the shipped **doc comment** rather than only in `progress/`. Deleting `!double.IsFinite(rate)` kills exactly one theory case (`raw: "NaN"`), so the row's Guard column is not decorative — the failure mode the ledger itself grew in feature 19. **Two features, two ledger rows that did work, at a cost of a few minutes each.**

### Notes for #9

- **An injectable random source turns every probabilistic claim into a deterministic one — so commit the measurement, do not probe it.** `Func<double>` with a `Random.Shared.NextDouble` default cost one constructor parameter and bought a 200 000-draw theory that is the **only** guard catching a rate that is applied at half strength. #7 ran the same measurement as a reviewer's probe and the repository kept nothing.
- **Assert an ordering by fixing the other branch to its certain-to-fire configuration.** *"A `.99` amount is rejected"* passes with the two checks swapped most of the time. `failureRate = 1, random() = 0` makes the swap fail deterministically, and it is the one test in the file that can tell the two orderings apart.
- **A composition root is code, and nothing here tests it.** The `Program.cs` env-read line is where R43's start-up clause actually lives, and it can be deleted without failing anything. Decide early whether the reads live in a testable loader that `Program.cs` merely calls.
- **When you inherit a predecessor's finding, inherit the fix it asked for, not the observation.** N2 asked for a guard and got a search. The search was correct, and it will be stale the first time someone adds a fixture.

**APPROVED** first pass (**0 blocking defects**, 3 non-blocking findings; **6 of 7 hostile mutations killed by a named test**, the seventh surviving on a pre-existing project-wide gap now filed). Full record: `progress/review_billing_credit_simulator.md`.

## billing_invoicing (id 21, phase 10) — 2026-09-06 — **the first feature whose review found nothing wrong with the code and two things wrong with what the tests could see — and the first live sighting of a terminal saga rejection**

**Effort:** **1 spec session + 1 human gate + 1 implementation session + 1 coordinator live-boot pass + 2 review passes + 1 fix pass — REJECTED on round 1, APPROVED on round 2.** Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-06), bracketed at the start by feature 20's approval at ≈06:45 (previous commit `19c623e` at 05:10): **spec ≈07:04 → 07:23, ≈19 min** (`specs/billing_invoicing/` birth 07:04:09, `tasks.md` 07:18, the gate record `progress/spec_billing_invoicing.md` 07:20–07:23 with 20 open points); **human approval gate ≈07:23 → ≈07:25**; **implementation ≈07:25 → 08:52, ≈1 h 27 min** (`InvoiceStateTests.cs` 07:37, `InvoiceResponderValidationTests.cs` 07:39, `InvoiceListTests.cs` 07:49, `requirements.md`'s `H6` flip 08:43, `current.md` to `in_review` 08:52:15); **coordinator live boot (`H1`–`H4`) ≈08:52 → 09:15, ≈23 min** (the three service logs close at 09:14:57) — a separate pass, because the implementer had declared it out of budget; **review round 1 ≈09:15 → 09:36, ≈21 min** (verdict 09:36:13 — six mutation probes across both families, the full 74-test Testcontainers suite, an independent re-read of both live databases, a real `diff -rq specs/shared` against the #7 checkout, and a scratch `net10.0` project built to settle the `CS8878` claim), verdict **REJECTED, 3 blocking**; **fix round ≈09:36 → 10:01, ≈25 min** (`InvoiceTests.cs` 09:38, `InvoiceIssueTests.cs` 09:40, `test-matrix.md` 09:45, `design.md` 09:48, the report 10:01:18); **review round 2 ≈10:01 → ≈10:20, ≈19 min** — six mutations of my own at the two fixed sites, the full 74-test suite re-run against real containers at 4 m 22 s, an independently-written coverage-summary derivation, and a `Deserialize<…>`-rooted enumeration for backlog id 55. **Total ≈3 h 16 min from the first spec artefact to the final verdict**, across six agent passes and one human gate. *Note: `src/Billing/Domain/InvoiceState.cs` carries 09:27 and `Invoice.cs`, `BillingRpcResponder.cs` and `BillingFactPayloadMapper.cs` carry 10:06 from reviewers' mutation restores across the two rounds; all four are byte-identical to the submitted versions (`cmp`-verified against backups taken before arming, and the whole solution rebuilt `--no-incremental` afterwards) and those timestamps are not implementation activity.*

**Against #7 — the like-for-like comparison, and #8 loses it by ≈1.8×:**

| Bucket | #8 | #7 | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| Spec pass | ≈19 min | ≈3 min | **≈6.3×** |
| Implementation | ≈1 h 27 min | ≈1 h 07 min (live boot included) | ≈1.30× |
| Live boot | ≈23 min (separate coordinator pass) | — (inside the implementation pass) | — |
| Implementation + live boot | ≈1 h 50 min | ≈1 h 07 min | **≈1.64×** |
| Review round 1 | ≈21 min | ≈10 min | ≈2.1× |
| Fix pass | ≈25 min | ≈9 min | ≈2.8× |
| Review round 2 | ≈19 min | ≈7 min | ≈2.7× |
| **Total, first spec artefact → verdict** | **≈3 h 16 min** | **≈1 h 51 min** | **≈1.77×** |

**#7's baseline in full, because the shape matters more than the ratio:** 1 spec session + 1 human gate + 1 implementation session + 2 review passes + 1 fix pass, **approved on the second review**, with **2 blocking defects in round 1, neither of them a functional defect in shipped behaviour** — one was the discount check's placement relative to the transaction, one was `0 of 60` task boxes ticked. #8 has the identical shape — same number of passes, same rejection, same approval on round 2 — and that is the interesting result: **the process converged to the same cost structure, and the extra ≈1 h 25 min is spread across every bucket rather than concentrated in one.** The spec pass is 6× longer and bought the reuse dividend below; the review passes are ~2.5× longer because they arm mutations the #7 protocol never required.

**The reuse dividend here is invisible in every defect count, because it shows up as absence.** **#8's spec pre-empted both of #7's round-1 blockers by design.** #7's `N2` — the discount check's placement inside the transaction — became `BI2` in `requirements.md`, discharged at the **unit** level by observing the responder's entry (`F1`), which also absorbs #7's round-2 `N10`. #7's `N1` — 60 unticked boxes — became a `tasks.md` preamble making honest ticking-with-rewording an explicit close criterion. #7's non-blocking `N3`, `N4` and `N7` were likewise written in from the start (the ledger-row re-read after a refusal, the three-element lock log, the whole-table outbox delta), and round 1 confirmed all three present. **That is one full review round plus one fix pass that #8 never had to spend on #7's mistakes — and it does not appear anywhere in the ≈1.77×, which is measured against #7's cost *including* those rounds.** Stated here as rounds avoided, because nothing else in this record can show it.

**What was built.** The `Invoice` aggregate with a closed `issued → paid` state machine (`InvoiceState` rendered as a sealed class hierarchy, not `design.md`'s literal `abstract record` sketch — the synthesised copy constructor of a non-sealed record cannot be `private protected`, `CS8878`, so the class rendering **strengthens** the closure), `Invoice.Issue` with a `checked` total, `Invoice.MarkPaid` delivered and uncalled for feature 22, the `billing.invoice.issue` / `billing.invoice.list` NATS responders, `EfCoreInvoiceNumberAllocator`, `EfCoreInvoiceReadRepository`, the `invoices` / `invoice_items` tables, and the `CreditRpcResponder` → `BillingRpcResponder` rename (`BI31`) with **zero assertion text changed** across the three inherited responder test files. `Billing.UnitTests` 136 → **199**, `Billing.IntegrationTests` 55 → **74**, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16.

**Approval evidence.** **Twelve hostile mutations across the two review rounds, ten killed, two survived and became blocking defects.** Round 2's six all died: four at the `PaymentReceived` raise site (`ValueDate`, `Source`, `InvoiceReference`, `Amount` — each to a distinct verbatim failure), and two at the discount, one attacking the **inbound** wiring at the responder and one the **outbound** payload at the mapper, dying on different assertions seven lines apart. A thirteenth mutation did not compile and that was the finding: swapping `OrderReference` for `InvoiceReference` at the raise site is blocked by `CS1503` (`OrderNumber` versus `string`), so that pair is closed by the type system rather than by a test.

### The two blocking defects, and why they are one defect

**D1 and D2 were both payload-corruption survivals on a fully green suite, and neither was a bug in the production code.** `Invoice.cs`'s raise site was correct; `BillingRpcResponder.cs`'s discount wiring was correct. What was wrong is that **no test could tell.** `R46`'s case asserted the fact's count, its type and one field, so corrupting `ValueDate` and `Source` at the raise site left 199/199 green — and `payment.received.v1` has **no live caller**, so the unit suite was the only reachable guard. `BillingHostFixture.IssueRequest` defaulted `discount = 0` and mapped `0` to `null`, so the `discount` key was **omitted from every issue request the suite had ever sent**; the two assertions that would have caught a drop both expected `0`, which a dropped discount satisfies exactly. Both fixes were **test-side only** — `git diff --stat` on the two production files was empty afterwards, and the whole feature's source is unchanged from what round 1 rejected.

That is the point worth carrying: **this feature's code was right on the first pass and its tests were blind in two places, and the only thing that found either was mutating a payload field rather than deleting an emission.** `CLAUDE.md`'s two-mutation-family rule, written after feature 17, is now 2-for-2 on defects that a deletion probe cannot see.

### Findings, with the confound column

| Finding | Would #7's standard have caught it? | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **D1** (`payment.received.v1`'s payload survives corruption) | **No — raised bar** | #7 has the identical gap: `apps/billing/src/domain/invoice.spec.ts:109–111` asserts only `toHaveLength(1)` and the event type. The payload-corruption family is #8's own rule |
| **D2** (the request's `discount` droppable, both suites green) | **No — raised bar** | #7's `invoice-issue.integration.spec.ts` also used `discount: 0` on its success path and a non-zero discount only to prove a refusal. What makes it #8's defect is that #8's task `F3` *claims* the payload's discount equals the request's |
| **D3** (the coverage summary wrong by three rows) | **Yes** | #7's Phase 25 traceability pass found the identical drift and wrote the rule — *derived from the rows, never incremented by hand* — into the file #8 copied |
| **R2-N1** (`git diff` offered as restore evidence for **untracked** files — a check that cannot fail) | **No — raised bar, and self-inflicted** | The backup/`cmp`/forced-rebuild restore protocol is #8's, added in feature 7. #7 never verified restores at all, so it could not have had a *weak* verification to notice |
| **R2-N2** (the fix report misattributes which assertion killed the discount mutation) | **Yes, weakly** | #7's reviewers re-ran mutations themselves; this is that check firing |
| **R2-N3** (the enumerating command for backlog id 55 targets a proxy, not the candidate set) | **No — raised bar** | The enumerating-command rule is #8's. **And the proxy was prescribed by the round-1 reviewer**, so this one is the harness's own error, found by the harness one round later |
| N1, N2, N3, N5 (record hygiene, still open) | **Yes** | #7 was rejected on this very feature partly for `0 of 60` boxes ticked |

**Two record corrections are owed before commit** and the approval says so explicitly: `tasks.md`'s `H1`–`H4` still tick over the sentence *"NOT PERFORMED this session"* about work the coordinator did perform and the reviewer independently confirmed, and `requirements.md`'s `BI22` row still reads `TODO`. Neither is a code defect and neither blocks; both would be permanently false in the committed record.

### The first live sighting of a terminal saga rejection — and what it says about deliberately corrupt fixtures

The coordinator's live boot resolved four parked `invoice.issue` commands to `sent` with four invoices (the allocator continuing past the seed to `INV-000006`), placed a fresh control order that traversed `placed → … → invoiced` unattended, and — the interesting one — resolved a **fifth** command to `rejected`. `ORD-000011` sat at `despatched` with its credit hold already gone, because a reviewer had released it by hand over raw NATS during feature 19's live-boot task `I4`, eleven hours earlier. The invoicing responder found the two write models in disagreement, answered `PRECONDITION_FAILED` / `NO_ACTIVE_HOLD`, and **feature 42's classification stopped the row instead of retrying it forever.** Three independent checks confirmed no saga path produced the state: no `credit.release` row in `saga_commands`, the order never cancelled, and the `reason: "order_cancelled"` in the payload traceable to the hand-typed probe's own request field.

**Does that argue for or against leaving such fixtures in a shared live stack? For — but only under a condition this feature did not meet.** The case for is strong and was free: `ORD-000011` produced the **only** end-to-end observation of the terminal-rejection path in the build so far, and no integration test can reach it, because no live caller can manufacture a released-hold-plus-despatched-order state. A corrupt fixture in a long-lived stack is an accumulating source of exactly the states the happy path cannot construct, and deleting it would have thrown that away to buy tidiness.

The case against is entirely about cost of *rediscovery*, and it was paid here: a review round spent real attention establishing that a `rejected` row was a feature and not a regression, and the hand-over note that stops the next reader repeating that (`design.md` §18) was written only after a reviewer asked for it — **two features after the fixture was created.** The condition, then: **the feature that corrupts a shared fixture records it, in that feature's own hand-over, in the same pass that corrupts it.** Feature 19 released the hold and wrote one line about it in its impl report; feature 21 paid for that line not being in a hand-over. Better still, and the recommendation for phase 11: a **single register of known-corrupt live fixtures** at the stack level, rather than a note in one feature's `design.md` §18 that phase 12 has no reason to open.

### Notes for #9

- **Deleting an emission and corrupting a payload find different defects, and a suite that only does the first will ship payload defects indefinitely.** Both of this feature's blocking defects were correct production code with a blind test, and both needed a field mutated rather than a row deleted. Twelve mutations, ten killed; the two that survived were the whole of the review's value.
- **A test that supplies `0` for a field cannot see that field being dropped.** `discount = 0` mapped to `null` and vanished from the wire, so the entire discount chain — responder to command to aggregate to column to fact — had never once been exercised at a non-zero value, in any test, in either assessment. Make every money field in a fixture a distinct non-zero number, and check that no two of them coincide.
- **An enumerating command must be rooted at the thing the claim quantifies over.** A claim about *"every test that deserialises an RPC reply"* is enumerated from `Deserialize<…>`, never from the member name the known instances happen to share — three reply types here carry their collection as `Lines`, `Reservations` or `Shortages`, and a search for `.Items` cannot see any of them. This is the third proxy-for-candidate-set finding in the build, and the first where the reviewer wrote the proxy.
- **`git diff` is not evidence about an untracked file.** Mid-feature, most source is untracked, so `git diff --stat` returns empty whether the file is pristine or still armed. `cmp` against a backup you took, plus re-reading the changed line, plus a forced rebuild — all three, as the protocol already says.
- **When a matrix group is broader than a backlog feature, say so in the spec before the reviewer has to work it out.** `specs/billing_invoicing/requirements.md` names R47–R49 as feature 22's in its first paragraph, which turned a potential rule-3 violation into a two-minute confirmation against #7's own precedent.

**APPROVED on round 2** (3/3 blocking defects closed and independently re-armed; **6/6 round-2 mutations killed by the named test**, at two independent corruption sites for the same field; 3 new non-blocking findings, one of them against the round-1 reviewer's own prescription; **2 record corrections owed before commit**). Backlog **id 55 closed** in the same verdict on combined evidence. Full record: `progress/review_billing_invoicing.md` (round 1 §§1–11, round 2 §§R2.1–R2.11 appended below it).

**Correction to commit `dad02dd`'s subject line.** It reads *"closes all four Billing features"*. It does not: it closes **three of four** — 19, 20 and 21. Feature 22 `billing_remittance_intake` is still `pending`, and Phase 10 is therefore not complete. Recorded here rather than by rewriting a pushed commit.

Same class as the 508→635 test-count error in `5a84e81`, and by the same mechanism: a summary sentence written from a sense of what the session had achieved rather than from the artefact that could settle it. The backlog was one query away. **The rule this repository already has covers it** — *do not write a figure into a commit message you have not read off a run in the same session* — and "all four" is a figure. The generalisable widening: **a claim of completeness is a count, and a count is a reading.** Neither "all", "every" nor "the last" belongs in a commit subject unless the thing counted was enumerated first.

Twice now the coordinator has done this in a commit message, both times about scale rather than about behaviour, and both times in the artefact the repository treats as process evidence. That the code was correct on both occasions is not mitigation; it is the reason nobody caught it before the push.

## billing_remittance_intake (id 22, phase 10) — 2026-09-06 — **closes Phase 10 and closes the order-to-cash cycle end to end for the first time in this repository**

**Effort:** **1 implementation session + 1 review pass, APPROVED first time, 0 blocking defects** — no spec phase, no human gate (`sdd: false`, exactly as #7); the specification of record is `specs/shared/requirements.md` R47–R49 plus `feature_list.json` id 22's three acceptance bullets. Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-06), bracketed at the start by the previous commit `530dc0b` at **11:12:51**: **implementation ≈11:26 → 12:22, ≈56 min** — `progress/current.md` 11:26:18 opens the pass, `PaymentSnapshot.cs` 11:32:08, `PaymentRowMapper.cs` 11:32:54, `RegisterPaymentCommand.cs` 11:35:56, `PaymentRegisterRequestValidator.cs` 11:37:50, `PaymentRegisterResponderValidationTests.cs` 11:42:59, `PaymentRegisterTests.cs` 11:57:42, `EfCoreInvoiceRepository.cs` 11:59:33, `PaymentRegisterServiceTests.cs` 12:09:50, `test-matrix.md` 12:13:56, the impl report 12:22:40 and the `in_review` transition 12:22:50 — **including seven armed mutations across two families and the live two-service boot whose walkthrough carried `ORD-000007` from `invoiced` to `completed` at 12:07 local**; **review ≈12:23 → ≈12:55, ≈32 min** — nine of the reviewer's own mutations with five forced `--no-incremental` solution rebuilds, the **whole** `Billing.IntegrationTests` project re-run end to end (83/83, 4 m 25 s) because the claim under test was that suite's own count, `Billing.UnitTests` 225/225, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, `./init.sh` exit 0, a real `diff -rq specs/shared` against the #7 checkout, a repo-wide timer enumeration across seven file types plus `AddHostedService` and `*Options` sweeps, nine read-only `sqlcmd` batches against the two live databases, an RCSI/lock-ordering check at the engine, and a YAML parse of `asyncapi.yaml`'s two payment schemas. **Total ≈1 h 28 min from `current.md` to verdict, ≈1 h 42 min on the commit bracket.** *Note: `src/Billing/Application/PaymentRegisterService.cs` carries a review-time mtime of 12:36 from mutation restores; it is md5-identical to the submitted version (`5eab1789…`), `cmp`-verified against a backup and rebuilt `--no-incremental` before the confirming green run, and that timestamp is not implementation activity.*

**Against #7 — the like-for-like comparison, and #8 loses it again, by less than the phase average:**

| Phase | #8 | #7 | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| Implementation | ≈56 min | ≈48 min | ≈1.17× |
| Review | ≈32 min | ≈13 min | ≈2.5× |
| **Total, first artefact → verdict** | **≈1 h 28 min** | **≈1 h 01 min** | **≈1.44×** |

**The implementation gap is the smallest of the phase and is almost entirely deliverable rather than friction.** #8 wrote nine integration tests to #7's seven and shipped #7's own review finding **N11** as *prevention* — the sequential cross-invoice reuse of a `paymentReference` that #7 answered with a success-shaped `duplicate` naming a different invoice, and which #7 left as a follow-up for its Gateway phase. #8's fast path compares the caller's identifier against the invoice the reference actually resolved, throws the same `PaymentReferenceConflictError` the concurrent path's UNIQUE-constraint backstop throws, and has a named test for each direction. That is one #7 review round's finding, closed before the code was first submitted, at a cost inside the 8-minute implementation delta.

**The review gap is real and is the harness, not the language.** #7's review ran one re-armed mutation, three targeted unit files and a bespoke probe spec, and it correctly declined to re-run the world. #8's ran nine mutations across two families and re-ran a container-backed suite whose 4 m 25 s is, on its own, a third of #7's entire review. What that bought is stated plainly in the confound column below: nothing #7's standard would have found, and two things it structurally could not have.

**Approval evidence.** **9 reviewer mutations, 8 killed by a named test, 1 survived.** The ordering swap (`credits.SaveChangesAsync` above `invoices.MarkPaidAsync`) was re-armed from scratch and killed **at both levels** — the unit test's shared `callLog`, into which both hand-built repository fakes append, is a call-order assertion across two aggregates rather than a row-existence one, and the integration test independently asserts the resulting `outbox.seq` order in real MS-SQL. Corrupting the **second** fact's `reason` (`invoice_paid` → `order_cancelled`) was killed at the wire by exactly one test, which is the correct discrimination; corrupting the duplicate reply's `paidAt` by one second was killed only by the integration suite, which is what proves the *"a repeat returns the ORIGINAL `paidAt`"* claim rather than merely a `duplicate` outcome. The survivor is finding **N2**.

**Three findings, none blocking, with the confound column:**

| Finding | Would #7's standard have caught it? | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **N1** — `credit.released.v1` carries the same `causationId` as `payment.received.v1` (both the request id) instead of the payment fact's `eventId`, and the two share an identical `occurredAt`, so the completion pair is a tie group with **no recorded causal edge** and will render in the projector's *"deterministic but causally arbitrary"* fallback order | **No — and it could not have.** This is #7's **amendment A1**, and #7 did not have it either at this point: it discovered the property eight phases later and fixed it as production rework in `bf59af9 fix(projector): order tied timeline entries by causal edge, not by luck`. #8 inherited the *amended* spec (`openapi.yaml`'s `TimelineEntry` description, `test-matrix.md`'s R24 row) and can therefore see, today, a defect #7 could only find by operating the system | Live outbox rows `seq 10015`/`10016`: same `causationId E95C9176…`, same `occurredAt 10:07:07.057`. #7's `payment-register.handler.ts:174–202` shows both the fix and the seam it needs — a `markPaid` that **returns** its event id, where #8's `Invoice.MarkPaid` returns `void` |
| **N2** — `command.ValueDate` can be corrupted by 42 days with **225/225 unit and 9/9 integration green**; the R47 integration test's own comment claims the payload is asserted *"field by field"*, and `valueDate` is the field it is not | **No — raised bar, and #7 has the identical gap** (verified: `payment-register.handler.spec.ts` uses `valueDate` only as fixture input, never as an assertion). The payload-corruption mutation family is #8's own rule, written after feature 17 | Probe P4, command and complete output in `progress/review_billing_remittance_intake.md` §7 |
| **N3** — `BuyerCredit.Release` returns `null` (no entry, no fact) when an order has no outstanding exposure, and `PaymentRegisterService` **discards the return value**, so such a payment would emit `payment.received.v1` alone and leave the order at `paid` forever, since completion is conditioned on `credit.released.v1` | **n/a — exact parity.** `#7`'s `payment-register.handler.ts:199` calls `credit.releaseHold(...)` with no assignment either, while both assessments' *release* RPC handles the `null` explicitly. Unreachable through any live path in either build; reachable only through a hand-corrupted fixture of `ORD-000011`'s kind. Recorded, not actioned — acting unilaterally would be a divergence in a benchmark repository | `src/Billing/Domain/BuyerCredit.cs:212–216`, `CreditReleaseService.cs:37–46`, `PaymentRegisterService.cs:128` |

**#7's own findings on this feature, checked for inheritance rather than rediscovered.** **N11** was inherited as prevention and independently verified in both directions (above). **N12** (the impl record's count off by one) did not recur where checkable — 225 unit, 83 integration and the five `issued` invoices in the pre-state are all exact, and the two derived `test-matrix.md` count cells recompute correctly. **N13** — #7 permanently stranded `ORD-000007` by putting a random UUID in the correlation header of its own walkthrough script, leaving an order whose invoice was paid, whose credit was released and which never completed, visible in shared demo data forever. **#8 used the order's own id and the order completed**; the outbox rows confirm it. That is the clearest single instance in the phase of a #7 review round bought outright by reading its record.

**What was built.** The `billing.payment.register` NATS responder and `PaymentRegisterService` — the sole live caller of `Invoice.MarkPaid` (feature 21) and `BuyerCredit.Release` (feature 19), both delivered uncalled. An R48 fast path outside any transaction; then, inside one `IUnitOfWork.ExecuteAsync`, the credit line locked first (`BI8`), the invoice row locked second, an authority re-read closing the race the fast path leaves open, `Invoice.MarkPaid` raising all three R49 refusals itself, `BuyerCredit.Release(invoice_paid)`, and `invoices.MarkPaidAsync` awaited strictly before `credits.SaveChangesAsync` so `payment.received.v1` takes a lower `outbox.seq` than `credit.released.v1`. Plus the `payments` UNIQUE-constraint backstop translated to a typed error the same way `ProcessedEventLedger` and `EfCoreSagaCommandStore` already do, six new repository methods, two new application errors with their wire mappings, and the sixth subject on the **same** responder rather than a fourth responder class. **No migration and no `Contracts` change — both verified unnecessary before code was written, not merely unattempted.** `src/Orders/` was not touched, and the saga still carried the order to `completed`. `Billing.UnitTests` 201 → **225**, `Billing.IntegrationTests` 74 → **83**; the implementer's full `quality.sh` reports **991 tests green across 13 projects**, exit 0, of which this review independently re-ran **324** (225 + 83 + 16).

**Something the review is obliged to say about its own limits.** N1 was not found by traceability, which is satisfied — R47 asks for the two facts *in that order in the same transaction*, and they are, provably, at two levels. It was not found by arming either: the behaviour is correct on every path any test takes, and nine mutations killed eight tests without touching it. It was found by asking the ledger's question — *what made this correct over there, and does that thing exist here?* — of a feature that, being `sdd: false`, has no `design.md` and therefore carries no ledger. That is the fourth time in this build the class has surfaced, and the first time it has surfaced **before** shipping.
