## Phase 10 closing assessment — four features, two rejections, the ledger's best and worst day, and a phase that cost ≈1.5× for reasons that are now individually itemisable

### 1. The clock, with the gate-ordered work separated from the baseline-comparable work

| Feature | #8, first artefact → verdict | #7 | Ratio | Rounds (#8 / #7) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 19 `billing_credit` | ≈5 h 39 min | ≈3 h 15 min | 1.74× | 2 reviews, 1 rejection / 2 reviews, 1 rejection |
| 20 `billing_credit_simulator` | ≈1 h 04 min | ≈39 min | 1.6× | 1 / 1, both first-pass |
| 21 `billing_invoicing` | ≈3 h 16 min | ≈1 h 51 min | 1.77× | 2 reviews, 1 rejection / 2 reviews, 1 rejection |
| 22 `billing_remittance_intake` | ≈1 h 28 min | ≈1 h 01 min | 1.44× | 1 / 1, both first-pass |
| **Phase total** | **≈11 h 27 min** | **≈6 h 46 min** | **≈1.69×** | **6 review rounds each, 2 rejections each** |

**Separating the work that has no #7 counterpart at all** — feature 19's human gate overruled two deferrals with a standing *"stop leaving issues to the next phase"* instruction, which produced a seven-file × three-service outbox-relay unification, a `required` Kafka client id, `checked` money arithmetic in `src/SharedKernel`, and five backlog closures. That is ≈45 min of gate-ordered work and ≈35 min of backlog closure inside feature 19, ≈1 h 20 min in total, against which #7 refactored no shipped service, touched no shared kernel and closed no backlog entries in this phase.

| Bucket | #8 | #7 | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Baseline-comparable** — the four Billing features as #7 built them | **≈10 h 07 min** | **≈6 h 46 min** | **≈1.49×** |
| **Gate-ordered, no #7 counterpart** | ≈45 min | none | n/a |
| **Backlog closures, no #7 counterpart** | ≈35 min | none | n/a |

**≈1.49× on the comparable subset**, against Phase 9's figures and Phase 8's 1.76×. The trend is real but it is flattening, and the honest reading is that it is flattening because the remaining gap is not one thing: it is ≈2–2.5× review on every feature, ≈30 min of per-feature dispatch and briefing overhead that #7 paid in five minutes, and a genuinely larger deliverable (committed probes, boot-level tests, ledger rows, arming tables) that #7 did not write. **The two features with no spec phase and no gate — 20 and 22 — are the cleanest comparisons in the phase, and they are 1.6× and 1.44×.** That is the number to carry into Phase 11, because Phases 11–13 are `sdd: false`-heavy.

### 2. The confound column across the whole phase

Thirteen findings and defects across the four features. **Four would have been caught by #7's own standard**: feature 19's D3 (its own `W3`/`N5` probe shape, which #7 ran and shipped as a nit), feature 20's N1 (a test-count arithmetic error — #7's reviewers re-ran suites and compared counts), feature 21's D3 (the coverage-summary drift, whose *"derive it from the rows"* rule #7 wrote in its Phase 25 and #8 copied) and feature 21's R2-N2 (a misattributed mutation, caught by re-running it).

**Nine are raised-bar finds** — they exist because #8's harness asks questions #7's did not: the two-mutation-family rule (feature 21's D1 and D2, feature 22's N2), the enumerating-command rule (feature 20's A1, feature 21's R2-N3), the *arm every countable claim, not only the flagged ones* rule (feature 19's D1, D2, D4), and the backup/`cmp`/forced-rebuild restore protocol (feature 21's R2-N1, which is #8 catching #8).

**And one is a category the confound column did not previously have: a defect #7's standard could not have caught, because #7 had not yet learned it.** Feature 22's N1 is #7's amendment A1, made eight phases later than the feature it belongs to. #8 inherited the amended spec, so a #8 reviewer can see at feature 22 what #7 could only find by operating a projector in Phase 18. **This is the reuse dividend appearing in the review column rather than the implementation column** — and it is the first time in the build it has done so. If the leader acts on it now, #8 skips a cross-service rework #7 paid for in full; if it is deferred to Phase 12, #8 will reproduce that rework exactly, from inside the projector's pass, in a closed Billing feature.

### 3. The ported-idiom ledger, on the evidence of two features that used it

Phase 10 is the ledger's first full phase, and it produced **its best and its worst evidence in the same feature**.

**The save is real and is this build's first observable one.** Feature 19's gate-ordered outbox unification copied one canonical relay family into three services, and in doing so destroyed a Kafka client id that had been supplied implicitly; the ledger row was what re-created it. Feature 20 produced the second, cheaper data point (the `IsFinite` guard on the simulator's failure-rate parse). **The standing caveat stands and must be repeated in the final benchmark: you cannot observe a prevented defect.** *"It prevents"* is an inference from two instances, not a measurement.

**Its first new failure mode was a decorative row** — feature 19 shipped a ledger row whose guard test could not fail, which is the guard-that-does-not-guard pattern reappearing inside the very mechanism adopted to catch a different blindness. A ledger row is a claim, and a claim needs arming like any other.

**Its second new failure mode is structural, and Phase 10 is where it bit: the ledger lives in `design.md`, so an `sdd: false` feature has none.** Feature 22 ports #7's `payment-register.handler.ts` wholesale, has no `design.md`, carries no ledger — and its one finding of substance (N1) is exactly a ledger-class miss: *#7 relied on a `markPaid` that returns its event id; in #8 that property is supplied by nothing.* Traceability could not see it (R47 is satisfied), arming could not see it (nine mutations, eight kills, none nearby), and it took a reviewer reading #7's source for an unrelated question. **Phases 11–13 are Notifications, Projector and Gateway, all ported from the same source and all `sdd: false` in the current backlog.** The recommendation to the human gate is one line long: **bind the ledger to the port, not to the document** — an `sdd: false` feature that ports a #7 mechanism owes its ledger in `progress/impl_<feature>.md`, where its arming table already lives.

Backlog ids **52** (the retroactive ledger for pre-ledger services) and **54** (the missing row for the un-hinted in-transaction re-read) are both still `pending` — so the ledger's own backlog grew this phase rather than shrank. Feature 22 added a second instance of 54's pattern in a different service (`EfCoreInvoiceRepository.FindPaymentByInvoiceIdAsync`, correct under RCSI because it is issued after the credit-row `UPDLOCK` is granted, and its doc comment names that), so whoever closes 54 now writes two rows.

### 4. The backlog, and whether the attachment map earned its keep

**It did, and the evidence is a number: five closures in one feature, and six in the phase, against Phase 9's one.**

The map was written into `progress/current.md` before the first Phase 10 implementer was dispatched, and it did exactly one thing — it said, for each open entry, *which feature already has that file open*. Ids **48**, **50**, **51**, **53** closed inside feature 19 because its gate-widened group A had those files open anyway; **55** closed inside feature 21 for the same reason; **49** had closed inside feature 18 one phase earlier on the same logic. **52** stayed standalone because its own acceptance forbids fixing anything in place, and **56** was deliberately deferred to Phase 13 so the mechanism is chosen once for four composition roots instead of four times. Both of those are the map declining to attach, which is the harder half of the judgement and the part that proves it was a judgement rather than a wish.

Two corrections belong in the record. **First, id 54's status is wrong.** Its work landed (feature 19's task G3, `specs/fulfillment_stock/design.md:572` row `L13`, confirmed by two reviewers reading the row), feature 19's history entry counts it among the phase's closures — and `feature_list.json` still says `pending`. That is a backlog entry satisfied but not closed, which `init.sh`'s tripwire cannot detect, because a `pending` entry is a valid entry. **Owner: leader**, at wrap-up; this review deliberately did not flip it, because a reviewer's bookkeeping mandate covers the feature under review and nothing else. **Second, the phase opened four new entries and closed six**, which is the first phase in this build where the backlog shrank.

### 5. Two coordinator errors, and whether the rule that came out of the second one is adequate

They are recorded here rather than fixed by rewriting pushed commits.

**Commit `5a84e81` stated a test count of 635 that no run produced** (the true figure was 508). **Commit `dad02dd`'s subject said it closed *"all four Billing features"*; it closed three** — feature 22 was still `pending` and Phase 10 was not complete. The first produced the rule *do not write a figure into a commit message you have not read off a run in the same session*; the second produced the widening *a claim of completeness is a count, and a count is a reading — neither "all", "every" nor "the last" belongs in a commit subject unless the thing counted was enumerated first.*

**Is the widened rule adequate, given it is the same rule that failed to prevent the first?** **Partly, and the part it does not cover is the part that recurred.** As a *statement* it is correct and it does now name the failing case: "all four" is a count, and a `python3 -c` over `feature_list.json` would have settled it in one command. What it does not supply is any reason the rule would be **consulted** at the moment of writing a commit subject — and that is precisely what went wrong twice. Both errors were written at the same point in the workflow (a session-closing summary), by the same actor, about the same kind of quantity (scale, not behaviour), and in both cases the correct figure was one command away. A rule that depends on the author remembering it at the exact moment their attention is on wrapping up has already failed twice at that moment.

The honest assessment is therefore: **the rule is correctly stated and structurally weak, and the fix is not a third widening but a check that fires without being remembered.** `init.sh` already runs a backlog tripwire and already knows how to count features by status; a commit-message pass that refuses any subject line containing `all`, `every`, `both`, `the last` or a bare integer unless the same session recorded the enumeration would move this from discipline to mechanism. Until that exists, the third occurrence should be assumed rather than hoped against — and, per this repository's own standard, **a rule whose only enforcement is the author's memory is the guard-that-does-not-guard in its fourth disguise.** That is the phase's most useful process finding and it should go to the human gate as a recommendation with this evidence, not as an open question.

### 6. What Phase 10 delivered

The whole Billing service: the `BuyerCredit` aggregate with an append-only ledger and the `availableCredit = limit − activeHolds − openExposure` identity; the `.99` credit simulator behind an injected generator; the `Invoice` aggregate with the `issued → paid` state machine and `INV-######` allocation; and remittance intake, which made the first two callable and closed the cycle. Six NATS RPC subjects on one responder, Billing's own outbox and relay publishing five fact types, `Billing.UnitTests` 0 → **225** and `Billing.IntegrationTests` 0 → **83** inside the phase, and a solution suite of **991 green across 13 projects**.

And the thing the phase actually exists to have proved: **an order placed through the saga now reaches `completed` unattended, driven end to end by facts, with a payment arriving from outside the system and no internal timer anywhere.** That was verified live, from both databases, on real infrastructure, twice — once for the accepted path and once for a refusal that moved nothing.

### 7. Notes for #9

- **Bind the ported-idiom ledger to the port, not to the document.** A feature with no `design.md` still ports mechanisms, and the class the ledger exists to catch is invisible to both traceability and arming. Put the ledger in the implementation record when there is no design.
- **When you inherit an amended spec, the amendments are free findings — go looking for them deliberately.** #8 could see feature 22's causal-edge gap only because #7's later amendment was already in the shared spec it copied. Nobody had planned to look; it surfaced from asking *"what did #7 do here?"* about an adjacent question. Make it a standing check at every ported feature: `grep` the shared spec for `amendment` and ask which half of each one the current feature owns.
- **A repeat-request test must assert the value the first request produced, not merely the outcome word.** *"Returns `duplicate`"* survives a reply that fabricates a fresh `paidAt`; `Assert.Equal(firstPayload.PaidAt, repeatPayload.PaidAt)` does not.
- **Judge a claimed absence by its candidate set, not by its output.** The timer sweep here only became convincing when it left `.cs` for the n8n workflow that genuinely does schedule payments, and then went on to enumerate every `AddHostedService` and every `*Options` class — three independent enumerations of the same claim, two of which the original command could not reach.
- **A rule enforced only by the author's memory will fail at exactly the moment it is needed.** Two commit-message errors, one rule, one widening, and the widening does not address why the rule was not consulted. Prefer a check that runs.

---

## notifications_service (id 23, phase 11) — 2026-09-06 — **closes Phase 11; the same round count as #7 for entirely different reasons, and the first feature rejected on defects #7's standard could not have seen**

**Effort:** **2 implementation sessions + 2 review passes, REJECTED once then approved** — no spec phase, no human gate (`sdd: false`, exactly as #7; the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 23's three acceptance bullets, `specs/shared/domain-model.md` §6/§7.3 and R17/R18). Wall-clock from artefact mtimes and birth times (local CEST, 2026-09-06), bracketed at the start by the Phase 10 wrap commit `f925ff5` at **14:01** — the same bracket #7 used for its own entry. **Implementation round 1 + review round 1 together occupy 14:01 → 17:22**, the verdict file `progress/review_notifications_service.md` being born at 17:22; the boundary between them cannot be read precisely, because the fix round rewrote `progress/impl_notifications_service.md` (birth 18:01) and several round-1 source files carry restore mtimes from two rounds of mutation probes — from the round-1 review's own probe list (four suites including a 1 m 30 s real-container run, a live host session against Kafka/MS-SQL/Mailpit, and a 68-mutation template sweep) it was **≈50–60 min**, putting implementation round 1 at **≈2 h 20**. **Fix round 17:22 → 18:01, ≈39 min** — `NotificationFactsConsumerTests.cs` 17:28, `KafkaFactStreamSubscriberConfigTests.cs` 17:33, the seven `*TemplateTests.cs` 17:36, `feature_list.json` 17:59, the report 18:01. **Review round 2 18:12 → 18:40, ≈28 min** wall-clock, of which ≈25 min was an 89-site mutation sweep running in the background while the traceability walk and the C1–C7 checks proceeded. **Total ≈4 h 39 min commit-bracketed**, of which ≈1 h 30 was review across two passes.

**Against #7 — commit-bracketed on both sides, which is the only like-for-like available here:**

| Bucket | #8 | #7 | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| Implementation round 1 | ≈2 h 20 (derived) | 1 h 39 | ≈1.4× |
| Review round 1 | ≈50–60 min (derived) | 29 min | ≈1.9× |
| Fix round | 39 min | 28 min | ≈1.4× |
| Review round 2 | 28 min | 24 min | ≈1.2× |
| **Total, previous commit → final verdict** | **≈4 h 39 min** | **≈3 h 01 min** | **≈1.5×** |

**Same round count, opposite content.** #7 was rejected on **five blocking findings, four of them behavioural**, the headline one being an in-memory `Set` for the dedup ledger that produced three emails for one `eventId` against a real broker and cost a fourth MySQL database added mid-feature. #8 was rejected on **two blocking findings, both in the guards and neither in the code** — round 1's own words were *"everything the feature claims to do, it does"*, and it verified that live: seven real Mailpit messages for seven published facts, a `stock.reserved.v1` control producing none, and a byte-identical redelivery of all seven leaving the inbox and the ledger unchanged. **Identical arithmetic, entirely different meaning: #7 spent its second round making the service correct, #8 spent its second round making the correctness defended.**

**What was built.** An `INotificationSender` port with a console adapter (bound by every automated test, through the port) and a MailKit adapter driven by an injected `Func<ISmtpTransport>` seam (bound only by `Program.cs`, against Mailpit); seven pure `Envelope<TPayload> → NotificationMessage` templates in `Application/Templates/` — deliberately **not** #7's `infrastructure/templates/`, which would have made `Application` depend on `Infrastructure`; `NotificationDispatchService` with insert-first / send / delete-on-throw and #7's N6 and N13 lessons present **in the first submission**; seven commands and handlers through the hand-rolled dispatcher; the canonical `IdempotentConsumer`/`ProcessedEventLedger` pair copied byte-identically; and one Kafka `BackgroundService` over three fact topics. `Notifications.UnitTests` 0 → **58**, `Notifications.IntegrationTests` 7 → **12**, solution **1054 green across 14 projects**. No new NuGet package versions: everything was already pinned in `Directory.Packages.props`.

**The reuse dividend, which shows up as an absence and is therefore worth counting explicitly.** Four of #7's five round-1 blockers **could not occur here**: the acceptance bullet already said *durable ledger* (written into `feature_list.json` from #7's outcome), `otc_notifications.processed_events` already existed from phase 6 so no database was added mid-feature, `specs/outbox_and_idempotency/design.md` §6.3 had already named the record-before-or-after-send question **at design time**, and #7's N6 and N13 fixes both shipped in the first submission with named guards. **That is roughly one implementation round and one review round of #7's cost that did not happen** — and it is invisible in the round count, because #8 spent a round anyway, on defects of a class #7 did not have.

**The rejection, and the confound column — the most important line in this entry.** Round 1's two blockers were **D1**, five of the seven notified facts deletable from the consumer's filter with both suites green, and **D2**, 45 of 68 single-field template mutations surviving the unit suite, including the recipient address of the completed and cancelled emails. **Neither would have been caught by #7's standard, and that is not a guess:** #7's `notification-facts.controller.spec.ts` tested the *exclusion* side only and its reviewer's Probe 5 armed the seven **handlers**, exactly as #8's round-1 implementer did; and #7's handler specs asserted `toHaveBeenCalledWith(ENVELOPE, buildXMessage)` — builder **identity**, which catches a wrong-template copy-paste but never a wrong field inside the right template, and #7's reviewer explicitly signed that off as sufficient. Both findings exist only because `CLAUDE.md`'s **second mutation family** — *corrupt a payload field on the wire, do not only delete the emission* — was added after #7, in feature 17, and because round 1 applied it to the routing table as well as to the payload. **This is the strongest evidence in the build so far that the raised bar finds real defects rather than manufacturing rounds:** the extra round bought a routing table that cannot silently lose a fact type and seven templates whose every string-typed field is asserted in both renderings, and neither was reachable by the process that produced #7.

**The fix, and why it is the better fix rather than the smaller one.** D1 was closed by collapsing the `HashSet` filter and the routing `switch` into **one** `IReadOnlyDictionary` whose `Keys` **is** the notified-fact set — there is no longer a second list to agree with — guarded by a new test that drives `ExecuteAsync` itself rather than the handlers behind it. Round 2 confirmed `Keys` is genuinely the only enumeration (`grep` for the seven `eventType` literals across `src/Notifications/` returns the seven dictionary keys and nothing else) and killed it in both families: deleting one entry fails a named theory case, swapping two arms fails two. D2 was closed by asserting the recipient in all seven `*TemplateTests` and each template's identifying references in **both** `Text` and `Html`. D3 — the `AutoOffsetReset.Latest` ledger entry, argued at length but asserted by nothing — was closed with a reflection-based config guard that also pins `GroupId`, `ClientId` and the offset-store settings.

**Round 2's own evidence, none of it taken from the report.** The implementer's sweep reported `TOTAL=70 SURVIVED=0`, and *a sweep that reports every mutation caught is exactly as trustworthy as a suite that reports green*. So round 2 wrote its **own** sweep, enumerated the sites independently by regex, and proved the machinery before believing any verdict: a sentinel whose search text does not exist reports **not applied** (and all 89 real sites carried a SHA-256-verified file change), a doc-comment-only edit reports **SURVIVED** — proving the sweep *can* report a survivor — and round 1's worst survivor, the completed-order recipient, reports **CAUGHT**. The independent enumeration found **exactly 70** string-typed sites, matching the implementer's per-file counts file by file, and **70/70 caught**. **That check was not theoretical:** the reviewer's own first attempt at the D3 probe replaced the first textual occurrence of `AutoOffsetReset.Latest`, which is a `<see cref="..."/>` in a doc comment on line 76 rather than the assignment on line 99, and the suite stayed green — the same machinery error, in the same session, in the reviewer's own hand. A sweep with that bug reports 70 caught and approves; a reviewer with it rejects a working guard. Both directions live in the missing check.

**Findings open at approval (3, none blocking).** **R2-D5** (medium) — `NotificationFactsConsumer.ToEnvelope` copies seven envelope fields by hand, and corrupting the `correlationId` copy (so every email's subject carries the wrong one, the field `specs/shared` §7.1 puts there deliberately) leaves **unit 58/58 and integration 12/12 green** against real Kafka and real MS-SQL. Not blocking because the property was verified correct against real Mailpit bytes in round 1, and because round 1 prescribed the guard shape that was built; the fix is one assertion inside the existing theory and it wants a backlog entry. **R2-D6** (low) — the 14 `DateTimeOffset` interpolation sites across the seven templates are unasserted; the implementer's sweep was scoped to string-typed fields and said so, and round 2's wider sweep measured the residual rather than leaving it to inference. **R2-D7** (low, leader) — `progress/current.md`'s `**Status:**` line still says `in_progress`, one transition behind.

**#7's own N11 and D4, closed properly.** Round 1 found `progress/current.md` describing Phase 10 while `init.sh` reported it *"in lockstep with the backlog"* — the same finding #7 recorded as N11. The leader rewrote the body **and** changed `init.sh`'s success message to state what it actually reads: *"this check reads that line ONLY — a stale Goal/Decisions/Notes body below it is invisible here"*. That second half is the repair that generalises, and it is the pattern this repository keeps paying for: a guard that overstates its coverage is worse than one that admits its narrowness, because the overstatement is what stops anyone looking.

### Phase 11 closing assessment — one feature, one rejection, and the first clean measurement of what the raised bar is worth

**1. The phase in one line.** One feature, `notifications_service`, ≈4 h 39 min against #7's ≈3 h 01 min (**≈1.5×**), same round count, and the two blocking defects that caused the extra round were both **invisible to #7's process by construction**. The service consumes fourteen fact types across three topics, emails seven of them exactly once each, and is idempotent by `eventId` across a process restart — verified live in both rounds, by two different agents, against real containers and a real SMTP sink.

**2. What the ≈1.5× is made of, itemised rather than narrated.** Roughly 40 min of it is the fix round, which bought the two guards above. Another ≈25 min is round-2 review time spent on machinery verification that #7 never did — writing a sweep, proving it with three sentinels, and running 89 mutations instead of accepting 70. The remainder sits in implementation round 1, which delivered strictly more than #7's did: a `Func<ISmtpTransport>` seam so the real MailKit adapter is unit-testable without a socket, a layering correction moving the templates out of `Infrastructure/`, and a genuine harness repair — `IdempotentConsumerParityTests` — that #7 shipped **broken** and closed only in a follow-up briefing. **None of the delta is a language effect**, and the honest summary is that #8 bought more than it paid for here, but did pay.

**3. The guard that had only ever run against a set of one — and whether others share it.** `tests/Orders.UnitTests/IdempotentConsumerParityTests.cs` discovers its subjects from the filesystem: services carrying **both** a `ProcessedEventConfiguration.cs` **and** an `IdempotentConsumer.cs`. Until this feature that set was `{Orders}` — the canonical compared to itself — so the test could not fail, and its own non-vacuity line reads `Assert.NotEmpty(copies); // Orders itself always qualifies`, which is the check being satisfied by the one member that can never falsify it. Notifications is the first genuine second member, and the guard failed immediately, on a real difference C# has and TypeScript does not (a namespace-qualified `using` versus a relative import path). **The class is: a guard whose subject set is *discovered* rather than *declared*, and whose emptiness check is satisfied by a member that cannot fail.**

Asked which other guards share it, the answer from one enumeration of `tests/` for filesystem discovery — **not** a sweep, and it should be treated as a starting list rather than an audit — is **encouragingly few, and the repository already owns the right pattern**. `CentsRuleFixtureGuardTests` has an explicit non-vacuity test that writes a scratch fixture into the scanned directory and asserts the scan finds it at the right line with the right value. `BillingConsumesNoFactsTests` asserts `scannedFiles > 0` with the comment *"it cannot be trusted to have looked"*, and declares its expected hosted-service set explicitly rather than discovering it. Those two are the shape the parity guard should have had. **The parity guard still does not have it:** its membership check is still `NotEmpty`, which a regression to one member would still satisfy — and phases 12 and 13 add the Projector and the Gateway, which are its third and fourth candidate members. **The cheap fix, at the moment the third copy lands, is to assert the discovered set against a declared expected membership** (`{Orders, Notifications, Projector}`), so that a service silently dropping out of the comparison — by not creating the file that gates discovery, which is exactly the opt-out #7's own review closed — fails the test instead of shrinking it.

**4. What Phase 11 delivered.** The Notifications service end to end: seven templated emails, a durable dedup ledger inherited from phase 6 rather than built under rejection, a console adapter reachable through the same port as the real one, `AutoOffsetReset.Latest` decided at design time with the reasoning written down and now asserted, and a repaired parity guard that will hold for the five services still to be ported. Solution total **1054 tests green across 14 projects**.

### Notes for #9

- **A sweep is a guard, and a guard is not done until it has been seen to fail.** Prove the machinery with three sentinels before trusting a single verdict: a mutation that cannot apply must report *not applied*, a comment-only edit must report *survived*, and a known-bad mutation must report *caught*. Two of the three sentinels cost one line each, and without them a sweep reporting 70/70 and a sweep that silently mutated nothing are the same artefact.
- **Mutate by line number, not by first textual occurrence.** A doc comment naming the constant you are flipping sits above the assignment in every well-commented file in this build, and `replace(old, new, 1)` finds the comment.
- **Collapse two lists that must agree into one table whose `Keys` is the filter.** The routing defect was not a missing test, it was a second, independently-written enumeration of the same seven strings; the fix removes the disagreement rather than testing for it.
- **Scope a claimed absence to the population you enumerated, and say which population that was.** *"Zero survivors"* was true of string-typed fields and false of the fourteen date fields next to them. The implementer stated the scope honestly, and the wider sweep found the residual — which is exactly how it should go.
- **When a guard's subject set is discovered rather than declared, ask how many members it has today.** A parity check against a set of one cannot distinguish agreement from having nothing to compare, and `Assert.NotEmpty` will not tell you.

## completion_pair_has_no_causal_edge (id 57, phase 11 backlog, closed at the top of phase 12) — 2026-09-07 — **the Billing slice of #7's eight-phases-late rework, done before the projector exists**

**Effort:** **1 implementation session + 1 review pass, APPROVED first time, 0 blocking defects, 1 advisory** — no spec phase, no human gate (`sdd: false`; the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 57's four acceptance bullets, filed by feature 22's round-2 review as finding N1). Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-07): **implementation ≈04:30 → 04:56, ≈26 min** — `progress/current.md` 04:30:18 opens the pass, `src/Billing/Domain/Invoice.cs` 04:33:20, `tests/Billing.UnitTests/PaymentRegisterServiceTests.cs` 04:33:57, `tests/Billing.IntegrationTests/PaymentRegisterTests.cs` 04:36:50, the `in_review` transition 04:54:58, the impl report 04:56:19 — including four armed mutations (two families × two guards) and the `quality.sh` run the implementer reported at 1055/1055. **Review ≈04:59 → ≈05:08, ≈9 min** — two mutations of my own (the request-id revert at the service, and a fresh-id corruption at the domain return that the corroborating `NotEqual` assertions survive and the provenance `Equal` does not), both guards armed at both mutations with the failing line read off the stack trace (unit :166, integration :105 — the `causationId` equalities, not the `Seq` assertion at :94), forced `--no-incremental` rebuilds and `cmp`/md5 restores, `Billing.UnitTests` 226/226, `PaymentRegisterTests` 9/9 on real containers, `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, solution-wide `dotnet format --verify-no-changes`, a real `diff -rq specs/shared` against the #7 checkout, `./init.sh` exit 0, and a recursive re-enumeration of every transactional unit across all four fact-emitting services. **Total ≈38 min from `current.md` to verdict.** The commit bracket is not meaningful here — the previous commit `e3f958f` landed at 18:39 the evening before, and the gap is overnight, not work. *Note: `src/Billing/Application/PaymentRegisterService.cs` (04:39:58) carries an arming-restore mtime from the implementer's own probes, and both production files now carry review-time mtimes (≈05:03) from mine; all are byte-identical to the submitted versions (`cmp` against backups, md5 `19ce38a7…` / `a0ecbc10…`) and those timestamps are not authoring activity.*

**Against #7 — not a ratio, because #7's counterpart is not a feature.** #7 closed this defect as rework in `bf59af9` (2026-08-29 12:28, *"fix(projector): order tied timeline entries by causal edge, not by luck"*), eight phases after Billing shipped it, and by then the projector existed and had persisted timelines. The Billing slice of that commit is the same four files and roughly the same size as this one (`payment-register.handler.ts`, `invoice.ts`, two specs; 53 insertions, 7 deletions — against 4 files, ≈100 changed lines here). **Everything else in that commit is the cost of lateness:** 32 files and 1 446 insertions in total, of which the projector took ≈20 files — a `causationId` column on the timeline document, a causal-depth tiebreak in a MongoDB pipeline, a version-stamped read-model migration for legacy entries, a real `$arrayElemAt`-on-empty-`$filter` semantics bug found and fixed along the way, `openapi.yaml` and the generated contract types, seed data, a rewritten `PR10`/`PR15` and six new `PR30`–`PR35` requirements, and **five open points taken to a human gate.** #7's `history.md` records the surrounding feature-31 rework at **≈2 h 55 min of evidenced work on 2026-08-29** across eight sessions, of which the causal-edge amendment and its gate were one pass. **#8 paid ≈38 min, four Billing files, no migration, no gate**, because the fix landed before there was a read model to migrate — the attachment map's own argument, and the first time in this build that it has been measurable rather than asserted. The honest caveat: #8 did not *discover* the defect cheaper; it inherited the discovery from #7's spec amendment A1 and from its own feature-22 review, which is the reuse claim doing its job.

**Advisory carried forward (not blocking, recorded for phase 12):** the implementer's sweep for a second same-transaction fact pair used a non-recursive glob (`src/*/Application/*.cs`) that could not see Orders' `PlaceOrderCommandHandler` or `SagaFactHandler` (whose transaction is opened from `Infrastructure/Messaging/IdempotentConsumer.cs`). The review's recursive enumeration classifies both — one fact each, because `Order.ApproveCredit` is silent and only `Confirm` raises in the `credit.approved.v1` step — so the conclusion stands: `PaymentRegisterService` was the only same-transaction sibling pair in the solution. The enumeration of record is in `progress/review_completion_pair_has_no_causal_edge.md` §Probe 4. The `occurredAt` question in bullet two was decided by keeping the timestamps identical and supplying the edge, matching #7's shipped state and the shape its projector's causal-depth tiebreak expects.

**Review:** `progress/review_completion_pair_has_no_causal_edge.md` — APPROVED. Solution total as reported by the implementer's `quality.sh` run: 1055 tests green across 14 projects (one new test method); the review re-ran 226 + 9 + 16 of them.

## projector_read_model (id 24, phase 12) — 2026-09-08 — **four review rounds, all four on records rather than code; the ported-idiom ledger's most expensive and most productive row in this build**

**Effort:** **1 spec session + 1 human gate + 1 implementation session + 4 review passes + 3 fix rounds — REJECTED three times, then approved.** The first feature in this project rejected three times, and — like #7's own rejection of this same feature — **every rejection was on records, none on production behaviour**. The executable code changed exactly once across all four rounds: one exception message (round 1's advisory A2). - **Human approval gate — 2026-09-07**, one open point (the `apps/seed`-equivalent index question) decided at the gate.
- **Implementation ≈05:46 → ≈07:52, ≈2 h 06 min** — `src/Projector/Projector.csproj` 05:46:13 opens it, 86 source and test files, `DeltaToPipeline.cs` 07:51:45 closes it, with `specs/shared/test-matrix.md` updated at 07:13:02 mid-pass. Delivered 87 unit + 52 integration tests, 12 armed mutations across both families, a seeded oracle parity run and a live boot against the running compose stack.
- **Review pass 1 ≈07:52 → ≈08:16, ≈24 min** — eleven hostile mutations (all bit), a planted architecture violation, ten direct read-model queries. Verdict **REJECTED, 3 blocking**: **D1** the ledger's own flagged `L12` row answered backwards, **D2** two delivered tests that could not fail (`Assert.True(true, "…")`), **D3** `progress/current.md` stating the opposite of what was done. `progress/current.md` 08:16:09 records the rejection.
- **Fix round 1 ≈08:16 → ≈08:29, ≈13 min** — `KafkaFactStreamSubscriberConfigTests.cs` 08:22:15 and `UpdateSignalTests.cs` 08:28:23 close D2; D1 corrected in prose; D3 corrected.
- **Review pass 2 ≈08:29 → ≈09:11, ≈42 min** — five mutations, probe restores at 09:11:13. Verdict **REJECTED, 1 blocking**: **D4** — D1's corrected *operational answer* was right and its *mechanism* was wrong. It said the server normalises the `$type` alias to the numeric code **when storing**; it does not — it treats them as equivalent **when comparing**, and stores whichever rendering created the index. The wrong mechanism had reached a **test name**.
- **Fix round 2 ≈09:11 → ≈09:21, ≈10 min** — `design.md` 09:21:11; the `L12` case rewritten to run **both** creation orders and assert the stored rendering in each; the test renamed.
- **Review pass 3 ≈09:21 → ≈09:59, ≈38 min** — four fresh probes directly against the live `otcnet-mongodb` (`mongo:8.3.8`) including two controls nobody had run, and three mutations of the delivered test through the real driver (`P3a` value-level, `P3b` direction-independence, `P3c` server-side conflict), plus a full clean `dotnet test` at **1194/1194, 0 skipped**. SHA-256 of the mutated file recorded before the first probe and after the last restore, equal. Verdict **REJECTED, 1 blocking**: **D5** — the fix reached the five places round 2's **prose list** named, and the original, disproved hazard was still stated as live fact in **three more**, one of them the class summary of the production file the row exists to justify (`src/Projector/Infrastructure/Persistence/ReadModelIndexes.cs`).
- **Fix round 3 ≈09:59 → ≈10:20, ≈21 min** — five documentation edits, no production behaviour, no test logic, no assertion; a full `./quality.sh` (its sixteen coverage reports are timestamped 10:11:58 → 10:18:51); `feature_list.json` → `in_review` at 10:19:43.

- **Review pass 4 ≈10:33 → ≈10:50, ≈17 min** — no containers spent on re-arming, by design. Two enumeration patterns × two forms × eight runs each, plus the control neither prior record ran; full classification of **21 + 35** hits with zero unclassified lines; the corrected-mechanism prose checked against the reviewer's own round-3 measurements; solution `--no-incremental` build 0/0, `dotnet format` exit 0, `Projector.UnitTests` **87/87**, `ReadModelIndexesTests` **4/4** against the real container, `./init.sh` exit 0. Verdict **APPROVED**, one new non-blocking advisory (**A12**).

**Total ≈5 h 04 min from the first source file (05:46:13) to the final verdict (≈10:50)**, of which **≈2 h 01 min was review across four passes** and **≈44 min was fixing what review found** — i.e. **review and rework together cost ≈1.3× the original implementation**, the highest such ratio in this build, exceeding `gateway_rest_auth`'s in #7. The spec session is excluded because it is unmeasured.

**Against #7 — ≈5 h 04 min against ≈3 h 41 min, ≈1.4×, with two large offsetting items that must be read together.**

*What #8 paid more for.* Three extra rounds, all of them on one ledger row. #7 had **no ported-idiom ledger** — the convention was adopted at #8's own Phase-8 gate — so #7 never asked what its MongoDB server did with two renderings of `$type`; it simply edited its seed to match its projector and moved on. #8 asked, got the answer wrong, got the correction wrong, and then failed twice to propagate the correction completely. **Every one of the four findings was a real falsehood about a real engine, in an artefact a later port would have inherited**, and none was visible to traceability (the requirement was met every time), to arming (the behaviour was correct on the tested path every time) or to the suite (green every time — 1195, 1194, 1194, 1194).

*What #8 did not pay at all.* **#7 re-specified this feature's central invariant eight phases later.** Its amendment **A1** replaced the timeline's `occurredAt` ordering with a recorded-causal-edge ordering (`causationId` → `eventId`, bounded `$reduce` depth, a version-stamped read-model migration, and a Billing change making `credit.released.v1` follow `payment.received.v1` rather than being its sibling) — landed in `bf59af9`, **32 files, 1 446 insertions**, taken to a human gate with five open points, and rejected twice before it stuck. **#8 folded A1 into its first draft**: `TimelineOrder.cs`'s `__depth`, `timelineOrderVersion`, `TimelineOrderMigrationTests` and `TimelineCausalOrderTests` (`PR31`/`PR32`/`PR35`) are in the delivered feature, because backlog id **57** closed the underlying Billing causal edge the day before at a cost of ≈38 min. **The honest accounting is that #8's ≈1.4× on this feature buys the avoidance of #7's ≈2 h 55 min of eight-phases-late rework plus its gate** — but that dividend is *work that did not happen*, so it appears in no session count and in no ratio, and it is the clearest instance in this build of #7's experience transferring as **avoided rework** rather than as faster typing.

**The confound column, per defect — would #7's standard have caught it?**

| Defect | Answer |
|---|---|
| **D1** (`L12` answered backwards) | **No.** No ledger in #7; the mechanism is #8's own Phase-8 convention |
| **D2** (two tests that cannot fail) | **Partly.** #7's review of this feature audited no test for falsifiability; the `Assert.True(true)` shape is the same class as #8's feature-19 finding, which post-dates #7 |
| **D3** (`current.md` false) | **Yes — and it is the sharpest line in the record.** It is the same box #7 was rejected on for this same feature, inherited into this task list as a prevention task (`N7`), and it happened anyway. **An inherited prevention task that did not prevent** — evidence that inheriting a lesson as a *task* is weaker than inheriting it as a *check* |
| **D4** (the corrected mechanism also wrong) | **No.** Same reasoning as D1; it produced a new binding `CLAUDE.md` convention — *an engine claim probed in one direction is a claim about that direction only* |
| **D5** (the retired claim alive in three more places, one in production source) | **No.** A defect *in a fix*, in a repository whose fix-completeness claims were prose lists. #7 never made the claim, so it never had to unmake it |
| **A12** (the fix's own enumeration silently dropped five hits) | **No**, and it is the one nobody was looking for: the enumerating command was malformed in a way that hides preferentially from the reviewer |

**The finding that generalises furthest, and the reason this row was worth four rounds.** The round-3 fix closed with a command, as required — and *the command had a hole*. `grep -rn <pat> --include='*.cs' . | grep -v '/bin/\|/obj/'` reads as a path exclusion and is not one: `grep -rn` emits `path:lineno:content`, so the filter matches **content**, and silently drops any hit whose matched line mentions a build directory. Measured at close, on this tree, eight runs each: the post-filtered form returns **16**, the path-excluding form returns **21**, and the *same* recursive `grep` with the post-filter simply removed also returns **21** — so the discrepancy is entirely the filter's, and the tool is exonerated. (Fix round 3 had diagnosed this as a nondeterministic `ugrep`; it is not, and both forms are stable.) **All five suppressed lines were quotations of the enumeration command itself**, because the command contains `/bin/`. So the filter preferentially deletes *the records of the sweep* from *the artefact whose purpose is to prove the sweep was complete*, and a reviewer re-running the pasted command reproduces the same 16 and confirms nothing. That is the guard-that-does-not-guard's fifth disguise in this repository, and the first that hides specifically from the checker. Both resulting rules are now in `CLAUDE.md` under the enumeration convention.

**The closing judgement, revised at round 4 from what round 3 had written.** Round 3 called this *"the ledger working, expensively, with the cost sitting entirely in detection, which failed the same way three times"*. Half of that survives. It is **not** the same failure a fourth time: round 3 prescribed closing with a command, the fix round obeyed, and its **conclusion was correct** — four independent re-runs confirm it. What round 4 found is a different failure, one level down, in the command. And round 4 itself was **cheap — ≈17 min against ≈40 for each of the first three** — because round 3 closed with two things rather than one: a required fix *and* a standing evidentiary record (its own three `L12` probes) that explicitly discharged the need to re-arm. **A review that tells the next round what it need not re-prove is what turns a fourth round from expensive into cheap**, and that is a process win worth as much as the ledger lesson.

**Notes for #9, in the order they would have saved time here:**

- **Enumerate on the wording of the claim being *retired*, not the claim being *written*.** A correction is about a mechanism, so everyone greps the mechanism word — and the residue carries the *first* wrong version, which shares no term with the correction. One `grep` for a five-word phrase from the original text found all seven hits in under a second, against three prose sweeps that each missed them.
- **Exclude by path at the source; never post-filter `grep -rn`'s output.** `find … -not -path '*/bin/*' -print0 | xargs -0 grep -n <pat>`, or anchor to the path against a **file list** as `init.sh:191` does. The harness has never carried this defect; every ad-hoc sweep in this feature did.
- **Paste the output; do not retype it.** Fix round 3's classification was substantively right and its line numbers had drifted by ±2-3 for its own self-referential file, while asserting they had been captured last. A retyped output is a reading wearing the clothes of a search result, and grouping several hits into one sentence per file re-creates precisely the invisibility the rule forbids.
- **Where a ledger row's claim involves two parties — two writers, two orderings, two creation sequences — probe both ways, or state which way you probed.** Running it one way answered half the question twice here, and the wrong half reached a test name.
- **Add controls, not just directions.** The probe that made the corrected row *precise* rather than merely *not-wrong* was the one nobody had asked for: two `$type` aliases naming **different** types (`"string"` vs `"int"`) **do** conflict (code 86), which is what proves the server compares the filter semantically rather than ignoring it once the index name matches.
- **A rejection should say what the next round must not re-prove.** Naming the standing evidence, and forbidding its repetition, is the difference between a cheap final round and an expensive one.

**Findings open at approval (4, none blocking):** **A7** — the coverage gate is inert (per-report `line-rate`, no aggregate threshold), feature 34's, correctly disclosed and the reason `CHECKPOINTS.md` C4's coverage box stays unticked. **A8** — the round-1 A2 message fix has no guard: `PR22_RefusesToStartAgainstANonPartialIndexOfTheSameName_NamingTheIndex` asserts only the index name and `dropIndex`, so a regression to a hard-coded *"code 85"* stays green; cheap to close next time `ReadModelIndexes.cs` is opened. **A9** — `progress/current.md:4` still reads `in_progress — … fix round in flight` and the file carries two `## Notes` headings; leader-owned bookkeeping, to be reset at session close. **A12** — fix round 3's pasted enumeration output was transcribed rather than pasted and its self-referential line numbers do not resolve; the conclusion was independently re-derived and holds.

**Phase 12 is not closed by this feature.** Ids **58** (`notification_envelope_copy_is_unguarded`) and **59** (`date_typed_payload_sites_survive_mutation`) are both filed against phase 12 and both `pending`, so **no phase-12 closing assessment is due here**.

**Review:** `progress/review_projector_read_model.md` — four rounds, APPROVED at round 4. Solution total at the last full run (review round 3, verified against the implementer's independent `./quality.sh`): **1194 tests green across 16 projects, 0 failed, 0 skipped**; round 4 re-ran 87 + 4 of them plus a solution-wide `--no-incremental` build and format check.
