## notifications_mutation_gaps — ids 58 (`notification_envelope_copy_is_unguarded`) + 59 (`date_typed_payload_sites_survive_mutation`), phase 12 — 2026-09-08 — **closes Phase 12; one loop, two backlog entries, two review rounds, and not one production line changed in either**

**Effort:** **1 implementation session + 2 review passes + 1 fix round + 1 leader turn — REJECTED once, then approved.** No spec phase, no human gate (both `sdd: false`; the specification of record is `feature_list.json`'s six acceptance bullets across the two entries). Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-08), read off a single `find . -newermt '2026-09-08 11:00' -not -path './.git/*' -not -path '*/bin/*' -not -path '*/obj/*' -printf '%T+ %p\n' | sort` run by the reviewer at close — a proxy for elapsed work, not a stopwatch:

- **Implementation ≈13:47 → ≈14:13, ≈26 min** — bracketed at the start by `docs/PROCESS.md` 13:47:27, the last artefact of the projector wrap-up. Visible writing window `OrderPlacedTemplateTests.cs` 14:12:02 → `OrderCompletedTemplateTests.cs` 14:13:11, five template test files. Delivered the seven date-fixture bracketings, the two envelope-fixture collision fixes, and 20 hand-armed guards. **This figure is a lower bound**: reading and planning before the first file write leave no trace, and the boundary with review round 1 is only recoverable because round 1's own two probe restores are identifiable by name (`NotificationFactsConsumerTests.cs` 14:36:09 and `OrderConfirmedTemplateTests.cs` 14:38:17 are the reviewer's collision-restoration probes, not implementation).
- **Review pass 1 ≈14:13 → 14:47:47, ≈35 min** — 14 mutation runs, 26 mutations across five families, all re-armed rather than accepted; a 95-site enumeration that widened the predecessor's 89 by the three nested collection-element sites nobody had ever counted; the money partition measured for the first time (5/5). Verdict **REJECTED, 2 blocking**: **D1** a live survivor at `src/Notifications/Application/Templates/OrderCancelledTemplate.cs:16`, and **D2** two of id 59's three acceptance bullets dropped in the implementation. Three non-blocking (D3–D5).
- **Leader turn ≈14:48 → ≈14:51, ≈3 min** — `progress/current.md` 14:51:03. Ruled the committed sweep script **required** rather than re-litigable, on the ground that the population had been counted three times by three parties and yielded 14, 89 and 95, and that D1 lived in a nested element no hand enumeration had ever included.
- **Fix round ≈14:51 → 15:28:57, ≈38 min** — `scripts/notification-template-payload-sweep.sh` 15:24:14, `progress/impl_notifications_mutation_gaps.md` 15:28:49, the `in_review` transition 15:28:57. D1 closed at the fixture, the sweep built and its three sentinels run, D5 closed, and a `pipefail`/`set -e` bug found and fixed **in the tool** before any of its verdicts were trusted.
- **Review pass 2 ≈15:33 → ≈16:02, ≈29 min** — the enumeration reproduced independently and `diff`ed against the record's pasted output (identical but for a markdown fence); six probes through the delivered tool plus two by hand; a fixture-value containment enumeration across all seven test fixtures; a full `./quality.sh` (**16 projects, 1201 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped**), `Architecture.Tests` 16/16, `Notifications.UnitTests` 65/65 four times, a real `diff -rq specs/shared` against the #7 checkout. Verdict **APPROVED**, five non-blocking findings (**A13**–**A17**).

**Total ≈2 h 18 min from the wrap-up commit to the final verdict**, of which **≈1 h 04 min was review across two passes** and **≈38 min was fixing what review found** — i.e. **review and rework together cost ≈3.9× the original implementation, the highest ratio in this build by a wide margin** (feature 24's was ≈1.3×, itself the previous record). That ratio is real but it is not a scandal: the denominator is a 26-minute lower bound on a feature whose entire content is guards, and the numerator includes building an instrument the implementation had declined to build.

*Note: every file under `src/Notifications/` carries review-time mtimes from arming probes in both rounds; all are byte-identical to their pre-review state — the eight files in `Application/Templates/` verified by `sha256sum -c` at close, and `Presentation/NotificationFactsConsumer.cs` at `2e63770b555ac391661f08fc874eec22295d1f6fe54c4535416236d50ba9b291`, the same value round 1 recorded before its first probe. Those timestamps are not authoring activity. `git status --porcelain` is identical before and after both review passes.*

### Was one loop the right call? Yes, and the evidence is specific rather than a preference

Three things make it a genuine save rather than a convenient bundling. **The two entries share their fixtures, not merely their directory:** id 58's guard lives in `NotificationFactsConsumerTests.cs` and id 59's in the seven `*TemplateTests.cs`, but the *reason* both were unguarded is one fixture defect — values that collide, so a correctly-written assertion cannot see a wrong field. The bracketing that made id 59's date assertions load-bearing and the `aggregateId`/`correlationId` de-collision that made id 58's envelope assertions load-bearing are the same edit made twice. **The review overhead was paid once:** two passes covered six acceptance bullets, and the second pass re-armed id 58's flagship guard as a by-product of already having the project built. **And the fix round's blast radius was one file** — split, the D1 fixture fix and the D5 bracketing would have landed in two features against the same file, in some order, with the second one rebasing on the first.

The counter-evidence, stated honestly: the loop's **rejection** was entirely id 59's (both blocking defects), and id 58 was `pending` throughout, so id 58 paid a round of latency it did not earn. That is the cost of the rider mechanism and it is small. Recommend continuing to use it where the entries share a *cause*, not merely a directory — that is the discriminator this loop supplies.

### Every defect in both rounds was in test or tooling code, and no production line changed. Correctly filed, or over-engineered?

**Correctly filed — but the honest reading is narrower than the round count suggests, and #9 should have the narrow version.**

The case that they were correctly filed is not that the code was wrong; it is that **three interpolation sites had never been in anybody's population**, and one of them was unguarded. `OrderCancelledTemplate.cs:16` rendered `step.Step`, and substituting `step.EventType` left 65/65 green, because the fixture's `"credit.release"` is a **prefix** of its `"credit.released.v1"` and the assertion was `Assert.Contains`. That is a new shape of the collision class — not two fields holding the same value, but one value containing another — and the feature that existed to remove collisions had looked only for the equality shape. It was found by a reviewer widening the population by hand, twenty-four hours after two other parties had each declared the population closed. A backlog entry that surfaces a defect class nobody had named is correctly filed by definition.

The case that it was over-engineered for what it protected is also real and must be recorded beside it. **Nothing was shipping broken.** All 95 sites render the right field today; the loop bought *future* detection, not a bug fix. Six guard sites and one script cost ≈2 h 18 min, and the biggest single line item — the sweep tool — was ruled in by the leader over an implementer argument that was itself sound (all-red implies all-applied; 95 hand-armed sites beat an unvalidated instrument). That ruling was vindicated, but not by the tool finding anything: **the tool found nothing the hand enumeration had not already found.** What it bought is that the next person to open these seven files does not re-derive the population by hand for a fourth time, and that #9's equivalent service inherits an instrument rather than a number.

**So the entries were guard gaps, correctly filed, and the loop's cost was dominated by one deliberate investment in a re-runnable instrument rather than by the gaps themselves.** If #9 wants the cheap version: fix the fixtures, arm the six sites by hand, skip the tool, and accept that the population will be re-counted next time. If it wants the version that compounds, build the tool — but **build it at the moment the population is first counted, not after it has been counted three times and disagreed with itself.**

### Notes for #9, in the order they would have saved time here

- **`Assert.Contains` cannot distinguish two fields when one value contains the other.** The equality collision is well known here; the containment collision is its quiet half, and it survived a feature whose entire purpose was removing collisions. The check is one command and it is population-wide rather than site-by-site: enumerate every constructor-argument string literal per fixture file (excluding assertion lines, or the fixture's own expected strings drown the signal) and test pairwise containment. Seven files, zero hits, under a second. Run it once per service, not once per defect.
- **A must-survive control that is outside the instrument's population is not a control for the instrument.** The round demonstrated survival on `CompensationStep.OccurredAt`, a field the sweep can never reach, and disclosed that accurately — so the sweep shipped with a clean sheet and no case it had ever been seen to fail. The falsifying case had to be supplied at review, by re-creating the defect the sweep exists to catch and probing it in-population (`--probe 93` → `SURVIVED`, then `CAUGHT` on the fix). **Pick a real site and break its guard.** This is the arming protocol's own lesson one level up, and it now has three instances in this build: a guard that cannot fail (feature 19), a ledger row whose guard could not fail (feature 19 again), and an instrument with no falsifying case (here).
- **When a population has been counted three times and produced three numbers, the argument for an instrument is settled — but the instrument is worth building at count one.** 14, then 89, then 95. Each count was made in good faith by someone who believed the set was closed, and each was scoped by the *question that party was asking* rather than by the code. The tool's value is not that it counts better; it is that it counts **the same way twice**.
- **Probe an instrument through its interface, not by editing it.** The round demonstrated its `NOT-APPLIED` path by corrupting the tool's own lookup table, which proves the branch executes but requires trusting that the table was restored. The same branch is reachable from outside by making the source violate the tool's precondition — here, duplicating a token on the enumerated line, which returned `NOT-APPLIED (… found 2)` **and** re-derived the population from 95 to 96 in the same run, settling "is the enumeration cached?" for free.
- **A restore check placed after the mutation run is also a baseline check.** `probe_site` requires the *restored* suite to be green before it will emit `CAUGHT`. That single ordering decision means a suite that is red for any unrelated reason cannot produce a sweep of false `CAUGHT`s — it aborts instead. Cheap, and worth copying into #9's equivalent harness.

## Phase 12 closing assessment — three deliverables, five review rounds, and a gap against #7 that is 100% process and 0% stack

Feature 24's entry above carries this phase's two biggest items — the four-round `L12` ledger story and the A1 dividend — and they are not restated here. What follows is the phase-level arithmetic they sit inside.

### 1. The clock

| Deliverable | #8 evidenced wall-clock | #7's counterpart |
|---|---|---|
| id 57 `completion_pair_has_no_causal_edge` (phase-11 backlog, closed at the top of phase 12) | **≈38 min** | none as a feature — done eight phases late inside `bf59af9`, part of ≈2 h 55 min of rework plus a human gate |
| id 24 `projector_read_model` | **≈5 h 04 min** (spec session unmeasured) | **≈3 h 41 min**, spec included |
| ids 58 + 59 `notifications_mutation_gaps` | **≈2 h 18 min** | **none, at all** |
| **Phase total** | **≈8 h 00 min** | **≈3 h 41 min** |

**As filed, ≈2.2×. Like-for-like on the one feature both assessments actually built, ≈1.4×** — and #8's figure excludes an unmeasured spec session while #7's includes its ≈8-minute one, so the true like-for-like is slightly worse than 1.4× for #8.

### 2. The confound column — where the ≈4 h 19 min gap actually went

| Component of the gap | Size | Share | #8-only process, or the stack? |
|---|---|---|---|
| ids 58 + 59 in full | ≈2 h 18 min | 53% | **#8-only.** #7's review of its notifications service never enumerated payload interpolation sites, never armed a template mutation, and filed no equivalent backlog entry. Nothing here is a C# cost |
| id 24's excess over #7's | ≈1 h 23 min | 32% | **#8-only.** Three of its four review rounds were on the ported-idiom ledger, a convention adopted at #8's own Phase-8 gate. #7 had no ledger and asked none of the questions |
| id 57 | ≈38 min | 15% | **#8-only, and a dividend booked as a cost.** #7 paid the same work later and dearer; #8 paid it before there was a read model to migrate |

**Every minute of the gap is attributable to #8's own process, and none of it to .NET.** That is the cleanest reading the benchmark has produced so far, and it should be stated with its caveat rather than as a win: "attributable to process" is not "wasted". The 38 minutes avoided ≈2 h 55 min and a gate; the ledger's four rounds produced two binding `CLAUDE.md` conventions and corrected two false claims about a real engine that a later port would have inherited; the notifications loop removed a defect class and left a committed instrument. The measurable cost is here in the numerator and the measurable benefit is mostly **work that did not happen**, so the ratio will always read worse than the truth. Phase 11's entry made this point on one feature; phase 12 makes it on three, and it is now the dominant fact about this benchmark rather than a caveat to it.

### 3. The pattern that defines this phase

**Across all five review rounds in phase 12, the executable production code changed exactly once** — one exception message, feature 24's round-1 advisory A2. Feature 24 was rejected three times and ids 58/59 once, and **every single defect was a false, unguarded or unfalsifiable *claim*: a ledger row answered backwards, two tests that could not fail, a record stating the opposite of what was done, a corrected mechanism that was also wrong, a fix's own enumeration with a hole in it, a live survivor behind a prefix collision, and an instrument with no falsifying case.** The behaviour was right on first submission every time.

That is the phase's finding, and it cuts both ways. It is evidence the implementation tier is working: three services' worth of production code arrived correct. It is also evidence that **the review bar has migrated almost entirely off code and onto the artefacts that make claims about code** — and those artefacts are exactly what #9 inherits. A wrong ledger row or a decorative guard costs #8 a review round; it costs #9 a false premise it has no reason to re-derive. On that reading the five rounds are cheap.

### 4. What Phase 12 delivered

The `Projector` service (62-file-equivalent, MongoDB `order_timeline` read model, causal-edge ordering folded in from #7's A1 amendment at first draft rather than eight phases late), the Billing causal edge that read model depends on, six newly-armed guard sites in Notifications, and `scripts/notification-template-payload-sweep.sh` — the repository's first committed **mutation instrument**, as opposed to a one-off probe. Solution at close: **16 test projects, 1201 tests, 0 failed, 0 skipped**, my own `./quality.sh` run.

### 5. What #9 inherits from Phase 12

- **The A1 causal-edge design, pre-folded.** #9 should specify the projector's ordering as `causationId` → `eventId` with a bounded reduce depth **from the first draft**, and should close its Billing equivalent of id 57 before the projector exists. #8's ≈38 min against #7's ≈2 h 55 min is the measured value of doing so.
- **The ported-idiom ledger, and the two rules phase 12 added to it** — probe an engine claim in both directions where the claim involves two parties, and a row's named guard is a countable claim that is not done until it has been seen to fail.
- **The containment-collision check** and the must-survive-in-population rule, both above.
- **The honest expectation for the ratio.** #9 should expect its phase-12 equivalent to land near #8's ≈8 h rather than #7's ≈3 h 41 min, and should not read the difference as FastAPI being slow. The bar rose; the stack did not change.

**Review:** `progress/review_notifications_mutation_gaps.md` — two rounds, REJECTED at round 1 (D1, D2 blocking), APPROVED at round 2 with five non-blocking findings (**A13** the must-survive sentinel outside the population, **A14** a residual latent fixture collision, **A15** `--all` never run end to end and does not fail fast, **A16** `enumerate()` depends on `grep`'s filename prefix, **A17** `progress/current.md` disagrees with the backlog for the fourth consecutive feature and now makes `./init.sh` FAIL). **A7** — the inert coverage gate — remains open and unfixed, as at the last four closes.

## orders_catalog_responder (id 40, phase 13) — 2026-09-08

**Effort:** **1 implementer pass + 1 fix round, 2 review rounds — REJECTED at round 1, APPROVED at round 2.** No spec session (`sdd: false`); the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 40's three acceptance bullets plus `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml`'s `catalogReferenceList` channel. Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local, 2026-09-08), bracketed below by the phase-12 close commit at **16:28** and the leader's phase-13 session file at **17:23:38**:

- **Implementation ≈17:30:32 → ≈17:45** — first production file (`IOrderReferenceCatalog.cs`) to the last pass-1 test artefact (17:35:30), plus an unmeasured verification tail (`quality.sh` and a 16-project solution run). **≈20–25 min of writing, ≈30 min with verification.** Built: the second subject on the existing `OrdersCreateResponder` (two `Task.WhenAll` subscribe loops, never a second `BackgroundService`), four list methods on the one existing `IOrderReferenceCatalog` port and its one `EfCoreOrderReferenceCatalog` adapter, `ListCatalogReferenceQuery`/handler, the wire DTOs and validator, +27 unit tests and +11 integration tests, four armed mutations, and a ported-idiom ledger row — the first written under phase 13's port-bound rule.
- **Review round 1 ≈17:45 → 18:10:40 — ≈25 min. REJECTED**, two blocking defects, four independent mutations run against live containers.
- **Fix round 18:11 → 18:39:35 — ≈28 min.** D1 + D2 + both advisories, two armed directions on the new guard, full re-verification.
- **Review round 2 ≈18:40 → ≈19:10 — ≈30 min. APPROVED**, six independent mutations, a full 84-test `Orders.IntegrationTests` run, and #7's cited source opened line by line to check the *corrected* ledger row.
- **Total ≈1 h 45 min across four passes.**

**#7's counterpart:** **1 implementer pass, approved first time — ≈21 min measured implementation plus an unmeasured live-E2E tail, ≈35 min review, ≈56 min.** **#8 is ≈1.9×, and lost on passes.** The two defects split the gap cleanly and in opposite directions:

- **D1 — a guard #7 had and #8 dropped in translation (≈30 min of the ≈58 min of extra rounds).** The `includeDisabled` wire flag was unguarded end to end: replacing `OrdersCreateResponder.cs:124` with a constant left 307 unit and 11 integration tests green, and the shared fixture seeded no disabled row, so the flag's effect had never been observed at all. #7 guarded exactly this seam in its first pass (`apps/orders/src/presentation/catalog-reference-list.controller.spec.ts:33`). **This is not review inflation — it is a real miss, and it belongs in the "not faster, and our fault" column.**
- **D2 — a ledger row whose "#7 relied on X" half was invented (≈28 min).** The row credited "NestJS's module-scoped provider singletons … with nothing to configure wrong"; #7's `app.module.ts:123-137` is an explicit `useExisting` alias between two distinct tokens, carrying its own warning comment about why it is not a second factory, and `catalog-reference-list.port.ts:1-21` keeps two separate ports on one adapter, deliberately rejecting the widened single port #8 chose. **#8-only process cost entirely** — #7 had no ledger — and it changed no behaviour, but it produced a binding `CLAUDE.md` convention: *a row's history half is a claim about #7's source, read out of #7's checkout with a file and line, never inferred from what the framework would plausibly have done.* A wrong guard fails when armed; **a wrong history half cannot fail at all**, and it is the half #9 inherits.

**The finding worth carrying to #9, because it links the two.** The ledger obligation caught prose, not behaviour — but the rule it produced forces the implementer to *open #7's source for this feature*, and #7's source is precisely where D1's missing guard was sitting. Writing the row honestly in pass 1 would have walked past the controller spec that asserts `includeDisabled: false`. **The ledger's real dividend is not the row; it is that writing the row honestly is the cheapest way to notice what the original guarded and the port did not.** On this feature the row was written from an assumption instead, and the repository paid for both halves of that in the same round.

**What was built:** `catalog.reference.list`, the NATS RPC feature 25's `GET /catalog/*` endpoints will call — products, retailers, companies and currencies read through the **same registered `IOrderReferenceCatalog` instance** `PlaceOrderCommandHandler` already resolves, never a second adapter or a parallel `OrdersDbContext` query. #8 diverges from #7 by widening one port instead of aliasing two tokens onto one adapter, and pays #7's predicted price for it (`FakeOrderReferenceCatalog` grew four methods place-order never calls). Solution at close, my own runs: `Orders.UnitTests` **307**, `Orders.IntegrationTests` **84** (was 82), `Architecture.Tests` **16**, all 0 failed / 0 skipped; the implementer's full-solution run reports **1241 passed** across 16 projects.

**Findings open at approval (3, none blocking):** **A4** — the corrected ledger row named two guards and only one had ever been seen to fail; the reviewer armed the second itself (adding a second constructor dependency to `ListCatalogReferenceQueryHandler` fails the exact-parameter-list assertion, which round 1's `Assert.Contains` version could not), so the claim is evidenced, but *naming a guard creates the obligation to arm it* was not honoured. **A5** — the "ordered by code" claim is guarded for `ListProductsAsync` only; deleting `.OrderBy` from the retailers, companies and currencies methods simultaneously left 13 catalog integration and 307 unit tests green. **A6** — the responder's `message.Data is null` branch remains untested (carried from round 1's A3, cosmetic). Two items for id 25's brief: `catalog.reference.list` is subscribed without a NATS queue group (safe today only because `NatsCollection` and `SagaCollection` disable parallelisation), and A5's ordering claim has the Gateway's list endpoints as its first consumer.

**Verdict:** APPROVED — see `progress/review_orders_catalog_responder.md` (two rounds).

## orders_cancel_responder (id 41, phase 13) — 2026-09-08

**Effort:** **1 implementer pass + 2 fix rounds, 2 review rounds — REJECTED at round 1 (D1, D2), APPROVED at round 2.** No spec session (`sdd: false`); the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 41's four acceptance bullets plus `specs/shared/saga.md` §4.3's generalisation table and `asyncapi.yaml`'s `orders.cancel` / `billing.credit.release` channels. Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local, 2026-09-08), bracketed below by id 40's approval at **19:07** and the leader's session-file rewrite at **19:09:46**:

- **Implementation ≈19:15 → ≈20:10 — ≈50–55 min.** First production file (`CancelOrderCommand.cs`) at **19:26:53**, last pass-1 test artefact at **19:39:03**, then seven armed mutations (rows A–G) with forced rebuilds and a full `./quality.sh` (**1276 passed**). Built: `orders.cancel` as the **third** subject on the existing `OrdersCreateResponder`, the four-branch `CancelOrderCommandHandler`, `SagaStepTable` widened from one step per fact type to `IReadOnlyList<SagaStep>` for the two now-multi-variant fact types (with `For` throwing on ambiguity and `ForStatus` added), a sixth `SagaCommandKind`/dispatch-owed event/`OrderSagas` branch, the reason-parametric `stock.release` factory, and **all four** §4.3 branches in one pass.
- **Review round 1 ≈20:10 → 20:43:27 — ≈30 min. REJECTED**, eight mutations (four deletion-family, four corruption-family) against real containers.
- **Fix round 2 20:43 → ≈21:20 — ≈35 min.** D1 + D2, one armed row, two full `./quality.sh` runs (**1281**).
- **Fix round 3 ≈21:20 → 22:00:00 — ≈40 min**, and it was **not asked for by the reviewer** — the leader rejected round 2's "transient resource-contention flake" verdict on `OrdersCancelAcceptanceTests.UnknownOrderId_RepliesNotFound` on the ground that a green run is not evidence about a red one, and was right: the cause was a genuine missing synchronisation in `SagaIntegrationTestSupport.StartHostAsync`, which returned as soon as `host.StartAsync()` did, before `OrdersCreateResponder`'s NATS subscriptions had landed server-side. Five of the six saga suites are protected only **by accident** (they warm up through an in-process `PlaceOrderAsync` first); this one test sent its RPC as the very next statement. Deterministic reproduction (`OrdersCancelResponderReadinessRaceTests`, 3 tests, a controlled 300 ms-delayed subscriber), the fix, two armed rows, two full `./quality.sh` runs (**1284**).
- **Review round 2 22:03 → ≈22:35 — ≈35 min. APPROVED**, six independent mutations, two of them against live containers.
- **Total ≈3 h 20 min across five passes.**

**#7's counterpart:** **2 implementer passes (≈2 h 5 min) + a leader `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml` amendment interlude + 1 review (≈40 min) — approved first time, ≈2 h 45 min.** **#8 is ≈1.2×, and lost on rounds while winning on passes.** The causes split three ways and only two of them are #8's:

- **D1 — a guard #7 had and #8 dropped in translation (≈35 min).** This feature turned `stock.release`'s `reason` from a constant into a two-way switch on the triggering fact's `eventType`, and nothing tested it: transposing the two arms left 337 unit tests and six real-container integration tests green, because the one integration test that captures the outgoing request asserted only `Assert.NotNull`. **#7 asserts the observed request's reason on both paths** — `apps/orders/src/saga-compensation-credit-rejected.integration.spec.ts:134` (`expect(stockReleaseRequest?.reason).toBe('credit_rejected')`) and `orders-cancel.integration.spec.ts:353` (matches on `request.reason === 'order_cancelled'`). **This is the second consecutive phase-13 feature whose rejection was a guard #7's own suite carried at the same seam and #8's port did not** (id 40's D1 was `includeDisabled`). That is a pattern, not a coincidence, and it belongs in the "not faster, and our fault" column.
- **D2 — a required wire field missing, and the skipped convention that let it ship (≈15 min).** Orders' `CreditReleaseReplyPayload` omitted the schema-required `availableCreditAfter`, and the pair of new payload records skipped the `BC23` guard the same file applies to the other ten. Harmless at runtime today, which is precisely why only a guard could have caught it. **Partly a dividend hazard**: #8 inherited the `billing.credit.release` schema already amended, so the record was retyped from a contract nobody in #8 had to design or argue for.
- **Fix round 3 — a real defect, and the most valuable pass in the feature (≈40 min + ≈15 min of review).** The race it closed predates this feature by six saga suites and would have kept firing at random. **#7 knew this exact mechanism and closed half of it**: `apps/orders/src/infrastructure/messaging/test-support/stub-stock-check-responder.ts:49-58` awaits a `connection.flush()` before returning, with a comment naming "no responders" as the symptom — but `saga-integration-harness.ts:413` starts the microservice and then blocks only on **Kafka** consumer-group readiness, so #7's own saga harness carries the NATS half unclosed to this day. #8's exposure is strictly larger (`BackgroundService.StartAsync` returns before `SubscribeAsync` is even called, where NestJS's `startAllMicroservices()` at least issues the SUB), and #8's fix is strictly more expensive: six hand-built probe loops where nats.js offered one `flush()`. **Booked as #8 cost, but it bought a guard neither assessment had.**

**The finding worth carrying to #9.** Fix round 3's second discovery is the transferable one: **an unpaced retry loop is not a retry loop.** `NatsNoRespondersException` is the server's immediate sentinel and does not consume the request timeout, so a loop whose only pacing is a 200 ms per-attempt timeout burns 100 attempts in ~200 ms — a patience budget a hundred times smaller than it reads. Verified independently by the reviewer: with the 50 ms pacing deleted the guard fails in **204 ms** against a 300 ms-away subscriber; with it, the loop waits **≈5 s**. **Six such loops exist in this repository and five are still unpaced** (backlog id 63, which the review widens from the four disclosed files to six). #9 should write the pacing in from the first probe loop it builds, and should prefer its client's own flush/ping primitive where the connection is reachable.

**What was built:** `orders.cancel`, the operator-initiated cancellation RPC, completing all four of `saga.md` §4.3's starting-state branches — `placed` (immediate, `operator_cancelled`), `stock_reserved` (enqueues `stock.release`), `credit_approved`/`confirmed` (enqueues `credit.release`, then `stock.release` on the returning fact, reverse order of acquisition, proven by an ordered queue rather than by co-occurrence), and terminal rejection with `ORDER_NOT_CANCELLABLE` rather than a 503. **`src/Orders/Domain` has a zero diff** — the aggregate already modelled every legal cancellation. Solution at close: **16 test projects, 1284 tests, 0 failed, 0 skipped** (implementer's `./quality.sh`, twice, exit 0); reviewer's own runs `Orders.UnitTests` **342**, `Architecture.Tests` **16**, and nine `Orders.IntegrationTests` cases against real NATS/MS-SQL/Kafka, all 0 failed.

**The dividend, stated plainly because it is the point of this repository:** #7 could not build the fourth branch at all — `billing.credit.release` existed nowhere, its implementer correctly refused to fabricate the contract, and a leader spec amendment plus a ≈1 h 30 min follow-up pass (a whole Billing responder among other things) were needed. **#8 inherited that amendment already applied and shipped all four branches in the first pass, with no follow-up pass, no spec amendment and no human gate.** The saving is real and it is invisible in the ratio, because the ratio counts the three rounds #8 then spent on guard quality.

**Findings open at approval (4, none blocking):** **B1** — the D2 guard cannot fail on the D2 defect: Orders' `BC23` theory compares a hand-retyped key list to the spec and never to the record, and the two new credit-release schemas have no `AssertKeys` counterpart, so removing `availableCreditAfter` again (with its two named-argument call sites adjusted) leaves `Orders.UnitTests` **342/342 green** — proven by the reviewer, whose own round-1 prescription is what shaped the narrow fix; Billing's equivalent file reflects over the record and would fail immediately, and porting that form into Orders is the recommended remedy. **B2** — the unpaced-loop disclosure names four files; enumeration finds **six** (`CatalogReferenceListAcceptanceTests.cs:351` and `StandInFulfillmentStockCheckResponder.cs:194` are missing), so backlog id 63 should be widened. **B3** — the readiness fix's own call site in `StartHostAsync` is unguarded and inherently so; the deterministic tests arm the loop, not its use. **B4** — #7's `connection.flush()` precedent and the `NatsConnection.PingAsync()` analogue, for id 25's brief. Acceptance bullet **4** (the operator note on the read-model timeline) remains genuinely unmet pending the human's `SA-2` ruling on the `openapi.yaml` / `asyncapi.yaml` inconsistency — identical in #7, disclosed honestly in both, and verified by enumeration in review round 1.

**Verdict:** APPROVED — see `progress/review_orders_cancel_responder.md` (two rounds).

## gateway_rest_auth (id 25, phase 13) — 2026-09-09

**Effort:** **1 implementer pass + 1 fix pass, 2 review rounds — REJECTED at round 1 (D1, D2, D3 blocking), APPROVED at round 2.** No spec session (`sdd: false`); the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 25's three acceptance bullets plus `specs/shared/openapi.yaml`, `asyncapi.yaml`'s eight Gateway channels and `domain-model.md` §9. Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local, 2026-09-09), bracketed by the leader's session-file write at **05:29:49** and this review's close at **≈08:05**:

- **Implementation ≈05:33 → ≈06:29 — ≈56 min.** First production file at 05:33, `specs/shared/test-matrix.md` Status update at **06:26:53**, last pass-1 artefact ≈06:29. Built: the whole Gateway service — 14 REST routes across seven endpoint files, hand-rolled HS256 JWT issue/verify over `System.Security.Cryptography`, `BearerAuthenticationMiddleware` (deny-by-default, `AllowAnonymous` as a pure metadata marker), a fixed-window login limiter on one route, `ProblemJsonMiddleware` with ten classification branches, the `IssuedOrderWindow` 202-vs-404 mechanism, a Mongo read-model adapter, a NATS RPC client over all eight subjects, `GET /docs` serving the committed `openapi.yaml` bytes as an embedded resource, and an eight-row ported-idiom ledger. **Zero new NuGet packages** — `Directory.Packages.props` has a zero diff for the whole feature; `Sdk.Web`'s shared framework supplied Kestrel, Minimal APIs, `IAllowAnonymous` and `Microsoft.AspNetCore.RateLimiting`, and `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Authentication.JwtBearer` was deliberately not adopted.
- **Review round 1 ≈06:30 → 07:06:17 — ≈36 min. REJECTED**, six mutations against real containers, **three of them survived**.
- **`CLAUDE.md` amended 07:06:57** — the sweep convention (*a sweep must not filter by the property it is testing; ask what a violation would do to the candidate list — if the answer is "leave it", the sweep cannot fail*), written directly off review D2 and now carrying three phase-13 instances.
- **Fix round ≈07:08 → 07:42:02 — ≈34 min.** All eight review items, `.env.example`/`docker-compose.infra.yml` at 07:16, one full `./quality.sh` (**1481** across 18 projects).
- **Review round 2 ≈07:45 → ≈08:05 — ≈20 min. APPROVED**, nine independent mutations, four against live containers; eight killed, one survived (E1, non-blocking).
- **Total ≈2 h 35 min across four passes.**

**#7's counterpart:** **1 implementation session + 3 review passes + 2 fix passes, rejected twice, ≈2 h 45 min.** **#8 is ≈0.94× — near-parity on wall-clock, and won on passes, 4 vs 6.**

**The confound, stated plainly, because this is not a win.** #8 was rejected **once**, and the defect it was rejected for is in the **same class #7 was first rejected for**: the Gateway's RPC subject constants unguarded, so every test that named a subject named it as `GatewaySubjects.X` on both sides and the assertion was `X == X`. Three of eight corrupted left 141 tests green. **The ledger and guard-enumeration rules changed *which* review pass found the seam, not *whether* a fix round was needed.** The pass count fell because #8's single fix round closed eight items at once where #7 needed two; the wall-clock is flat because the round that found them cost what it costs. Anyone reading 0.94× as a stack dividend is reading it wrong.

**The three round-1 survivors, split by whose fault they are:**

- **D1 — a guard #7 had and #8 dropped in translation.** Every other service in this repository asserts its subject constants against `asyncapi.yaml`'s channel `address:` lines (`StockSubjectsTests`, `RpcSubjectsTests`, `CreditSubjectsTests`, `InvoiceSubjectsTests`, `SagaRpcSubjectsTests`); the Gateway — the one service that calls **all eight** — had none, and no payload guard either. **This is the third consecutive phase-13 feature whose rejection was a guard #7's own suite carried at the same seam and #8's port did not** (id 40 `includeDisabled`, id 41 `stock.release` reason, id 25 the subject constants). That is now a pattern with three instances and it belongs squarely in the "not faster, and our fault" column. The fix reads `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml` as text at test time and reflects over the payload record's own properties — never a hand-typed key list, which is backlog id 64's point applied prospectively.
- **D2 — a sweep that could not fail, and the convention it produced.** The anonymous-route sweep selected its candidate set **by the `IAllowAnonymous` metadata under test**, so marking `GET /orders` public removed it from the sweep instead of failing it. #7's own `auth.integration.spec.ts:68,79` hard-codes its public set and derives the protected set by subtraction, which is why #7's would have caught what #8's could not — another dropped guard, but this one generalised into a binding rule the same hour. **Round 2 verified the rewrite in the direction that matters**: with the metadata assertion neutralised, the subtraction loop alone failed with `GET /orders was expected to reject an anonymous request with 401, but answered 500` — the violation lands *in* the population now.
- **D3 — an env read that was never written.** `JWT_SECRET`/`JWT_EXPIRES_IN`/`JWT_ISSUER` existed nowhere in #8 — not in code, not in `.env.example`, not in compose — while `GatewayMongoOptions`, `LoginThrottleOptions` and `OperatorIdentityLoader` in the *same feature* all read the environment. #7 reads all three (`jwt.config.ts:12-14`). The Gateway's signing key was a compile-time constant. **#8-only cost, an ordinary oversight, and the cheapest of the three to fix.**

**The finding worth carrying to #9.** The ledger's history halves were all correct this time — round 1 verified every one of the eight citations into #7's checkout, which is the half ids 40 and 41 got wrong — **and the gaps moved to the Guard column instead.** Row 6 asserted an engine property (ASP.NET Core route precedence is not registration-order-sensitive) with no guard and no stated probe direction; row 3, the highest-risk row in the build, named `jsonwebtoken` without enumerating the four properties it supplied (expiry, issuer, structural rejection, algorithm pinning). Both were true. Neither could fail. **This is the ledger rule's own failure mode arriving one column to the right of last phase's**: once "read #7's source, cite the file and line" is internalised, the residual risk is a row that is accurate and inert. #9 should treat every Guard cell as a countable claim on the same footing as the history half — and should note that round 2 proved row 3's algorithm-pinning claim by *introducing the vulnerability* (parse the header, honour `alg: none`) rather than by re-reading the code, which is the only probe that could have distinguished "correct" from "correct and guarded".

**What was built:** the Gateway — #8's only HTTP surface, and the first .NET target #7's n8n workflows and black-box API script could ever run against. 14 routes, all eight NATS RPC subjects now asserted against `asyncapi.yaml`, `GET /docs` serving the committed contract bytes verbatim, and an `OpenApiContractTests` that derives both sides from real artefacts (the embedded `openapi.yaml` and `((IEndpointRouteBuilder)app).DataSources`) and is falsifiable in **both** directions — declared-but-not-mapped armed by the implementer, mapped-but-not-declared armed by the reviewer. Solution at close: **18 test projects, 1481 tests, 0 failed, 0 skipped** (implementer's `./quality.sh`); reviewer's own runs `Gateway.UnitTests` **161**, `Gateway.IntegrationTests` **36** against real NATS/Mongo/MS-SQL/Kafka, `Architecture.Tests` **16**, all 0 failed — a +56 delta from 1425 that reconciles exactly with the claimed total.

**Findings open at approval (2, neither blocking):** **E1** — `OrderReadModelMapper.ToOrderDetail:84` transposes `initialAmount`/`initialDiscount` with **161/161 and 36/36 green**. The code is correct; nothing asserts it. The two `ToOrderDetail` unit tests assert a *placeholder*'s null totals and event causation, and `OrdersHttpTests.GetOrder_ReturnsTheProjectedDocument_…` asserts `orderReference`/`headerComplete`/events but not `totals`, so three money fields on `GET /orders/{id}`'s body are unasserted anywhere. The round-1 prescription is what scoped this — it cited `:76` and `OrderSummary` — so the shortfall is the reviewer's under-scoping, and the shared `CompleteDocument()` fixture already carries distinguishing values `(124950, 700, 124250)`; the remedy is three assertions. **E2** — the ledger **table**'s rows 3 and 6 are stale relative to their own corrections: row 3's Guard cell still reads "7 cases" and row 6's is still empty, with both fixes living 550 lines away in the fix-round prose. Row 6's stated purpose is to save feature 26 from re-deriving route precedence, and feature 26 will read the table. A two-cell edit to `progress/impl_gateway_rest_auth.md`, owed before commit.

**Also carried, unexercised:** the `n8n/workflows/*.json` have never been fired against the .NET Gateway, and the black-box API script has not been run. These are the sharpest parity tests this repository has, they are now *possible* for the first time, and no acceptance bullet names them — so they need scheduling rather than drifting. Six of the eight RPC subjects are still exercised only against stubs (only `fulfillment.stock.list`/`fulfillment.stock.replenish` run against a real `FulfillmentHost`); the subject strings and payload key sets are now contract-derived, but a `billing.invoice.list` + `billing.payment.register` real-responder end-to-end remains the recommended follow-up.

**Verdict:** APPROVED — see `progress/review_gateway_rest_auth.md` (two rounds).

**Effort:** **2 implementation passes (round 1 build + round 2 fix) + 2 completed review passes — REJECTED at round 1 (D1 blocking), APPROVED at round 2.** No spec session (`sdd: false`); the specification of record is `feature_list.json` id 26's two acceptance bullets plus `specs/shared/openapi.yaml`'s `/orders/stream` frame-format and reconnection sections and `R55`. Wall-clock from artefact mtimes (local CEST, 2026-09-09):

- **Round-1 implementation ≈08:20:11 → 09:51:20 — ≈1 h 31 min.** `progress/current.md` rewritten at the dispatch, `progress/impl_gateway_sse_push.md` complete at 09:51:20. Built: `GET /orders/stream` end to end — a pure `CursorGenerator`/`ReplayBuffer<T>` in `Domain/Sse`, a `StreamHub` fanning out over one bounded `Channel<StreamFrame>` per live connection, a `NatsStreamSignalSubscriber` `BackgroundService` on the projector's two `readmodel.*` wildcard subjects, and a `StreamEndpoints` writing raw `id:`/`event:`/`data:` frames (ASP.NET Core Minimal APIs have no SSE helper). 43 tests added, three full `quality.sh` passes, and a pre-existing Kestrel-bind race in feature 25's own test classes found and fixed on the way (8/47 → 2/47 → 0/47). **Zero new NuGet packages** — `Channel<T>` and `PeriodicTimer` are BCL.
- **Review round 1 ≈09:55 → 10:33:47 — ≈38 min. REJECTED**, five mutations against real containers, **one survived**.
- **Round-2 fix pass ≈10:34 → 10:57:06 — ≈23 min.** Ledger rows 10 and 11 written, the D1 guard added and armed, all eleven `#7 relied on` citations given file **and** line, and one doc comment corrected to name a test that exists. **No behavioural change to `src/Gateway`** — proved at review by diffing the file against the reviewer's own round-1 copy: the only delta is the doc comment.
- **Review round 2 ≈10:58 → ≈11:30 — ≈32 min. APPROVED.** Four fresh mutations; three killed the new guard, one survived and produced finding R2-1.

**Traceable total ≈3 h 04 min across two implementation passes and two completed review passes.**

**#7's counterpart:** **2 implementation passes + 2 completed review passes, rejected once, ≈1 h 22 min** (its `progress/history.md:1041`; a third, interrupted review attempt produced no artefact and is excluded from both totals). **#8 is ≈2.2×** — the largest per-feature ratio in phase 13, and the pass counts are **identical**, which is the number that matters: the same amount of process, more wall-clock inside it.

**Where the extra 1 h 42 min went, honestly.** Not into rework — the fix round was 23 minutes. Three things: **container time** (a single `Gateway.IntegrationTests` assembly run is 5 m 14 s against real NATS/Kafka/Mongo/MS-SQL, and the two reviews spent six such runs between them, where #7's equivalents were Vitest against lighter fixtures); **more built** (a real in-process `ProjectorHost` end-to-end test instead of #7's `tsx`-spawned one that its own review rejected; a *strong-form* heartbeat test — a raw `TcpClient` with `NetworkStream.ReadTimeout` proving the connection would die **without** the ping — which #7 never had; an `Interlocked` cursor concurrency guard that #7's single-threaded runtime made unnecessary; a teardown guard reached through a real client disconnect where #7's was a unit-level `stop()` test); and **a neighbouring feature's flaky harness fixed** rather than left for the next feature to trip over.

**The rejection cause is the finding, and it is the third of its kind this phase.** #7 was rejected here for **a false equivalence claim in a harness comment** (`spawn-real-projector.ts` claimed a `tsx` child was equivalent to the real dev command). #8 was rejected for **a ported idiom with no ledger row and no guard**: #7's teardown is Express's engine-supplied `res.on('close')` (`stream.controller.ts:80-83`); #8 hand-builds it from `HttpContext.RequestAborted` + `try/catch (OperationCanceledException)/finally { hub.Unsubscribe(...) }`, and **deleting that `Unsubscribe` left `Gateway.UnitTests` 193/193 and the SSE HTTP tests 10/10 green**. Every disconnected browser `EventSource` would have leaked a 256-slot channel that `StreamHub.Publish` iterates on every fact — in the one endpoint whose clients *define themselves* by disconnecting and reconnecting. That is the same family as this phase's two earlier rejections (ids 40 and 41): **a guard #7 had, dropped in translation, invisible to traceability because the requirement was met and invisible to arming because the behaviour was correct on every path a test took.**

**The round-2 guard was armed three ways, not one**, and the second and third are the transferable part: deleting the `Unsubscribe` kills it (10 s), **not threading the request's own token** into the read/ping loop also kills it (41 s — the loop then unwinds only when a ping hits a dead socket, past the guard's deadline), and making the endpoint subscribe on a **different `StreamHub` instance** kills it at the mid-test assertion. Only the first is the classic deletion probe; the other two are what answer *"does this guard execute the code the ledger row is actually about?"* — a question the ledger has needed an answer to since it was adopted.

**Finding R2-1, open at approval, non-blocking — and the most portable artefact this feature produced.** Ledger row 11 correctly declines to write a guard for #7's `stop() unsubscribes both subscriptions`: in #8 that property comes from `BackgroundService`'s stopping token plus NATS.Client.Core's cancellation-aware `IAsyncEnumerable`, both framework-supplied, so the ledger rule (which binds only where a property must be **hand-built**) does not fire. The row then offered evidence for its confidence — *"a hung subscription loop would hang every test's teardown"* — and **the reviewer broke exactly that plumbing and measured nothing at all**: one of the two consume loops given `CancellationToken.None`, three host start/stop cycles, **1 m 35.5 s mutated against 1 m 35.4 s restored**. The row's conclusion is right; its stated evidence cannot fire. The correction is one sentence, and it is stronger than what it replaces: *the property is not independently observable here, and that unobservability — not an absence of effort — is why no guard is written.*

**For #9, and for the guard-hardening loop (ids 60, 61, 63, 64, 65).** These two findings sit at three positions on one axis. Ids 60-65 are all *a guard whose assertion cannot detect the defect it names*. **D1 is one step upstream** — no assertion existed at all, and what hid it was a **missing ledger row**, not a weak one. **R2-1 is one step sideways** — the row exists, correctly names no guard, and then supplies evidence that cannot fire. So the loop's instrument should not stop at *"delete the behaviour this test names — does it go red?"*; it should also ask of every ledger row **"if this row's stated evidence were false, what would turn red, and has anyone seen it?"** That question costs one line at spec time and would have caught R2-1 in seconds. Nothing else in this harness catches this class: the requirement is met, traceability is satisfied, and the code is correct.

**Also carried, unchanged:** the `n8n/workflows/*.json` still have never been fired against the .NET Gateway and the black-box API script has not been run (ids 31 and 33, phases 18 and 20); `GatewayTestHost.StartAsync` still leaves a partially built `WebApplication` undisposed when `StartAsync` throws anything other than `TaskCanceledException`; and R55's **web half** remains genuinely unproven, owed to `apps/web` (ids 29/30, both `pending`).

**Verdict:** APPROVED — see `progress/review_gateway_sse_push.md` (two rounds). **This does not close phase 13**: ids 56, 60, 61, 63, 64 and 65 remain `pending`.

## Shared amendment SA-2 (raised here, applied to #7 in the same session) — 2026-09-09

**Effort:** n/a — an amendment, not a feature. No code, no tests; the code that consumes the new field is a separate feature.
**Raised by:** assessment #8 (this repository), Phase 13, after feature 41 (`orders_cancel_responder`) disclosed acceptance bullet 4 as unsatisfiable for the second time in the trilogy.
**Touches:** `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml` only — three lines, one optional property on `OrderCancelledPayload`. No requirement, no channel, no operation, no `required` list, no other schema.

**What was wrong:**

`specs/shared/` contradicted itself, and had done so in both assessments since the spec was written.

`openapi.yaml:1344-1347` types `CancelOrderRequest.note` as *"Free-text operator note **recorded on the timeline entry**"* — a promise about where the value ends up, not merely about what the caller may send. `asyncapi.yaml`'s `OrderCancelledPayload` (at `:2602-2627` as the file stood before this amendment) had **no field able to carry it**, and that payload is the only thing the projector ever builds an `order.cancelled` timeline entry from. The note therefore reached the Orders aggregate over `orders.cancel` (the RPC request payload has always declared `note`) and stopped there, because the fact that leaves Orders had nowhere to put it.

So the REST half of the shared specification promised a behaviour the event half could not deliver. Feature 41's acceptance bullet 4 — *"the operator note from `CancelOrderRequest` lands on the read-model timeline"* — was **never satisfiable as written**, in either stack, by any implementer, however careful.

**The fix:**

An **optional** `note` property added to `OrderCancelledPayload`: `type: string`, matching how `CancelOrderRequest.note` and `OrdersCancelRequestPayload.note` are already typed (a bare string in both — no named schema, no `maxLength`, nothing to reuse), with a description in the file's own style and a cross-reference of the form `PartyView` already uses (*"Named identically in `openapi.yaml`"*).

**Optional, deliberately.** Every existing producer and every stored envelope predates the field, and the field is meaningless for the two cancellations the saga decides — `stock_rejected` and `credit_rejected` have no operator to have written a note. Adding it to the `required` list would have made a spec amendment into a breaking wire change and invalidated every envelope already on the retained topics of both assessments.

**Neither assessment's process found it — both disclosed it and shipped:**

#7 hit this wall first. Its implementer disclosed it in full (`progress/impl_orders_cancel_responder.md:99`), its reviewer confirmed the disclosure honest by reading the wire schema, recorded it as *"F3 (informational)"*, and ruled that *"the **next** feature that touches `OrderCancelledPayload` must close it"* (`progress/review_orders_catalog_and_cancel_responders.md:54`). No further feature of #7 touched `OrderCancelledPayload`; #7 closed with the gap open.

The next feature to touch `OrderCancelledPayload` was, one assessment later, **#8's id 41** — which read the same two files, reached the same conclusion independently, and disclosed it again with the same honesty and the same disposition: not a rejection basis, out of scope, `specs/shared/` is read-only.

**And the stated reason for not fixing it does not survive contact with #7's own commit.** Both reports gave the same justification: the fix touches `specs/shared/`, which is out of a feature's scope. But `32da6e9` — the commit that closed #7's features 40 and 41, and whose own message discloses the note gap — **adds 111 lines to `specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml`**, minting the entire `billing.credit.release` channel because the cancel path needed it:

```
$ git show 32da6e9 --stat -- specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml
 specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml | 111 +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++
 1 file changed, 111 insertions(+)
```

So in the one session where the gap was found, the file was open, the author was already editing that exact document for that exact feature, and 111 lines went in — none of them the three that would have closed it. The scope objection was not, at that moment, a real constraint. What made the difference was that `billing.credit.release` **blocked the feature** and the note **only failed an acceptance bullet**, and an acceptance bullet can be disclosed.

That the deferral then went nowhere is enumerable rather than inferred:

```
$ git log --oneline -- specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml
32da6e9 feat(orders): catalog and cancel NATS responders, closing two contract gaps
8635b66 checkpoint(observability): requestId dedup, dead-letter, trace propagation
cf795db docs(spec): stack-agnostic shared specification, written before the code
$ git log --oneline 32da6e9..HEAD | wc -l
39
```

Feature 41 is the **last** commit in #7 to touch `asyncapi.yaml`. Thirty-nine commits followed it, including the whole of #7's Phase 25 audit and its final checkpoint, and not one reopened the file. "The next feature that touches `OrderCancelledPayload` must close it" turned out to be a condition that never fired.

Every individual judgement in that chain was defensible in isolation. The #8 implementer genuinely could not fix it — this repository's read-only rule on `specs/shared/` is stricter than #7's was, and correctly so. The reviewers could not reject for it — nothing built was wrong. And the outcome of two defensible chains of judgement was that a known, twice-documented, three-line defect in the shared contract survived an entire completed assessment and most of a second one.

**A defect that both runs disclose and neither fixes is a defect the process routes around, and that is the transferable finding.** The escalation happened only because a coordinator asked *why* bullet 4 was unsatisfiable instead of accepting the disclosure — the same shape as the three ported-idiom defects: found by someone asking a question, not by any instrument. The concrete correction it argues for is narrow: **a disclosure whose stated root cause is `specs/shared/` itself is an amendment candidate, and must leave the feature as a backlog entry rather than as a sentence in a report.** #7's reviewer deferred to "the next feature that touches this payload", which is a deferral to nobody in a repository one phase from closing; a backlog id would have been read at the next planning pass.

**The second finding — the two halves of a shared spec can disagree with nothing noticing:**

`openapi.yaml` and `asyncapi.yaml` are separate documents, and **no test in either repository loads both and compares them.** Enumerated, because a claim of absence is a search result and not a reading — and the first draft of this paragraph asserted "no file mentions both", which the command below immediately disproved:

```
$ comm -12 <(grep -rln "asyncapi" --include=*.cs --include=*.ts --include=*.tsx src/ tests/ apps/ | sort) \
           <(grep -rln "openapi"  --include=*.cs --include=*.ts --include=*.tsx src/ tests/ apps/ | sort)
src/Gateway/Application/Ports/IRpcClient.cs
src/Gateway/Domain/Problem/RpcErrorClassification.cs
src/Projector/Application/Signals/OrderStreamUpdate.cs
tests/Gateway.UnitTests/CancelOrderCommandHandlerTests.cs
tests/Gateway.UnitTests/GatewayRpcPayloadTests.cs
```

Five hits, classified one line each:

- `IRpcClient.cs:5,37,47` — doc comments citing each document for a different concept (`RpcHeaders`/`RpcTimeout` from one, `UpstreamUnavailable` from the other). No parse of either. **Not a comparison.**
- `RpcErrorClassification.cs:3,13-52` — doc comments describing a translation *from* asyncapi's `RpcError` *to* openapi's `Problem`. Prose only; the class reads neither file. **Not a comparison.**
- `OrderStreamUpdate.cs:4,8` — a doc comment saying the signal mirrors openapi's `OrderStreamUpdate` shape while the subject is versioned by asyncapi. **Not a comparison.**
- `CancelOrderCommandHandlerTests.cs:14,49` — two doc comments citing one document each to justify two assertions about code. **Not a comparison.**
- `GatewayRpcPayloadTests.cs:76-92` — the closest thing in the repository, and the one that makes the point. It **parses** `asyncapi.yaml` and asserts `InvoiceViewPayload` carries every declared property plus exactly one documented extra, `lines` — and the justification for that extra is that *"`openapi.yaml`'s `Invoice` (`:1752`) DOES declare an optional `lines`"*, written as a **hand-typed comment citing a line number**. The openapi half of that reconciliation is prose. **Not a comparison** — and it is a second live instance of the same seam.

So both documents are each independently checked against code, by real guards that read them as text at test time, and **neither is ever checked against the other.**

**#7 was checked separately, not assumed.** The same enumeration there returns fourteen files, and #7 is the more interesting case because it *does* have machinery that opens both documents — `packages/contracts` generates TypeScript types from each:

```
$ comm -12 <(grep -rln "asyncapi" --include=*.ts --include=*.tsx --include=*.vue apps/ packages/ | sort) \
           <(grep -rln "openapi"  --include=*.ts --include=*.tsx --include=*.vue apps/ packages/ | sort)
apps/gateway/src/application/commands/{cancel-order,place-order,register-payment,replenish-stock}.command.ts
apps/gateway/src/application/ports/rpc-client.port.ts
apps/gateway/src/application/queries/{list-catalog,list-stock}.query.ts
apps/gateway/src/domain/problem/rpc-error-mapping.ts
packages/contracts/scripts/{check,generate}.spec.ts
packages/contracts/src/index.ts
packages/contracts/src/generated/asyncapi.types.ts
packages/contracts/dist/index.d.ts
packages/contracts/dist/generated/asyncapi.types.d.ts
```

Nine are doc comments citing one document each for a different concept, exactly as in #8. `src/index.ts` and the two `dist/` files **re-export** both generated type sets side by side. And `scripts/generate.spec.ts:21` — *"produces byte-identical `asyncapi.types.ts` and `openapi.types.ts`"* — asserts that regeneration is **deterministic**, which is a claim about the generator, not about agreement between the documents. `check.spec.ts:53` mentions both only in a comment about neither file existing yet.

So #7's codegen reads both documents and emits **two type sets that never meet**: a contradiction between the halves generates two perfectly valid, mutually silent type families. Having a build step that opens both files did not help, and would not have helped. That is the seam SA-2 lived in for two assessments.

**Recommendation on guarding it — a process guard, not a test; and specifically *not* the schema-consistency test that first suggests itself:**

**Against the obvious candidate.** The natural design is: load both YAMLs, intersect `components.schemas`, assert that every shared name has the same property keys and the same `required` list. It is cheap and definable, so I checked what it would actually buy:

```
asyncapi schemas: 99   openapi schemas: 57   shared names: 23
shared names whose property/required sets differ: 0
```

The 23 are scalars and reference codes (`CancellationReason`, `CurrencyCode`, `Instant`, `MinorUnits`, `OrderStatus`, `PartyCode`, `UniqueId`, …) plus four small shared views (`PageInfo`, `Party`, `PartyRef`, `Product`). They have never drifted, and they are not where drift happens. **And it would have missed both cross-document divergences this repository actually has**: SA-2's, because `CancelOrderRequest` and `OrderCancelledPayload` share no name with anything on the other side; and `InvoiceView` against `Invoice`, for the same reason. A guard with a known 0-for-2 record against the real defect population, green on the day it is written and green forever after, is the guard-that-does-not-guard by construction — this repository's most-repeated failure shape, and I am not going to add another instance of it.

**Against the general form, too.** 99 schemas against 57, describing different surfaces (36 channels against 17 REST paths), with no total mapping between them. A check that cannot be defined precisely gets defined loosely, and a loose check goes inert.

**For a process guard, which is what would have caught this.** The instrument that detects this class already exists and already worked: **the acceptance bullet, which fired in #7 and fired again in #8.** Nothing failed to see the defect; what failed is that seeing it twice produced two paragraphs and no work item. So the guard belongs at the point of disclosure:

> **When a feature report discloses an acceptance bullet as unmet and names `specs/shared/` itself as the reason, the reviewer must open a numbered backlog entry — an `SA-n` candidate — before approving. Approval prose may not discharge it by deferring to "the next feature that touches X".**

That is checkable at review time, costs one backlog row, and is the only thing on this list that would have closed SA-2 at either of the two moments it was visible. #7's reviewer wrote precisely the forbidden sentence (*"the next feature that touches `OrderCancelledPayload` must close it"*) in a repository one phase from closing, so the deferral was to nobody. Recommended as a backlog entry and a `reviewer` convention, not as part of this amendment.

**One small thing worth having on its own merits, unrelated to SA-2.** `GatewayRpcPayloadTests.cs:77` cites `openapi.yaml` by **line number** (`:1752`), and this amendment has just demonstrated that line numbers in that file move. A check that every `<file>:<line>` citation in a doc comment still points at what it claims is enumerable and cheap. It has nothing to do with cross-document consistency and should not be sold as if it did.

**Mechanics, per the `SA-1` convention:**

Applied to `order-to-cash-dotnet` and `order-to-cash-nestjs` in the same session, byte-identical:

```
$ diff order-to-cash-dotnet/specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml order-to-cash-nestjs/specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml ; echo "exit $?"
exit 0
$ sha256sum order-to-cash-{dotnet,nestjs}/specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml
449674a438653103f0eafa82c2f7d04f837fe48eeb139c7431e715d76f9c725d  order-to-cash-dotnet/specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml
449674a438653103f0eafa82c2f7d04f837fe48eeb139c7431e715d76f9c725d  order-to-cash-nestjs/specs/shared/asyncapi.yaml
```

Three lines added in each; nothing else in either repository's `specs/shared/` touched. `./init.sh` exit 0 here — `29 [OK] / 2 [WARN] / 0 [FAIL]`, counted off the run (`grep -c` on its captured output), both warnings expected: uncommitted changes mid-session, and `quality.sh` deliberately not run.

**A third finding, and the one with the widest blast radius — found only because this amendment went looking for the check that was supposed to cover it.** The dispatching brief for `SA-2` stated that `init.sh` *"has a check that `specs/shared/` matches #7's, and this amendment is exactly the case where that check should stay green because both sides moved together."* **There is no such check.** Enumerated:

```
$ grep -n "specs" init.sh
131:    if (!fs.existsSync(`specs/${f.name}/${doc}`)) { fail(`specs/${f.name}/${doc} missing (feature is ${f.status})`); missing++; }
$ grep -nE "order-to-cash-nestjs|Assessments/|\.\./" init.sh ; echo "exit $?"
exit 1
```

One hit, and it is the SDD triple-doc existence check for per-feature spec folders. `init.sh` never opens `specs/shared/`, never references the sibling repository, and contains no hash, `cmp` or `diff` against #7. **The trilogy's single most load-bearing invariant — `specs/shared/` byte-identical across all three repositories — has no automated guard anywhere in either repository.** A silent fork is currently detectable only by a human running `diff` by hand, exactly as this amendment did.

That reframes the guard recommendation above. Between *"the two halves of the spec might disagree"* (real, but 0-for-2 on mechanical detection and already caught twice by acceptance criteria) and *"the shared spec might silently fork between repositories"* (the thing this whole assessment's parity claim rests on, currently unguarded and trivially checkable), **the second is the one worth building.** It is a handful of `sha256sum` comparisons across the eleven files of `specs/shared/`, needs the sibling checkout path from an env var or a skip, and it would fail loudly the first time anyone edited one copy alone. Recommended as a backlog entry, at higher priority than the cross-document check this amendment was asked about.

**And it is the guard-that-does-not-guard in its purest form yet: a check believed to exist, cited in a brief as active coverage, that was never written.** The previous three disguises in `CLAUDE.md` were a check that fires on nothing, a check run against the wrong artefact, and a check whose invariants a wrong state satisfies. This is a fourth — a check whose only existence is in someone's description of the process. Recorded in both repositories' `progress/history.md` and in #8's README amendment registry.
